and all other pairings between these basis vectors are zero. The lift
\widetilde{\mathrm{Fil}}
is spanned by
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
where

(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})=(e_{1},\ldots,e_{n},\Pi e_{1},\ldots,\Pi e_{n})\begin{pmatrix}A\epsilon\\
I_{n}\end{pmatrix}

where
A\in M_{n}(\bar{\kappa})
and
A={}^{t}A
since
\widetilde{\mathrm{Fil}}
is isotropic.
Assume
\mathcal{F}^{\bot}\subset\mathrm{Fil}=\Pi\cdot M_{\bar{\kappa}}
is spanned by

\sum_{i=1}^{n}b_{n+i}\Pi\cdot e_{i}.

Then
b_{n+i}\neq 0
for some
i
and we can assume without loss of generality
b_{n+1}=1
. The lift
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}^{\bot}
is spanned by

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{b}_{i}e_{i}+\sum_{i=n+1}^{2n}\tilde{b}_{i}\Pi\cdot e_{i},

where
\tilde{b}_{n+1}=1
and
\tilde{b}_{n+i}=b_{n+i}+\epsilon c_{i}
for
2\leq i\leq n
and some
c_{i}\in\bar{\kappa}
. Let

\lambda={}^{t}(\tilde{b}_{n+1},\ldots,\tilde{b}_{2n}).

Equations (4.7), (4.8) and (4.10) of
[
Krä03
]
tell us that

A=\gamma_{1}\lambda\cdot{}^{t}\lambda

for some
\gamma_{1}\in\bar{\kappa}
. Equation (4.5) of loc.cit. tells us

{}^{t}(\tilde{b}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{b}_{n})=A\lambda,

which is equal to
\gamma_{1}\lambda\cdot{}^{t}\lambda\cdot\lambda=0
as
{}^{t}\lambda\cdot\lambda=0
by (4.9) of loc.cit..
In particular
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}^{\bot}\subset\Pi M_{\mathcal{O}}
and a point in
T_{z}(\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda,\bar{\kappa}})
is determined by the
n-1
parameters
c_{i}
for
2\leq i\leq n
together with the additional parameter
\gamma_{1}
. Now the condition
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}^{\bot}\subset(\Lambda_{M,\mathcal{O}})^{\bot}
(condition (e) of Lemma
3.8
) imposes further
n-m
independent linear equations on the parameters
c_{i}
for
2\leq i\leq n
. This shows that the tangent space
T_{z}(\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda,\bar{\kappa}})
has dimension less than or equal to
m
. This finishes the proof of the proposition.
∎

3.3.
Isomorphism between
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}
and
Y_{V,\bar{\kappa}}

By
[
RTW14
, Lemma 6.1]
, the lattices
\breve{\Lambda}
and
\breve{\Lambda}^{\sharp}
(see (
3.3
)) are closed under
\Pi
,
\mathbf{V}
and
\mathbf{F}
, hence determine supersingular
p
-divisible strict
O_{F_{0}}
-modules with
O_{F}
-action
X_{-}
and
X_{+}
(denoted by
X_{\Lambda^{-}}
and
X_{\Lambda^{+}}
resp. in
[
RTW14
, §6]
) of dimension
n
over
\bar{\kappa}
together with quasi-isogenies
\rho_{-}:X_{-}\rightarrow\mathbb{X}
of height
m
and
\rho_{+}:X_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{X}
of height
-m
. The inclusion
\breve{\Lambda}\subset\breve{\Lambda}^{\sharp}
also defines an isogeny
\rho_{\Lambda}:X_{-}\rightarrow X_{+}
of height
2m
.
Since
X_{-}\cong\mathbb{Y}^{n}
as an
O_{F}
-module for any
\bar{\kappa}
-scheme
S
, on the special fiber condition (1) in Definition
3.4
is equivalent to the condition

(3.13)

(1)^{\prime}:\text{ The quasi-isogeny }\rho_{X,-}:=\rho^{-1}\circ(\rho_{-})_{S}:(X_{-})_{S}\rightarrow X\text{ is an isogeny}.

This is further equivalent by loc.cit. to the condition

(3.14)

(1)^{\prime\prime}:\text{ The quasi-isogeny }\rho_{X,+}:=(\rho_{+})^{-1}_{S}\circ\rho:X\rightarrow(X_{+})_{S}\text{ is an isogeny}.

Lemma 3.13
.

The functor
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda,\bar{\kappa}}
is representable by a projective scheme over
\bar{\kappa}
. The functor morphism
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda,\bar{\kappa}}\rightarrow\mathcal{N}
is a closed immersion.

Proof.

\mathcal{Z}(\Lambda)_{\bar{\kappa}}
is a closed formal subscheme of
\mathcal{N}
.
Since for any
\bar{\kappa}
-scheme
S
, Condition (1) in Definition
3.4
is equivalent to (
3.13
), the functor
\mathcal{Z}(\Lambda)_{\bar{\kappa}}
can be represented by a projective scheme over
\bar{\kappa}
by exact the same argument as that of
[
VW11
, Lemma 3.2]
. Condition (2) of Definition
3.4
defines
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda,\bar{\kappa}}
as a closed subscheme of
\mathcal{Z}(\Lambda)_{\bar{\kappa}}
, hence is itself projective over
\bar{\kappa}
and a closed formal subscheme of
\mathcal{N}
. This finishes the proof of the lemma.
∎

In the following discussion we assume that
\Lambda
has type greater than or equal to
2
.
Let
V=\Lambda^{\sharp}/\Lambda
and define a symplectic form
\langle\,,\,\rangle_{V}
on
V
as follows. For
\bar{x},\bar{y}\in V
with lifts
x,y\in\Lambda^{\sharp}
, define
\langle\bar{x},\bar{y}\rangle_{V}
by the image of
\pi_{0}\delta\langle x,y\rangle
in
\mathbb{F}_{q}
(see §
2.2
). Extend this form bilinearly to
V_{\bar{\kappa}}
. Note that
\tau
induces identity on
V
and the Frobenius
\Phi
on
V_{\bar{\kappa}}
.
Let
R
be a
\bar{\kappa}
-algebra and
(X,\iota,\lambda,\rho,\mathcal{F})\in\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}(R)
. As in the proof of
[
VW11
, Corollary 3.9]
,
\mathrm{Image}(D((\rho_{\Lambda})_{R}))
is a locally free direct summands of
D((X_{+})_{R})
of corank
2m
and

D((X_{+})_{R})/\mathrm{Image}(D((\rho_{\Lambda})_{R}))\cong\breve{\Lambda}^{\sharp}/\breve{\Lambda}\otimes_{\bar{\kappa}}R=V_{R}.

As
(\rho_{\Lambda})_{R}=\rho_{X,+}\circ\rho_{X,-}
, we know
\ker(\rho_{X,+})=\ker((\rho_{\Lambda})_{R})/\ker(\rho_{X,-})
as a quotient of finite group schemes over
\mathrm{Spec}\,R
. Since
\Pi\breve{\Lambda}^{\sharp}\subset\breve{\Lambda}
, by relative Dieudonné theory, we know
\ker(\rho_{\Lambda})\subset X_{-}[\iota_{X_{-}}(\pi)]
or equivalently
\iota_{X_{-}}(\pi)\cdot\ker(\rho_{\Lambda})=\{0\}
. Hence
\iota(\pi)\cdot\ker(\rho_{X,+})=\{0\}
or equivalently
\ker(\rho_{X,+})\subset X[\iota(\pi)]
. Thus there exists an isogeny
\tilde{\rho}_{X,+}:X_{+}\rightarrow X
such that
\tilde{\rho}_{X,+}\circ\rho_{X,+}
is the isogeny
\iota(\pi):X\rightarrow X
.
Recall
\mathrm{Fil}
in the exact sequence (
3.4
).

Lemma 3.14
.

D(\tilde{\rho}_{X,+})^{-1}(\mathrm{Fil})
is a locally free direct summand of
D(X_{+})
that contains
D((\rho_{\Lambda})_{R})
. Moreover the quotient

(3.15)

U(X):=D(\tilde{\rho}_{X,+})^{-1}(\mathrm{Fil})/\mathrm{Image}(D((\rho_{\Lambda})_{R}))

is a locally free direct summand of
V_{R}
of rank
m
.

Proof.

By universality, it suffices to check the case when
\mathrm{Spec}\,R
is an affine sub formal scheme of
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}
. In this case, by Nakayama’s lemma, it suffices to check the condition on the
\bar{\kappa}
-points of
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}
. A point
z\in\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}(\bar{\kappa})
corresponds to a pair
(M,M^{\prime})
as in Corollary
3.6
. Then the isogeny
\tilde{\rho}_{X,+}
is induced by the map of relative Dieudonné modules
\breve{\Lambda}^{\sharp}\rightarrow M:x\mapsto\Pi\cdot x
. Recall
\mathrm{Fil}=\mathbf{V}M
. So

D(\tilde{\rho}_{X,+})^{-1}(\mathrm{Fil})=\Pi^{-1}\mathbf{V}M/\pi_{0}\breve{\Lambda}^{\sharp}=\tau^{-1}(M)/\pi_{0}\breve{\Lambda}^{\sharp}.

Since
\breve{\Lambda}\subset M
, we have
\breve{\Lambda}=\tau^{-1}(\breve{\Lambda})\subset\tau^{-1}(M)
. So

\mathrm{Image}(D((\rho_{\Lambda})_{R}))=\breve{\Lambda}/\pi_{0}\breve{\Lambda}^{\sharp}\subset D(\tilde{\rho}_{X,+})^{-1}(\mathrm{Fil}).

The condition
M=M^{\sharp}
is equivalent to the fact that
\Phi(U(X))
is Lagrangian in
V
, which in turn is equivalent to the fact that
U(X)
is Lagrangian which implies
\dim_{\bar{\kappa}}U(X)=m
.
∎

By Condition (2) of Definition
3.4
, we know
\mathrm{Image}(D((\rho_{\Lambda})_{R}))\subset D(\rho_{X,+})(q_{X}^{-1}(\mathcal{F}))
where
q_{X}:D(X)\rightarrow\Lie X
is the natural quotient homomorphism of
R
-modules (see the proof of Corollary
3.6
). Define

\mathcal{F}(X):=D(\rho_{X,+})(q_{X}^{-1}(\mathcal{F}))/\mathrm{Image}(D((\rho_{\Lambda})_{R})).

Then
\mathcal{F}(X)
is a locally free direct summand of
U(X)
of rank
m-1
.
We define a map
\phi:\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda,\bar{\kappa}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Gr}(m,V_{\bar{\kappa}})\times\mathrm{Gr}(m-1,V_{\bar{\kappa}})
by

\phi:(X,\iota,\lambda,\rho,\mathcal{F})\mapsto(U(X),\mathcal{F}(X))\in(\mathrm{Gr}(m,V_{\bar{\kappa}})\times\mathrm{Gr}(m-1,V_{\bar{\kappa}}))(R).

Lemma 3.15
.

\phi
defines a bijection between
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}(\bar{\kappa})
and
Y_{V}(\bar{\kappa})
.

Proof.

A point
z\in\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}(\bar{\kappa})
corresponds to a pair
(M,M^{\prime})
as in Corollary
3.6
.
By the definition of
\phi
we have
\phi(z)=(U,U^{\prime})
where

(U,U^{\prime})=(\Pi^{-1}\mathbf{V}M/\breve{\Lambda},M^{\prime}/\breve{\Lambda})=(\tau^{-1}(M)/\breve{\Lambda},M^{\prime}/\breve{\Lambda})=(\Phi^{-1}(M/\breve{\Lambda}),M^{\prime}/\breve{\Lambda}).

As in the proof of Lemma
3.14
, the condition
M=M^{\sharp}
is equivalent to the condition that
U
is Lagrangian.
The condition
M^{\prime}\subset M
is equivalent to
U^{\prime}\subset\Phi(U)
. The condition
M^{\prime}\subset\tau^{-1}(M)
is equivalent to
U^{\prime}\subset U
. This shows that
\phi(z)\in Y_{V}(\bar{\kappa})
.

Conversely assume
(U,U^{\prime})\in Y_{V}(\bar{\kappa})
and let
M=\Pr^{-1}(\Phi(U))
and
M^{\prime}=\Pr^{-1}(U^{\prime})
where
\Pr:\breve{\Lambda}^{\sharp}\rightarrow\breve{\Lambda}^{\sharp}/\breve{\Lambda}
is the natural quotient map. Then
by definition
\breve{\Lambda}\subset M^{\prime}\subset M\subset\breve{\Lambda}^{\sharp}
, and
M=M^{\sharp}
as
U
is Lagrangian.
Since
\mathbf{V}M\subset\mathbf{V}\breve{\Lambda}^{\sharp}=\Pi\breve{\Lambda}^{\sharp}\subset\breve{\Lambda}\subset M^{\prime}
, we have
\mathbf{V}M\subset M^{\prime}
.
We also have

\Pi M\subset\Pi\breve{\Lambda}^{\sharp}\subset\breve{\Lambda}=\tau^{-1}\breve{\Lambda}\subset\tau^{-1}(M),

and

(3.16)

\tau^{-1}(M)\subset\tau^{-1}(\breve{\Lambda}^{\sharp})=\breve{\Lambda}^{\sharp}\subset\Pi^{-1}\breve{\Lambda}\subset\Pi^{-1}M.

Hence
\Pi M\subset\tau^{-1}(M)\subset\Pi^{-1}M
.
This shows that
(M,M^{\prime})
satisfies the conditions in Proposition
3.5
and Corollary
3.6
. This defines the inverse of
\phi
on the level of
\bar{\kappa}
-points.
Hence
\phi
defines a bijection between
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}(\bar{\kappa})
and
Y_{V}(\bar{\kappa})
.
∎

Theorem 3.16
.

Let
\Lambda
be a vertex lattice of type
2m
(
m\geq 1
) in
\mathbb{V}
. Then
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}
is reduced and the morphism

\phi
defines an isomorphism
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}\rightarrow Y_{V,\bar{\kappa}}
. In particular
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}
is smooth of dimension
m
over
\bar{\kappa}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{red}}_{\Lambda}
be the underlying reduced
\bar{\kappa}
-scheme of
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}
. Lemma
3.15
shows that
\phi
induces a morphism
\phi^{\mathrm{red}}:\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{red}}_{\Lambda}\rightarrow Y_{V,\bar{\kappa}}
which is a bijection on
\bar{\kappa}
-points, in particular quasi-finite. Since
\phi^{\mathrm{red}}
is a morphism between projective varieties, it is projective.
Moreover using the theory of relative displays and windows, working with Cohen rings instead of the Witt ring, we can show that
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{red}}(R)=Y_{V}(R)
for any field
R
containing
\bar{\kappa}
by the same proof as that of Lemma
3.15
. In particular
\phi^{\mathrm{red}}
is birational. Being quasi-finite and proper at the same time, it is an isomorphism by Zariski’s main theorem since
Y_{V,\bar{\kappa}}
is normal. Now Proposition
3.12
implies that
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{red}}_{\Lambda}=\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda,\bar{\kappa}}
. By
[
RTZ13
, Lemma 10.3]
and Corollary
3.11
, we have
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}=\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda,\bar{\kappa}}
.
This finishes the proof of the theorem.
∎

Proposition 3.17
.

Let
\Lambda
be a vertex lattice of type
0
in
\mathbb{V}
. Then
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}
is the exceptional divisor
\mathrm{Exc}_{\Lambda}
and is isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{n-1}_{\bar{\kappa}}
.

Proof.

Let
R
be any
\bar{\kappa}
-algebra and
z
be any point in
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}(R)
and
(X,\iota,\lambda,\rho,\mathcal{F})
be the pullback of the universal object of
\mathcal{N}
to
z
.
As
\Lambda
is a unimodular lattice, the quasi-isogeny
\rho_{-}
has height
0
. Thus the isogeny

\rho_{X,-}=\rho^{-1}\circ(\rho_{-})_{R}:(X_{-})_{R}\rightarrow X

has height
0
and is an isomorphism, hence we can identify
(X,\ldots,\rho)
with
((X_{-})_{R},\ldots,(\rho_{-})_{R})
.
As
\Pi|_{\Lambda}=\mathbf{V}|_{\Lambda}
for any vertex lattice
\Lambda
, and
\Lie X_{-}=M(X_{-})/\mathbf{V}M(X_{-})
, the action of
\iota(\pi)
on
\Lie X_{-}
is trivial. The point
z
is uniquely determined by the filtration
\mathcal{F}\subset\Lie X
.
Hence
\mathcal{F}
can be any rank
n-1
locally free
R
-module on
\Lie X
. This shows that
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{n-1}_{\bar{\kappa}}
and is in particular reduced.
Moreover if
R=\bar{\kappa}
, then
\rho(M(X))=\breve{\Lambda}
. This shows that
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}
is a subscheme of
\mathrm{Exc}_{\Lambda}
according Definition
2.4
. By the proof of Lemma
2.5
, we know that
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}
and
\mathrm{Exc}_{\Lambda}
have the same
\bar{\kappa}
-points. As they are both reduced subscheme of
\mathcal{N}
, they must be the same. This proves the proposition.
∎

3.4.
Bruhat-Tits stratification

Lemma 3.18
.

For any pair
(M,\mathcal{F})
satisfying the condition in Proposition
3.5
, there is a unique vertex lattice
\Lambda(M)
such that
\Lambda(M)\subset M
and
\Lambda(M)
is maximal among all such vertex lattices.

Proof.

This is essentially
[
RTW14
, Proposition 4.1]
as such
M
satisfies the conditions in Proposition 2.4 of loc.cit..
∎

Theorem 3.19
.

There is a stratification of
\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{red}}
by closed strata
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}
given by

(3.17)

\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{red}}=\bigcup_{\Lambda}\ \mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}.

where the union is over all vertex lattices in
\mathbb{V}
. We call this the Bruhat-Tits stratification of
\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{red}}
. In the following, assume that
\Lambda
and
\Lambda^{\prime}
are vertex lattices of type greater than or equal to
2
, and
\Lambda_{0}
and
\Lambda^{\prime}_{0}
are vertex lattices of type
0
.

(1)

If
\Lambda\subset\Lambda^{\prime}
, then
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}
is a subscheme of
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}
.

(2)

The intersection of
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}\cap\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}
is nonempty if and only if
\Lambda^{\prime\prime}=\Lambda+\Lambda^{\prime}
is a vertex lattice, in which case we have
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}\cap\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}=\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda^{\prime\prime}}
.

(3)

The intersection of
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda^{\prime}_{0}}\cap\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda_{0}}
is always empty if
\Lambda_{0}\neq\Lambda^{\prime}_{0}
.

(4)

The intersection
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}\cap\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda_{0}}
is nonempty if and only if
\Lambda\subset\Lambda_{0}
in which case
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}\cap\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda_{0}}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}_{\bar{\kappa}}^{m-1}
where
2m
is the type of
\Lambda
.

Proof.

To prove (
3.17
),
it suffices to check this on
\bar{\kappa}
-points. A point
z\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{red}}(\bar{\kappa})
corresponds to a pair
(M,M^{\prime})
as in Proposition
3.5
. Take
\Lambda=\Lambda(M)
as in Lemma
3.18
.
If
\Lambda
has type
0
, then both
\breve{\Lambda}
and
M
are unimodular and
\breve{\Lambda}\subset M
, so they have to be equal.
Hence
z\in\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}
by Corollary
3.6
.
If
\Lambda
is not of type
0
, then
M
is not
\tau
-invariant, hence
M^{\prime}=M\cap\tau^{-1}(M)
is uniquely determined. Since
\Lambda
is
\tau
-invariant,
\breve{\Lambda}\subset M^{\prime}
. Hence
z\in\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}(\bar{\kappa})
by Corollary
3.6
. This proves (
3.17
).

(1) follows immediately from Definition
3.4
.

(2). If
\Lambda^{\prime\prime}
is a vertex lattice, then
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}\cap\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}=\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda^{\prime\prime}}
by Definition
3.4
.
Conversely if
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}\cap\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}(\bar{\kappa})
is nonempty, let
(M,M^{\prime})\in\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}\cap\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}(\bar{\kappa})
. Then
\Lambda(M)\supset\Lambda+\Lambda^{\prime}
by the maximality of
\Lambda(M)
. Then
\Lambda+\Lambda^{\prime}\subset\Lambda(M)\subset\Lambda(M)^{\sharp}\subset\Lambda^{\sharp}\cap(\Lambda^{\prime})^{\sharp}=(\Lambda+\Lambda^{\prime})^{\sharp}
. Hence
\Lambda+\Lambda^{\prime}
is a vertex lattice.

(3) follows directly from Corollary
3.6
.

(4). By Corollary
3.6
, a point
(M,M^{\prime})\in\mathcal{N}(\bar{\kappa})
is in
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}\cap\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda_{0}}
if and only if
M=\Lambda_{0}\otimes_{O_{F}}O_{\breve{F}}
and
\Lambda\subset M^{\prime}\subset M
. This show that
\Lambda\subset\Lambda_{0}
and
M^{\prime}
corresponds to a point in
\mathbb{P}(\Lambda_{0}/\Lambda)(\bar{\kappa})
.
Hence
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}\cap\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda_{0}}(\bar{\kappa})=\mathbb{P}(\Lambda_{0}/\Lambda)(\bar{\kappa})
. Similarly one can show that

\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}\cap\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda_{0}}(R)=\mathbb{P}(\Lambda_{0}/\Lambda)(R)

for any
\bar{\kappa}
-algebra
R
. This finishes the proof of (4).
∎

Proposition 3.20
.

For a rank
r
lattice
L\subset\mathbb{V}
, the reduced subscheme
\mathcal{Z}(L)_{\mathrm{red}}
of
\mathcal{Z}(L)
is a union of Bruhat-Tits strata:

(3.18)

\mathcal{Z}(L)_{\mathrm{red}}=\bigcup_{L\subset\Lambda}\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda},

where the union is taken over all vertex lattices
\Lambda
such that
L\subset\Lambda
.
Moreover, the intersection of
\mathcal{Z}(L)
with
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}
is nonempty if and only if
L\subset\Lambda^{\sharp}
.

Proof.

The proof of (
3.18
) is the same as that of
[
Shi18
, Proposition 3.8]
.

If
L\subset\Lambda^{\sharp}
and
L
is integral, define
\Lambda^{\prime}:=L+\Lambda
. Then
\Lambda^{\prime}
is a vertex lattice and
\Lambda\subset\Lambda^{\prime}
. By Theorem
3.19
(1) and the definition of
\mathcal{Z}(L)
,
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}
is in the intersection of
\mathcal{Z}(L)
and
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}
.

Conversely if the intersection of
\mathcal{Z}(L)
and
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}
is not empty, then by (
3.18
) and Theorem
3.19
, there exists a vertex lattice
\Lambda^{\prime}
such that
\Lambda\subset\Lambda^{\prime}
and
L\subset\Lambda^{\prime}
. Since
\Lambda^{\prime}\subset(\Lambda^{\prime})^{\sharp}\subset\Lambda^{\sharp}
, we know that
L\subset\Lambda^{\sharp}
. This finishes the proof of the lemma.
∎

4.
Fourier transform: the geometric side

4.1.
Horizontal and vertical part of
{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})

Definition 4.1
.

Let
L^{\flat}
be a rank
n-1
integral lattice in
\mathbb{V}
. We say that
L^{\flat}
is horizontal if one of the following conditions is satisfied.

(1)

L^{\flat}
is unimodular.

(2)

L^{\flat}
is of the form
L^{\flat}=M\obot L^{\prime}
where
M
is a unimodular sublattice of rank
n-2
such that
(M_{F})^{\bot}
(the perpendicular complement of
M_{F}
in
\mathbb{V}
) is nonsplit.

We denote the set of horizontal lattices by
\mathrm{Hor}
.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
L^{\flat}
be a rank
n-1
lattice in
\mathbb{V}
. Then

L^{\flat}
is horizontal if and only if there is a unique vertex lattice
\Lambda
which contains
L^{\flat}
. If this is the case,
\Lambda
is of type
0
.

Proof.

We first prove the “only if” direction. Let
\Lambda
be any vertex lattice containing
L^{\flat}
.
If
L^{\flat}
is unimodular, then
\Lambda
has to be of the form
L^{\flat}\obot L^{\prime}
where
L^{\prime}
is the unique unimodular lattice in
(L^{\flat}_{F})^{\bot}
. If
L^{\flat}
is of the form
M\obot L^{\prime}
such that
M
is of rank
n-2
and
(M_{F})^{\bot}
is nonsplit, then the proof of
[
Shi18
, Theorem 3.10]
implies that there is a unique vertex lattice
\Lambda^{\prime}
in
(M_{F})^{\bot}
which is of unimodular (this corresponds to the fact that the Bruhat-Tits building of
(M_{F})^{\bot}
has only one point). Then
\Lambda
must be of the form
M\obot\Lambda^{\prime}
. In both cases,
\Lambda
is unique and is of type
0
.

We now prove the “if” direction. If
t(L^{\flat})\geq 2
, then there exist a type
2
vertex lattice
\Lambda_{2}
containing
L^{\flat}
and any type
0
vertex lattice containing
\Lambda_{2}
(there are
q+1
of them) also contains
L^{\flat}
. Hence
t(L^{\flat})\leq 1
and
L^{\flat}
is of the form
M\obot L^{\prime}
such that
M
is of rank
n-2
. If
(M_{F})^{\bot}
is split and
{\mathrm{val}}(L^{\prime})>0
, then
[
HSY23b
, Corollary 3.11]
implies that there are more than one type
0
vertex lattices
\Lambda^{\prime}
in
(M_{F})^{\bot}
containing
L^{\prime}
. For any such
\Lambda^{\prime}
,
M\obot\Lambda^{\prime}
is a vertex lattice of type
0
containing
L^{\flat}
. This shows that in order for such
\Lambda
to be unique,
L^{\flat}
must satisfies the conditions in Definition
4.1
. The lemma is proved.
∎

For a rank
n-1
lattice
L^{\flat}
in
\mathbb{V}
, define

(4.1)

\mathrm{Hor}(L^{\flat})\coloneqq\{M^{\flat}\in\mathrm{Hor}\mid L^{\flat}\subset M^{\flat}\}.

When
\mathrm{dim}(\mathbb{V})=2
and
\chi(\mathbb{V})=-1
, for
y\in\mathbb{V}
,
define

\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}(y)^{\circ}\coloneqq\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathcal{Z}_{{\mathrm{val}}(y)}^{+}\sqcup\mathcal{Z}_{{\mathrm{val}}(y)}^{-}&\text{ if }{\mathrm{val}}(y)>0,\\
\mathcal{Z}_{0}&\text{ if }{\mathrm{val}}(y)=0.\end{array}\right.

Here
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cong{\mathrm{Spf}\,\mathcal{O}_{\breve{F}}}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{s}^{+}\cong\mathcal{Z}_{s}^{-}\cong\mathrm{Spf}\,W_{s}
are quasi-canonical lifting cycles defined in
[
Shi22
, §3]
where
W_{s}
is a totally ramified abelian extension of
O_{\breve{F}}
of degree
q^{s}
.
When
\mathrm{dim}(\mathbb{V})=2
and
\chi(\mathbb{V})=1
, for
y\in\mathbb{V}^{=0}
, define

\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}(y)^{\circ}
to be
\mathcal{Z}^{h}(y)
, where
\mathcal{Z}^{h}(y)\cong\mathrm{Spf}\,O_{\breve{F}}
is as in
[
HSY23b
, Theorem 4.1]
. In all cases,
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}(y)^{\circ}
is a closed subscheme of
\mathcal{N}_{2}
.

For a
M^{\flat}\in\mathrm{Hor}
, we can decompose
M^{\flat}
as
M\obot\mathrm{Span}\{y\}
where
M
is unimodular and
{\mathrm{val}}(y)
has to be zero if
(M_{F})^{\bot}
is split. By
[
HSY23a
, Proposition 2.6]
, the unimodular lattice
M
induces a closed embedding
\mathcal{N}_{2}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{N}_{n}
.
We define
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}(M^{\flat})^{\circ}
to be the image of the composed embedding
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}(y)^{\circ}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{N}_{2}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{N}_{n}
where
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}(y)^{\circ}
is the closed formal subscheme of
\mathcal{N}_{2}
defined above. Moreover by loc. cit., the definition of
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}(M^{\flat})^{\circ}
is independent of the choice of
M
.
The following is
[
HSY23a
, Theorem 4.2]
.

Theorem 4.3
.

Let
L^{\flat}
be a rank
n-1
non-degenerate integral lattice in
\mathbb{V}
, then

(4.2)

\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})_{\mathscr{H}}=\bigcup_{M^{\flat}\in\mathrm{Hor}(L^{\flat})}\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}(M^{\flat})^{\circ}.

In particular,
\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})_{\mathscr{H}}
is of pure dimension
1
. We have the following identity in
\mathrm{Gr}^{n-1}K_{0}^{\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})}(\mathcal{N})
:

(4.3)

[\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})_{\mathscr{H}}}]=\sum_{M^{\flat}\in\mathrm{Hor}(L^{\flat})}[\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}(M^{\flat})^{\circ}}].

Lemma 4.4
.

For any formal subscheme
Z
of
\mathcal{N}
and
0\leq i\leq n
,
F^{i}K_{0}^{Z}(\mathcal{N})
is an ideal in
K_{0}(\mathcal{N})
.

Proof.

By definition
F^{i}K_{0}^{Z}(\mathcal{N})
is generated by elements of the form
[\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}]
where
\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}
is a finite complex of locally free coherent
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{N}}
-modules acyclic outside a sub formal scheme
Y
of
Z
such that the codimension of
Y
in
\mathcal{N}
is greater than or equal to
i
.
By Kunneth formula for chain complexes, the product complex
\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{N}}}\mathcal{K}^{\bullet}
is acyclic outside
Y
as well for any finite complexes of locally free coherent
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{N}}
-modules
\mathcal{K}^{\bullet}
. This proves the lemma.
∎

By Lemma
4.4
, for any formal subscheme
Z
of
\mathcal{N}
, we can define a quotient ring (not necessary with identity)

(4.4)

\mathrm{Gr}^{\prime}K_{0}^{Z}(\mathcal{N}):=K_{0}^{Z}(\mathcal{N})/F^{n}K_{0}^{Z}(\mathcal{N}).

In particular
\mathrm{Gr}^{n-1}K_{0}^{Z}(\mathcal{N})=F^{n-1}K_{0}^{Z}(\mathcal{N})/F^{n}K_{0}^{Z}(\mathcal{N})
is a subgroup of
\mathrm{Gr}^{\prime}K_{0}^{Z}(\mathcal{N})
.

Let
L^{\flat}
be a rank
n-1
non-degenerate integral lattice.
Since
\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})_{\mathscr{H}}
is one-dimensional, the intersection
\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})_{\mathscr{H}}\cap\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})_{\mathscr{V}}
must be
0
-dimensional if nonempty. It follows that there is a decomposition

(4.5)

\mathrm{Gr}^{\prime}K_{0}^{\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})}(\mathcal{N})=\mathrm{Gr}^{\prime}K_{0}^{\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})_{\mathscr{H}}}(\mathcal{N})\oplus\mathrm{Gr}^{\prime}K_{0}^{\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})_{\mathscr{V}}}(\mathcal{N}).

Under this decomposition, we have

(4.6)

{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})={}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})_{\mathscr{H}}+{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})_{\mathscr{V}}\in\mathrm{Gr}^{\prime}K_{0}^{\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})}(\mathcal{N}),

where we denote by the same notation the image of
{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})
under the natural quotient map
K_{0}^{\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})}(\mathcal{N})\rightarrow\mathrm{Gr}^{\prime}K_{0}^{\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})}(\mathcal{N})
.
It follows that the element
{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})_{\mathscr{V}}\in\mathrm{Gr}^{\prime}K_{0}^{\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})}(\mathcal{N})
is canonically defined although
\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})_{\mathscr{V}}
depends on the choice of a large integer
m\gg 0
.

Since
\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})_{\mathscr{H}}
has expected dimension,
{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})_{\mathscr{H}}
is in fact in
\mathrm{Gr}^{n-1}K_{0}^{\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})}(\mathcal{N})
and is represented by the structure sheaf of
\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})_{\mathscr{H}}
. In order to match the analytic side of our conjecture, we need to slightly modify
{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})_{\mathscr{H}}
.

Definition 4.5
.

Let
L^{\flat}
be a horizontal lattice in
\mathbb{V}
. Define
{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})^{\circ}\in\mathrm{Gr}^{\prime}K_{0}^{\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})}(\mathcal{N})
by

{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})^{\circ}=\begin{cases}[\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}(L^{\flat})^{\circ}}]+\frac{1-(-1)^{n-1}}{2}[\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}_{\Lambda}}]&\text{ if }L^{\flat}\text{ is unimodular},\\
[\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}(L^{\flat})^{\circ}}]+[\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}_{\Lambda}}]&\text{ otherwise},\end{cases}

where
\Lambda
is the unique type
0
vertex lattice containing
L^{\flat}
as in Lemma
4.2
and
\mathbb{P}_{\Lambda}
is a projective line over
\bar{\kappa}
in
\mathrm{Exc}_{\Lambda}
.

Remark 4.6
.

{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})^{\circ}
is the difference cycle
\mathcal{D}(L^{\flat})
defined in
[
HSY23a
, Definition 2.15]
.

Definition 4.7
.

Let
L^{\flat}
be a rank
n-1
non-degenerate integral lattice. Define
{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})_{\mathscr{H}}^{*}\in\mathrm{Gr}^{\prime}K_{0}^{\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})}(\mathcal{N})
by

{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})_{\mathscr{H}}^{*}:=\sum_{M^{\flat}\in\mathrm{Hor}(L^{\flat})}{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}(M^{\flat})^{\circ},

where
\mathcal{Z}(M^{\flat})_{\mathscr{H}}^{\circ}
is as in Definition
4.5
.
Define the modified vertical part of the derived special cycle
{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})
by

{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})_{\mathscr{V}}^{*}:={}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})-{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})_{\mathscr{H}}^{*}\in\mathrm{Gr}^{\prime}K_{0}^{\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})}(\mathcal{N}).

For any
x\in\mathbb{V}\setminus L^{\flat}_{F}
, define

(4.7)

\displaystyle\Int_{L^{\flat},\mathscr{H}}(x):=\chi(\mathcal{N},{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})_{\mathscr{H}}^{*}\cdot[\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}(x)}]),\hbox{ and }\Int_{L^{\flat},\mathscr{V}}(x):=\chi(\mathcal{N},{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})_{\mathscr{V}}^{*}\cdot[\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}(x)}]).

Lemma 4.8
.

For a rank
n-1
non-degenerate integral lattice
L^{\flat}
, we have

{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})_{\mathscr{V}}^{*}\in\mathrm{Gr}^{\prime}K_{0}^{\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})_{\mathscr{V}}}(\mathcal{N}).

Proof.

By the definition of
{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})_{\mathscr{V}}^{*}
, the decomposition (
4.6
) and Theorem
4.3
, we have

\displaystyle{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})_{\mathscr{V}}^{*}=

\displaystyle{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})_{\mathscr{V}}+{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})_{\mathscr{H}}-\sum_{M^{\flat}\in\mathrm{Hor}(L^{\flat})}{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}(M^{\flat})^{\circ}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})_{\mathscr{V}}+\sum_{M^{\flat}\in\mathrm{Hor}(L^{\flat})}([\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}(M^{\flat})^{\circ}}]-{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}(M^{\flat})^{\circ}).

We know all terms in the last expression are in
\mathrm{Gr}^{\prime}K_{0}^{\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})_{\mathscr{V}}}(\mathcal{N})
by the definition of
{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})_{\mathscr{V}}
and Definition
4.5
.
∎

Lemma 4.9
.

If
L^{\flat}
is a horizontal lattice of rank
n-1
in
\mathbb{V}
, then

(4.8)

{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})_{\mathscr{H}}^{*}={}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat}).

In particular for any
x\in\mathbb{V}\setminus L_{F}^{\flat}
we have

\Int_{L^{\flat},\mathscr{H}}(x)=\Int_{L^{\flat}}(x).

Proof.

Let
\Lambda
be the unique type
0
vertex lattice containing
L^{\flat}
as indicated by Lemma
4.2
. Then
\Lambda\cap L^{\flat}_{F}
is the unique unimodular lattice in
\mathrm{Hor}(L^{\flat})
.
By Theorem
4.3
, we have

{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})_{\mathscr{H}}^{*}-{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})=(m-1+\frac{1-(-1)^{n-1}}{2})[\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}_{\Lambda}}]-{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})_{\mathscr{V}},

where
m:=|\mathrm{Hor}(L^{\flat})|
.
By Proposition
3.20
and Lemma
4.2
, we know that
{}^{\mathbb{L}}Z(L^{\flat})_{\mathscr{V}}\in\mathrm{Gr}^{\prime}K_{0}^{\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}}(\mathcal{N})
.
[
HSY23a
, Corollary 3.5]
implies that in fact
{}^{\mathbb{L}}Z(L^{\flat})_{\mathscr{V}}\in\mathrm{Gr}^{n-1}K_{0}^{\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}}(\mathcal{N})
, hence

{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})_{\mathscr{V}}=m^{\prime}[\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}_{\Lambda}}]

for some integer
m^{\prime}
. In order to prove (
4.8
), it suffices to show

(4.9)

m^{\prime}=m-\frac{1+(-1)^{n-1}}{2}.

Now assume
L^{\flat}=M\obot L^{\prime}
where
M
is unimodular and of rank
n-2
and
{\mathrm{val}}(L^{\prime})=a
.
Then
m=a+1
. By
[
HSY23a
, Lemma 4.4]
, we know that

\chi(\mathcal{N},{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})_{\mathscr{H}}\cdot[\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}}])=2a+1=2m-1.

By
[
HSY23a
, Corollary 3.7]
, we know

\chi(\mathcal{N},{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})_{\mathscr{V}}\cdot[\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}}])=m^{\prime}\cdot\chi(\mathcal{N},[\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}_{\Lambda}}]\cdot[\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}}])=-2m^{\prime}.

On the other hand, by
[
HSY23a
, Corollary 3.6]
,

\chi(\mathcal{N},{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})_{\mathscr{H}}\cdot[\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}}])+\chi(\mathcal{N},{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})_{\mathscr{V}}\cdot[\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}}])=\chi(\mathcal{N},{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})\cdot[\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}}])=(-1)^{n-1}.

Combine the above equations, we get (
4.9
).
∎

4.2.
Hermitian lattices and Fourier transform

We fix an additive character
\psi:F_{0}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\times}
whose conductor is
O_{F_{0}}
.
Recall that the Fourier transform with respect to
\psi
is defined by

(4.10)

\widehat{\varphi}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{V}}\varphi(y)\cdot\psi(\tr_{F/F_{0}}(x,y))d\mu(y),

where
d\mu
is the unique self-dual Haar measure on
\mathbb{V}
with respect to this transform.
For a lattice
L
in
\mathbb{V}
we use
L^{\vee}
to denote its dual under the quadratic form

\tr_{F/F_{0}}((\,,\,))
. The following lemma is well-known and easy to check.

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
L\subset\mathbb{V}
be a lattice of rank
n
and
1_{L}\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{V})
be its characteristic function. Then

\widehat{1_{L}}=\vol(L,d\mu)\cdot 1_{L^{\vee}}.

Lemma 4.11
.

Let
L
be a rank
n
lattice in
\mathbb{V}
.
A function
\varphi\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{V})
is
L
-invariant (invariant under the translation of
L
) if and only if its Fourier transform
\widehat{\varphi}
is supported on
L^{\vee}
.

Proof.

We first prove the “only if” direction.
For any
\mu\in\mathbb{V}
, let
\bar{\mu}
be its image in the quotient
\mathbb{V}/L
. Define

L(\bar{\mu}):=\mu+L.

Any
L
-invariant function
\phi\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{V})
is a linear combination of the characteristic functions
1_{L(\bar{\mu})}
. So it suffices to assume
\phi=1_{L(\bar{\mu})}
. In this case,

\widehat{\phi}(x)=\psi(\tr_{F/F_{0}}(x,\mu))\cdot\widehat{1_{L}}(x).

So
\widehat{\phi}(x)
is supported on
L^{\vee}
by Lemma
4.10
. This proves the “only if” direction.

For the “if” direction. It suffices to show that if
\varphi
is supported on
L^{\vee}
, then
\widehat{\varphi}
is
L
-invariant.
For any
z\in L
, we have

\widehat{\varphi}(x+z)=\int_{\mathbb{V}}\varphi(y)\cdot\psi(\tr_{F/F_{0}}(x,y))\cdot\psi(\tr_{F/F_{0}}(z,y))d\mu(y).

Since
\psi(\tr_{F/F_{0}}(z,y))=1
for any
z\in L
and
y\in L^{\vee}
and
\varphi
is supported on
L^{\vee}
, the above is equal to
\widehat{\varphi}(x)
.
This finishes the proof of the lemma.
∎

For an integer
m
, recall that

\mathbb{V}^{\geq m}=\{x\in\mathbb{V}\mid{\mathrm{val}}(x)\geq m\}.

Definition 4.12
.

Define
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{V})^{\geq m}
to be the subspace of
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{V})
consisting of functions
\varphi
such that
\widehat{\varphi}
is supported on
\mathbb{V}^{\geq m}
.

Lemma 4.13
.

Let
\Lambda
be a vertex lattice in
\mathbb{V}
.
Any
\Lambda
-invariant function in
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{V})
is in
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{V})^{\geq-1}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.11
, it suffices to show that
\Lambda^{\vee}\subset\mathbb{V}^{\geq-1}
. Since
\Lambda
is a vertex lattice, we have

\Lambda^{\sharp}=H^{t}\obot I_{n-2t},

for some
t
. Simple calculation gives then

\Lambda^{\vee}=\frac{1}{\pi}\Lambda^{\sharp}=\frac{1}{\pi}H^{t}\obot\frac{1}{\pi}I_{n-2t}\subset\mathbb{V}^{\geq-1}.

∎

4.3.
Fourier transform of
\Int_{L^{\flat},\mathscr{V}}

Theorem 4.14
.

Let
\Lambda
be a vertex lattice and
\mathcal{K}\in K^{\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}}_{0}(\mathcal{N})
. For any
x\in\mathbb{V}\setminus\{0\}
, the function that takes
x
to
\mathcal{K}\cdot[\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}(x)}]\in K^{\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}}_{0}(\mathcal{N})
is
\Lambda
-invariant. More precisely, for any
y\in\Lambda
such that
x+y\neq 0
, we have

(4.11)

\mathcal{K}\cdot[\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}(x)}]=\mathcal{K}\cdot[\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}(x+y)}].

Moreover, the function

\Int_{\mathcal{K}}(x):=\chi(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{K}\cdot[\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}(x)}])

extends to a
\Lambda
-invariant function in
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{V})^{\geq-1}
.

Proof.

Any element
\mathcal{K}\in K^{\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}}_{0}(\mathcal{N})\cong K^{\prime}_{0}(\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda})
is a sum of elements of the form
[\mathcal{F}]
where
[\mathcal{F}]
is a coherent sheaf of
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}}
-module. Hence it suffices to prove the theorem for
\mathcal{K}=[\mathcal{F}]
.
By
[
How19
, Corollary C]
, we know

[\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}(y)}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{N}}}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}(x)}]=[\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}(y)}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{N}}}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}(x+y)}].

For any
y\in\Lambda
with
x+y\neq 0
,
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}
is a subscheme of
\mathcal{Z}(y)
by Proposition
3.20
. Hence we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}\cdot[\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}(x)}]=

\displaystyle[\mathcal{F}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{N}}}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}(x)}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[\mathcal{F}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}}}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}(y)}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}(y)}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{N}}}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}(x)}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[\mathcal{F}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}}}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}(y)}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}(y)}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{N}}}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}(x+y)}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}\cdot[\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}(x+y)}]

We have proved the
\Lambda
-invariance of
\mathcal{K}\cdot[\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}(x)}]
. It follows that
\Int_{\mathcal{K}}(x)
is also
\Lambda
-invariant. Hence we can define
\Int_{\mathcal{K}}(0)
to be
\Int_{\mathcal{K}}(x)
for any
0\neq x\in\Lambda
and obtain a (unique)
\Lambda
-invariant function (still denoted by
\Int_{\mathcal{K}}(x)
) for all
x\in\mathbb{V}
. In particular
\Int_{\mathcal{K}}(x)
is locally constant.
If
x\notin\Lambda^{\sharp}
, by Proposition
3.20
, the intersection of
\mathcal{Z}(x)
with
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}
is empty, which implies
\Int_{\mathcal{K}}(x)=0
. This shows that the function
\Int_{\mathcal{K}}(x)
is compactly supported. Hence it is in
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{V})
and is in fact in
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{V})^{\geq-1}
by Lemma
4.13
.
This finishes the proof of the theorem.
∎

Theorem 4.15
.

For every non-degenerate lattice
L^{\flat}
of
\mathbb{V}
of rank
n-1
, the function
\mathrm{Int}_{L^{\flat},\mathscr{V}}
on
\mathbb{V}\setminus L^{\flat}_{F}
can be extended to an element in
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{V})^{\geq-1}
which we denote by the same notation.

Proof.

Lemmas
4.8
and
2.8
imply that
{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})_{\mathscr{V}}^{*}\in\mathrm{Gr}^{\prime}K_{0}^{\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{red}}}(\mathcal{N})\cap\mathrm{Gr}^{\prime}K_{0}^{\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})_{\mathscr{V}}}(\mathcal{N})
.
Lemma
2.7
implies that there exist finitely many classes
\mathcal{K}_{i}\in\mathrm{Gr}^{\prime}K_{0}^{\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{red}}}(\mathcal{N})_{\mathbb{Q}}
together with
C_{i}\in\mathbb{Q}
such that

\Int_{L^{\flat},\mathscr{V}}(x)\\
=\sum_{i}C_{i}\cdot\chi(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{K}_{i}\cdot[\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}(x)}]).

By Theorem
3.19
we may assume that
\mathcal{K}_{i}
is supported on some
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}
. Now we can apply Theorem
4.14
to conclude the proof.
∎

4.4.
Partial Fourier transform

Let
L^{\flat}
be a rank
n-1
non-degenerate lattice in
\mathbb{V}
. Let
\mathbb{W}=(L^{\flat}_{F})^{\bot}
.
For any function
\varphi
defined on
\mathbb{V}\setminus L^{\flat}_{F}
, we define its partial Fourier transform
\varphi^{\bot}
as a function on
\mathbb{W}\setminus\{0\}
by

(4.12)

\varphi^{\bot}(x):=\int_{L^{\flat}_{F}}\varphi(x+y)dy,\quad\forall\,x\in\mathbb{W}.

Theorem 4.16
.

The partial Fourier transform
\Int_{L^{\flat},\mathscr{V}}^{\bot}\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{W})^{\geq-1}
and is
\mathbb{W}^{\geq 0}
-invariant. In particular it is constant on
\mathbb{W}^{\geq 0}
.

Proof.

It is easy to see that partial Fourier transform maps
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{V})
to
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{W})
.
It remains to show that the Fourier transform of
\Int_{L^{\flat},\mathscr{V}}^{\bot}\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{W})
is supported on
\mathbb{W}^{\geq-1}
. For
x\in\mathbb{W}
, we have

\widehat{\Int_{L^{\flat},\mathscr{V}}^{\bot}}(x)=\widehat{\Int_{L^{\flat},\mathscr{V}}}(x),

where
\widehat{\Int_{L^{\flat},\mathscr{V}}}(x)
is the Fourier transform of
\Int_{L^{\flat},\mathscr{V}}\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{V})
.
Since
\widehat{\Int_{L^{\flat},\mathscr{V}}}
is supported on
\mathbb{V}^{\geq-1}
by Theorem
4.15
, we know that
\widehat{\Int_{L^{\flat},\mathscr{V}}^{\bot}}(x)
is supported on
\mathbb{W}^{\geq-1}
.

Since
\mathbb{W}
is one-dimensional,
\mathbb{W}^{\geq m}
is a full rank lattice in
\mathbb{W}
for any
m\in\mathbb{Z}
. By Lemma
4.11
and what we just proved,
\Int_{L^{\flat},\mathscr{V}}^{\bot}
is invariant under the translation of
(\mathbb{W}^{\geq-1})^{\vee}=\mathbb{W}^{\geq 0}
.
∎

5.
Review of local densities and primitive local densities

In this section, we recall various explicit formulas of local density polynomials following Section 5 of
[
HSY23a
]
.

5.1.
Basic properties of local density and primitive local density polynomials

Definition 5.1
.

Let
M
and
L
be two hermitian
O_{F}
-lattices of rank
m
and
n
respectively. Let
a
be an integer such that
(x,y)\in\pi_{0}^{-a}\partial_{F/F_{0}}^{-1}
for
x,y\in M
or
x,y\in L
. Define the local density of
M
representing
L
as

\mathrm{Den}(M,L):=\lim_{d\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\left|\mathrm{Herm}_{L,M}(O_{F_{0}}/(\pi_{0}^{d+a}))\right|}{q^{2(d+a)nm-dn^{2}}},

which is independent of the choice of
a
. Here

\mathrm{Herm}_{L,M}(O_{F_{0}}/(\pi_{0}^{d+a}))
is given by the set

\displaystyle\{\phi\in\mathrm{Hom}

{}_{O_{F}}(L/\pi_{0}^{d+a}L,M/\pi_{0}^{d+a}M)\mid(\phi(x),\phi(y))\equiv(x,y)\mod(\pi_{0}^{d}\partial_{F/F_{0}}^{-1}),\,x,y\in L\}.

In this paper, we only deal with the case when we can and will choose
a=0
.
It is well-known that there is a local density polynomial
\mathrm{Den}(M,L,X)\in\mathbb{Q}[X]
such that

(5.1)

\mathrm{Den}(M,L,q^{-2k})=\mathrm{Den}(M\obot H^{k},L).

Moreover, we denote
\mathrm{Den}(M,L)=\mathrm{Den}(M,L,1)
and

(5.2)

\mathrm{Den}^{\prime}(M,L)\coloneqq-2\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial X}\mathrm{Den}(M,L,X)|_{X=1}.

Similarly, the primitive local density polynomial
\mathrm{Pden}(M,L,X)
is defined to be the polynomial in
\mathbb{Q}[X]
such that

(5.3)

\mathrm{Pden}(M,L,q^{-2k})=\lim_{d\to\infty}q^{-d(2n(m+2k)-n^{2})}|\mathrm{Pherm}_{L,M\obot H^{k}}(O_{F_{0}}/(\pi_{0}^{d}))|,

where

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pherm}_{L,M\obot H^{k}}(O_{F_{0}}/(\pi_{0}^{d}))

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{\phi\in\mathrm{Herm}_{L,M\obot H^{k}}(O_{F_{0}}/(\pi_{0}^{d}))\mid\text{ $\phi$ is primitive}\}.

Here we recall that
\phi\in\mathrm{Herm}_{L,M\obot H^{k}}(O_{F_{0}}/(\pi_{0}^{d}))
is primitive if
\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{q}}((\phi(L)+\pi(M\obot H^{k}))/\pi(M\obot H^{k})=n
. In particular, we have
\mathrm{Pden}(M,M)=\mathrm{Den}(M,M)
since any
\phi\in\mathrm{Herm}_{M,M}(O_{F_{0}}/(\pi_{0}^{d}))
is primitive for large enough
d
.

Recall that without explicit mentioning, we assume
\epsilon=\chi(L)
. As an analogue of (
1.3
) and (
1.4
), we define

(5.4)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pden}^{\prime}(L)\coloneqq-2\cdot\frac{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}X}\big|_{X=1}\mathrm{Pden}(I_{n},L,X)}{\mathrm{Den}(I_{n},I_{n})}\text{ and }\mathrm{Pden}_{t}(L)\coloneqq\frac{\mathrm{Pden}(\Lambda_{t}^{\sharp},L)}{\mathrm{Den}(\Lambda_{t}^{\sharp},\Lambda_{t}^{\sharp})}.

To save notation, we simply denote
\mathrm{Pden}_{0}(L)
by
\mathrm{Pden}(L)
.
We define

(5.5)

\partial\mathrm{Pden}(L)\coloneqq\mathrm{Pden}^{\prime}(L)+\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{\frac{t_{\max}}{2}}c_{2j}\cdot\mathrm{Pden}_{2j}(L),

where
c_{2j}
is as in (
1.5
).

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
L
be a lattice. If there exists
x\in L
such
{\mathrm{val}}(x)\leq-1
, then
\partial\mathrm{Den}(L)=\mathrm{Den}^{\prime}(L)=\partial\mathrm{Pden}(L)=\mathrm{Pden}^{\prime}(L)=0
.

Proof.

Assume
M\cong I_{n}
or
M\cong\Lambda_{2t}
for some
t
. Then
\mathrm{Den}(M\obot H^{k},L)=0
and
\mathrm{Pden}(M\obot H^{k},L)=0
since there is no vector in
M
with valuation less than or equal to
-1
.
∎

Now we record several results that describe the relation between local density and primitive local density polynomials.

Lemma 5.3
.

[
HSY23a
, Lemma 5.1]

Let
M
and
L
be lattices of rank
m
and
n
. Then we have

\mathrm{Den}(M,L,X)=\sum_{L\subset L^{\prime}\subset L_{F}}(q^{n-m}X)^{\ell(L^{\prime}/L)}\mathrm{Pden}(M,L^{\prime},X),

where
\ell(L^{\prime}/L)=\mathrm{length}_{O_{F}}L^{\prime}/L
. Here
\mathrm{Pden}(M,L^{\prime},X)=0
for
L^{\prime}
with fundamental invariant less than the smallest fundamental invariant of
M
. In particular, the summation is finite.

Corollary 5.4
.

Let
L
be a lattice. We have the following identity:

\partial\mathrm{Den}(L)=\sum_{L\subset L^{\prime}\subset L_{F}}\partial\mathrm{Pden}(L^{\prime}).

Proof.

Since
\mathrm{Pden}(I_{n},L^{\prime},1)=\mathrm{Pden}(I_{n},L^{\prime})=0
, we have by Lemma
5.3

\displaystyle-2\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}X}\bigg|_{X=1}\mathrm{Den}(I_{n},L,X)=-2\sum_{L\subset L^{\prime}\subset L_{F}}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}X}\bigg|_{X=1}\mathrm{Pden}(I_{n},L^{\prime},X)=\sum_{L\subset L^{\prime}\subset L_{F}}\mathrm{Pden}^{\prime}(I_{n},L^{\prime}).

Similarly, according to Lemma
5.3
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Den}(\Lambda_{2j},L)=\sum_{L\subset L^{\prime}\subset L_{F}}\mathrm{Pden}(\Lambda_{2j},L^{\prime})

for
0<j\leq t_{\max}/2
. Now the corollary follows from (
1.5
) and (
5.5
).
∎

Conversely, the primitive local density polynomial is a linear combination of local density polynomials.

Theorem 5.5
.

[
HSY23a
, Theorem 5.2]

Let
M
and
L
be lattices of rank
m
and
n
. We have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pden}(M,L,X)=\sum_{i=0}^{n}(-1)^{i}q^{i(i-1)/2+i(n-m)}X^{i}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}L\subset L^{\prime}\subset\pi^{-1}L\\
\ell(L^{\prime}/L)=i\end{subarray}}\mathrm{Den}(M,L^{\prime},X).

Corollary 5.6
.

Let
L
be a lattice of rank
n
. Then

\displaystyle\partial\mathrm{Pden}(L)=\sum_{i=0}^{n}(-1)^{i}q^{i(i-1)/2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}L\subset L^{\prime}\subset\pi^{-1}L\\
\ell(L^{\prime}/L)=i\end{subarray}}\partial\mathrm{Den}(L^{\prime}).

Recall that for two lattices
L,L^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{V}
of rank
n
,
n(L^{\prime},L)=|\{L^{\prime\prime}\subset L_{F}\mid L\subset L^{\prime\prime},L^{\prime\prime}\cong L^{\prime}\}|.

Lemma 5.7
.

For two lattices
L
and
M
of the same rank
n
, we have

(5.6)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pden}(M,L)=\begin{cases}\mathrm{Den}(M,L)&\text{ if $M\cong L$},\\
0&\text{ if $M\not\cong L$}.\end{cases}

Moreover,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Den}(M,L)=n(M,L)\cdot\mathrm{Den}(M,M).

In particular, if
\chi(M)\not=\chi(L)
, then
\mathrm{Den}(M,L)=0
.

Proof.

First of all, for
M\cong L
,
\mathrm{Pden}(M,L)=\mathrm{Den}(M,L)
by the definition of primitive local density.
Now we show that if
\mathrm{Pden}(M,L)\not=0
, then
M\cong L
. If
\mathrm{Pden}(M,L)\not=0
, then for any large enough
d
we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pherm}_{L,M}(O_{F_{0}}/(\pi_{0}^{d}))\not=0.

Let
\phi\in\mathrm{Pherm}_{L,M}(O_{F_{0}}/(\pi_{0}^{d}))
be a primitive embedding and
L_{(d)}=L\otimes_{O_{F_{0}}}O_{F_{0}}/(\pi_{0}^{d})
. Let
\overline{\phi(L_{(d)})}
be the image of
\phi(L_{(d)})
in
\overline{M_{(d)}}
. Since
\phi
is primitive, we have
\overline{\phi(L_{(d)})}=\overline{M_{(d)}}
. Then by Nakayama’s lemma, we know
\phi(L_{(d)})=M_{(d)}
. Hence
\phi
is an isometry between
L_{(d)}\cong M_{(d)}
. Since this holds for any large enough
d
, we have
L\cong M
.

Now the formula of
\mathrm{Den}(M,L)
follows from (
5.6
) and Lemma
5.3
.
∎

Corollary 5.8
.

Let
L
be a lattice. Then for any even integer
t
such that
0\leq t\leq t_{\max}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Den}_{t}(L):=\frac{\mathrm{Den}(\Lambda_{t}^{\sharp},L)}{\mathrm{Den}(\Lambda_{t}^{\sharp},\Lambda_{t}^{\sharp})}\in\mathbb{Z}.

Corollary 5.9
.

Assume
L\not\cong\Lambda_{t}^{\sharp}
for any vertex lattice
\Lambda_{t}
with
t>0
. Then

\displaystyle\partial\mathrm{Pden}(L)=\mathrm{Pden}^{\prime}(L).

Corollary 5.10
.

Let
c_{t}
be the coefficients in (
1.6
) with even
t
and
0<t\leq t_{\max}
. Then

\displaystyle c_{t}=-\mathrm{Pden}^{\prime}(\Lambda_{t}^{\sharp}).

Proof.

On the one hand, combining Corollary
5.6
with (
1.6
), we obtain

\displaystyle\partial\mathrm{Pden}(\Lambda_{t}^{\sharp})=0.

On the other hand, by Lemma
5.7
and (
1.5
),

\displaystyle\partial\mathrm{Pden}(\Lambda_{t}^{\sharp})=\mathrm{Pden}^{\prime}(\Lambda_{t}^{\sharp})+c_{t}.

∎

Write
\Lambda_{t}^{\sharp}=H^{t/2}\obot L_{1}
where
L_{1}
is unimodular of rank
n_{1}
. Then by Lemma
5.13
, Corollaries
6.2
and
5.10
, we have (see the following subsections for the relevant notations)

\displaystyle c_{t}=-2\frac{\prod_{\ell=1}^{t/2-1}(1-q^{2\ell})}{\mathrm{Den}(I_{n},I_{n})}\cdot\sum_{i=0}^{n_{1}}\prod_{\ell=0}^{n_{1}-i-1}(1-q^{2(\ell+t/2)})\cdot\sum_{V_{1}\in\mathrm{Gr}(i,\overline{L_{1}})(\mathbb{F}_{q})}|\mathrm{O}(V_{1},\overline{I_{n}})|.

Combining this formula with Lemma
5.16
and Lemma
5.18
, we can compute
c_{t}
explicitly. We give some examples here.

Example 5.11
.

If
n
is odd, we have

(5.7)

\displaystyle c_{t_{\mathrm{max}}}=-\frac{\mathrm{Pden}^{\prime}(I_{n},\Lambda_{t_{\max}}^{\sharp})}{\mathrm{Den}(I_{n},I_{n})}=\frac{(-1)^{\frac{n+1}{2}}}{q^{(\frac{n-1}{2})^{2}}(q^{\frac{n-1}{2}}+1)}.

If
n
is even and
\epsilon=1
, we have

\displaystyle c_{t_{\mathrm{max}}}=-\frac{\mathrm{Pden}^{\prime}(I_{n},\Lambda_{t_{\max}}^{\sharp})}{\mathrm{Den}(I_{n},I_{n})}=\frac{(-1)^{\frac{n}{2}}}{q^{\frac{n}{2}(\frac{n}{2}-1)}(q^{\frac{n}{2}}+1)}.

We also give a list of
c_{t}
for small
n
,
t
and
\epsilon=1
in the following table:

2

3

4

5

6

2

\frac{-1}{q+1}

\frac{1}{q(q+1)}

\frac{-1}{q^{2}(q+1)}

\frac{1}{q^{3}(q+1)}

\frac{-1}{q^{4}(q+1)}

4

0

0

\frac{1}{q^{2}(q^{2}+1)}

\frac{-1}{q^{4}(q^{2}+1)}

\frac{1}{q^{6}(q^{2}+1)}

6

0

0

0

0

-\frac{1}{q^{6}(q^{3}+1)}

In fact, computation suggests that
c_{t}
has the following simple formula:

(5.8)

\displaystyle c_{t}=\begin{cases}\frac{(-1)^{n+t/2}(q^{n/2}+1)}{q^{t/2(n-t-1)}(q^{n/2}-1)(q^{t/2}+1)}&\text{ if $n$ is even and $\epsilon=-1$},\\
\frac{(-1)^{n+t/2}}{q^{t/2(n-t/2-1)}(q^{t/2}+1)}&\text{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

We believe this formula can be proved by similar method as in §
7
. Since this formula is not needed in our proof, we omit the details.

5.2.
Explicit formulas for some simple primitive local density polynomials

Lemma 5.12
.

(
[
LL22
, Lemma 2.15]
)
Assume
L
is an integral lattice of rank
n
. Then

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pden}(H^{k},L)=\prod_{\ell=0}^{n-1}(1-q^{-2k+2\ell}).

Lemma 5.13
.

[
HSY23a
, Corollary 5.8]

Assume
L=H^{j}\obot L_{1}
where
j>0
and
L_{1}
is an integral lattice of rank
n_{1}
. Then

\displaystyle\mathrm{Den}(I_{m},L,X)

\displaystyle=\Big(\prod_{\ell=0}^{j-1}(1-q^{2\ell}X)\Big)\mathrm{Den}(I_{m},L_{1},q^{2j}X),

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pden}(I_{m},L,X)

\displaystyle=\Big(\prod_{\ell=0}^{j-1}(1-q^{2\ell}X)\Big)\mathrm{Pden}(I_{m},L_{1},q^{2j}X).

In particular,

(5.9)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pden}^{\prime}(I_{m},L)=2\Big(\prod_{\ell=1}^{j-1}(1-q^{2\ell})\Big)\mathrm{Pden}(I_{m},L_{1},q^{2j}).

Proof.

First, by
[
HSY23a
, Corollary 5.8]
and Lemma
5.12
,

(5.10)

\mathrm{Den}(I_{m},L,X)=\Big(\prod_{\ell=0}^{j-1}(1-q^{2\ell}X)\Big)\mathrm{Den}(I_{m},L_{1},q^{2j}X).

Notice that if
L\subset L^{\prime}
and
L^{\prime}
is not of the form
H^{j}\obot L_{1}^{\prime}
, then there exists
v\in L^{\prime}\setminus L
such that
\mathrm{Pr}_{H_{F}^{j}}(v)\neq 0
and
\mathrm{Pr}_{H_{F}^{j}}(v)\not\in H_{j}
. Hence some fundamental invariant of
L^{\prime}
is less than or equal to
-2
. Hence
\mathrm{Den}(I_{m},L^{\prime},X)=0
by Lemma
5.2
. Now Theorem
5.5
and (
5.10
) implies

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pden}(I_{m},L,X)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{n}(-1)^{i}q^{i(i-1)/2+i(n-m)}X^{i}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1}\subset L_{1}^{\prime}\subset\pi^{-1}L_{1}\\
\ell(L_{1}^{\prime}/L_{1})=i\end{subarray}}\mathrm{Den}(I_{m},H^{j}\obot L_{1}^{\prime},X)

\displaystyle=\Big(\prod_{\ell=0}^{j-1}(1-q^{2\ell}X)\Big)\sum_{i=0}^{n_{1}}(-1)^{i}q^{i(i-1)/2+i(n-m-2j)}(q^{2j}X)^{i}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1}\subset L_{1}^{\prime}\subset\pi^{-1}L_{1}\\
\ell(L^{\prime}/L)=i\end{subarray}}\mathrm{Den}(I_{m},L_{1}^{\prime},q^{2j}X)

\displaystyle=\Big(\prod_{\ell=0}^{j-1}(1-q^{2\ell}X)\Big)\mathrm{Pden}(I_{m},L_{1},q^{2j}X)

as expected.
∎

Definition 5.14
.

Assume
U
and
V
are quadratic spaces over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. We define
\mathrm{O}(U,V)
to be the set of isometries from
U
into
V
, and
M(U,V)
to be the set of subspaces
V_{1}\subset V
such that
V_{1}\cong U
. Moreover, we define
m(U,V)=|M(U,V)|
.

Definition 5.15
.

We define
U_{i}^{\epsilon}
to be the
i
-dimensional non-degenerate quadratic space over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
with
\chi(U_{n}^{\epsilon})=\epsilon.
Moreover, we define
0_{i}
to be the
i
-dimensional totally isotropic space.

Lemma 5.16
.

[
HSY23a
, Lemma A.11]

Assume
L=I_{n-t}^{\epsilon_{1}}\obot L_{2}
where
L_{2}
is a lattice of full type
t
and
n\leq m
. Then

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pden}(I_{m}^{\epsilon_{2}},L)=q^{-mn+n^{2}}\cdot|\mathrm{O}(0_{t}\obot U_{n-t}^{\epsilon_{1}},U_{m}^{\epsilon_{2}})|.

Specifically, we have by
[
LZ22b
, Lemma 3.2.1]
,

\displaystyle|\mathrm{O}(0_{j}\obot U_{k}^{\epsilon_{1}},U_{m}^{\epsilon})|

\displaystyle=q^{(k+j)(2m-k-j-1)/2}\prod_{\lfloor\frac{m-k}{2}\rfloor+1-j\leq l\leq\lfloor\frac{m-1}{2}\rfloor}(1-q^{-2l})

\displaystyle\quad\cdot\begin{cases}(1+\epsilon\epsilon_{1}q^{-\frac{m-k}{2}+j})&\mathrm{if}\,m\equiv k\equiv 1\pmod{2},\\
1&\mathrm{if}\,m\equiv k-1\equiv 1\pmod{2},\\
(1-\epsilon q^{-\frac{m}{2}})&\mathrm{if}\,m\equiv k-1\equiv 0\pmod{2},\\
(1-\epsilon q^{-\frac{m}{2}})(1+\epsilon\epsilon_{1}q^{-\frac{m-k}{2}+j})&\mathrm{if}\,m\equiv k\equiv 0\pmod{2}.\end{cases}

Corollary 5.17
.

Let
I_{n}
be the unimodular lattice of rank
n
and sign
-\epsilon
. Then

\displaystyle\mathrm{Den}(I_{n},I_{n})=\begin{cases}2q^{\frac{n(n-1)}{2}}\prod_{s=1}^{\frac{n-1}{2}}(1-q^{-2s})&\text{ if $n$ is odd},\\
2q^{\frac{n(n-1)}{2}}(1+\epsilon q^{-\frac{n}{2}})\prod_{s=1}^{\frac{n}{2}-1}(1-q^{-2s})&\text{ if $n$ is even}.\end{cases}

5.3.
Counting formulas for subspaces of a quadratic space over
\mathbb{F}_{q}

The main results of §
6
transforms the calculation of primitive local density polynomial into a sum over the subspaces of a quadratic space over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
.
In this subsection, we count the number of such subspaces with a fixed quadratic form.

Lemma 5.18
.

Given quadratic spaces
U
and
V
over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, let
M(U,V)
be the set of subspaces
V_{1}\subset V
such that
V_{1}\cong U
, and let
m(U,V)=|M(U,V)|
. Then

\displaystyle m(0_{j}\obot U_{k}^{\epsilon_{2}},U_{n}^{\epsilon})

\displaystyle=\frac{|\mathrm{O}(0_{j}\obot U_{k}^{\epsilon_{2}},U_{n}^{\epsilon})|}{q^{jk}|\mathrm{O}(U_{k}^{\epsilon_{2}},U_{k}^{\epsilon_{2}})|\cdot|\mathrm{GL}_{j}(\mathbb{F}_{q})|}.

In particular,

(5.11)

m(0_{j}\obot U_{k}^{\epsilon_{2}},U_{n}^{\epsilon})=q^{-jk}m(0_{j},U_{n-k}^{\delta(n,k,\epsilon,\epsilon_{2})})m(U_{k}^{\epsilon_{2}},U_{n}^{\epsilon}),

where

(5.12)

\delta(n,k,\epsilon,\epsilon_{2})=\begin{cases}\epsilon&\text{ if $k=0$},\\
-\epsilon\epsilon_{2}&\text{ if both $k$ and $n-k$ are odd},\\
\epsilon\epsilon_{2}&\text{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

Proof.

We prove the first identity first.
Fix a subspace
V_{1}
of
U_{n}^{\epsilon}
such that
V_{1}\cong 0_{j}\obot U_{k}^{\epsilon_{2}}
. Then by Witt’s theorem we have a surjection

\displaystyle\mathrm{O}(0_{j}\obot U_{k}^{\epsilon_{2}},U_{n}^{\epsilon})\to M(0_{j}\obot U_{k}^{\epsilon_{2}},U_{n}^{\epsilon}),\quad\phi\to\phi(V_{1}).

Moreover, each fiber of this surjection is in bijection with
\mathrm{O}(0_{j}\obot U_{k}^{\epsilon_{2}})
. Any
\phi\in\mathrm{O}(0_{j}\obot U_{k}^{\epsilon_{2}})
is determined uniquely by
\phi_{1}=\phi|_{0_{j}}
and
\phi_{2}=\phi|_{U_{k}^{\epsilon_{2}}}
. The number of different choices of
\phi_{1}
is
|\mathrm{GL}_{j}(\mathbb{F}_{q})|
. The number of different choices of
\phi_{2}
is
q^{jk}|\mathrm{O}(U_{k}^{\epsilon_{2}},U_{k}^{\epsilon_{2}})|
.
∎

Lemma 5.19
.

For any
\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}\in\{\pm 1\}
, we have

\displaystyle m(0_{j}\obot U_{k}^{\epsilon_{2}},0_{t}\obot U_{n-t}^{\epsilon_{1}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\mathrm{min}\{t,j\}}\binom{t}{\ell}_{q}\cdot q^{(t-\ell)(j+k-\ell)}m(0_{j-\ell}\obot U_{k}^{\epsilon_{2}},U_{n-t}^{\epsilon_{1}}).

Proof.

Let
V
and
U
be quadratic spaces over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
such that
V\cong 0_{t}\obot U_{n-t}^{\epsilon}
, and
U\cong 0_{j}\obot U_{k}^{\epsilon_{2}}.
Let
R\cong 0_{t}
be the radical of
V
.
First, we consider a partition of

M(U,V)=\bigsqcup_{\ell=0}^{\mathrm{min}\{t,j\}}M_{\ell}(U,V)

such that

\displaystyle V_{1}\in M_{\ell}(U,V)\text{ if and only if }\mathrm{dim}_{\mathbb{F}_{q}}(V_{1}\cap R)=\ell.

The number of choices of
\ell
-dimensional subspace of
R
is
\binom{t}{\ell}_{q}
. Now we fix an
\ell
-dimensional subspace
W
of
R
. Let
\overline{R}\cong 0_{t-\ell}
be the radical of the quotient space of
V/W
. Then a choice of
V_{1}\in M_{\ell}(U,V)
such that
V_{1}\cap R=W
corresponds to an element of

\displaystyle S=\{\overline{V_{1}}\subset V/W\mid\overline{V_{1}}\cap\overline{R}=\{0\}\text{ and }\overline{V_{1}}\cong 0_{j-\ell}\obot U_{k}^{\epsilon_{2}}\}.

Write
V/W=\overline{R}\obot\overline{V_{2}}\cong 0_{t-\ell}\obot U_{n-t}^{\epsilon_{1}}
. Let
\mathrm{Pr}:V/W\rightarrow\bar{V}_{2}
be the natural quotient map.
For
\overline{V_{1}}\in S
, the condition
\overline{V_{1}}\cap\overline{R}=\{0\}
implies that
\mathrm{Pr}(\overline{V_{1}})\cong 0_{j-\ell}\obot U_{k}^{\epsilon_{2}}
by the rank-nullity theorem. Moreover, the following map

\displaystyle S\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}M(0_{j-\ell}\obot U_{k}^{\epsilon_{2}},\overline{V_{2}}),\quad\overline{V_{1}}\mapsto\mathrm{Pr}(\overline{V_{1}})

is a surjection with each fiber in a bijection with
\overline{R}^{j+k-\ell}
.
∎

Corollary 5.20
.

\displaystyle|\mathrm{O}(0_{j}\obot U_{k}^{\epsilon_{2}},0_{t}\obot U_{n-t}^{\epsilon})|

\displaystyle=q^{jk}|\mathrm{O}(U_{k}^{\epsilon_{2}},U_{k}^{\epsilon_{2}})|\cdot|\mathrm{GL}_{j}(\mathbb{F}_{q})|\cdot m(0_{j}\obot U_{k}^{\epsilon_{2}},0_{t}\obot U_{n-t}^{\epsilon})

\displaystyle=|\mathrm{GL}_{j}(\mathbb{F}_{q})|\cdot\sum_{\ell=0}^{\mathrm{min}\{t,j\}}\binom{t}{\ell}_{q}\cdot q^{(t-\ell)(j+k-\ell)+\ell k}\cdot\frac{|\mathrm{O}(0_{j-\ell}\obot U_{k}^{\epsilon_{2}},U_{n-t}^{\epsilon})|}{|\mathrm{GL}_{j-\ell}(\mathbb{F}_{q})|}.

We will also need the following lemma later (Section 7), which follows from Lemmas
5.18
and
5.16
.

Lemma 5.21
.

For integers
0\leq r\leq n
and
\epsilon_{1},\epsilon=\pm 1
, we have

\frac{m(U_{r}^{-\epsilon_{1}},U_{n}^{\epsilon})}{m(U_{r}^{\epsilon_{1}},U_{n}^{\epsilon})}=\begin{cases}1&\mathrm{if}\,r\equiv n-1\equiv 1\pmod{2},\\
\frac{1-\epsilon\epsilon_{1}q^{-\frac{n-r}{2}}}{1+\epsilon\epsilon_{1}q^{-\frac{n-r}{2}}}&\mathrm{if}\,r\equiv n\equiv 1\pmod{2},\\
\frac{1-\epsilon_{1}q^{-\frac{r}{2}}}{1+\epsilon_{1}q^{-\frac{r}{2}}}&\mathrm{if}\,r\equiv n-1\equiv 0\pmod{2},\\
\frac{1-\epsilon\epsilon_{1}q^{-\frac{n-r}{2}}}{1+\epsilon\epsilon_{1}q^{-\frac{n-r}{2}}}\cdot\frac{1-\epsilon_{1}q^{-\frac{r}{2}}}{1+\epsilon_{1}q^{-\frac{r}{2}}}&\mathrm{if}\,r\equiv n\equiv 0\pmod{2},\end{cases}

and

\frac{m(U_{r+1}^{\epsilon_{1}},U_{n}^{\epsilon})}{m(U_{r}^{\epsilon_{1}},U_{n}^{\epsilon})}=\frac{q^{n-2r-1}(1-(-1)^{n-r}\epsilon\epsilon_{1}q^{-\lfloor\frac{n-r}{2}\rfloor})}{1-(-1)^{r+1}\epsilon_{1}q^{-\lfloor\frac{r+1}{2}\rfloor}}.

Lemma 5.22
.

Assume
i\leq r\leq n
and
\epsilon,\sigma,\delta^{\prime}\in\{\pm 1\}
. Let
\delta(r,i,\delta^{\prime},\sigma)
or
\delta(n,i,\epsilon,\sigma)
be as in (
5.12
). Then

m(U_{i}^{\sigma},U_{n}^{\epsilon})m(U_{r-i}^{\delta(r,i,\delta^{\prime},\sigma)},U_{n-i}^{\delta(n,i,\epsilon,\sigma)})=m(U_{r}^{\delta^{\prime}},U_{n}^{\epsilon})m(U_{i}^{\sigma},U_{r}^{\delta^{\prime}}).

Proof.

Let
V=U_{n}^{\epsilon}
and
S
be the following set of flags in
V
,

S=\{0\subset F_{1}\subset F_{2}\subset V\mid F_{1}\cong U_{i}^{\sigma},F_{2}\cong U_{r}^{\delta^{\prime}}\}.

We can count the cardinality of
S
in two ways. One way is to first count the number of
F_{1}\in M(U_{i}^{\sigma},V)
, then for a fixed
F_{1}
count the number of
F_{2}^{\prime}\in M(U_{r-i}^{\delta(r,i,\delta^{\prime},\sigma)},(F_{1})^{\bot})
which has a one-to-one correspondence with
F_{2}=F_{2}^{\prime}\obot F_{1}\in M(U_{r}^{\delta^{\prime}},V)
. This way we get

\#|S|=m(U_{i}^{\sigma},U_{n}^{\epsilon})m(U_{r-i}^{\delta(r,i,\delta^{\prime},\sigma)},U_{n-i}^{\delta(n,i,\epsilon,\sigma)}).

On the other hand, we can first count the number of
F_{2}\in M(U_{r}^{\delta^{\prime}},V)
, then count the number of
F_{1}\in M(U_{i}^{\sigma},F_{2})
and get

\#|S|=m(U_{r}^{\delta^{\prime}},U_{n}^{\epsilon})m(U_{i}^{\sigma},U_{r}^{\delta^{\prime}}).

This finishes the proof of the lemma.
∎

5.4.
q
-binomial theorem

In this subsection we discuss the
q
-binomial theorem and related results, which are used repeatedly in §
7
to obtain certain vanishing results and transform complicated linear combinations into simple formulas.

Definition 5.23
.

The
q
-analogue of
\binom{n}{i}
is defined to be

\binom{n}{i}_{q}\coloneqq\frac{(q^{n}-1)\cdots(q^{n-i+1}-1)}{(q^{i}-1)\cdots(q-1)}.

In fact,
\binom{n}{i}_{q}
is the number of
i
-dimensional subspaces of a
n
-dimensional vector space over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
.
Now we recall the
q
-binomial theorem.

Lemma 5.24

(
q
-binomial theorem)
.

The following identity between polynomials of
X
holds:

(5.13)

\displaystyle\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}(1-q^{i}X)=\sum_{i=0}^{n}(-1)^{i}q^{\frac{i(i-1)}{2}}\binom{n}{i}_{q}X^{i}.

Corollary 5.25
.

Let
f
be a polynomial of degree
\leq n-1
. Then

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{n}(-1)^{i}q^{\frac{i(i-1)}{2}}\binom{n}{i}_{q}\cdot f(q^{-i})=0.

Proof.

Let
f=a_{n-1}x^{n-1}+\cdots+a_{0}
. For
0\leq s\leq n-1
, by evaluating (
5.13
) at
X=q^{-s}
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{n}(-1)^{i}q^{\frac{i(i-1)}{2}}\binom{n}{i}_{q}\cdot a_{s}q^{-si}=0.

Hence

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{n}(-1)^{i}q^{\frac{i(i-1)}{2}}\binom{n}{i}_{q}\cdot f(q^{-i})=\sum_{s=0}^{n-1}\sum_{i=0}^{n}(-1)^{i}q^{\frac{i(i-1)}{2}}\binom{n}{i}_{q}\cdot a_{s}q^{-si}=0.

∎

The following is in some sense an inverse of
q
-binomial theorem that will be used in §
7
.

Lemma 5.26
.

\sum_{i=0}^{n}(-1)^{i}q^{\frac{i(i-1)}{2}}\cdot\binom{n}{i}_{q}\cdot\prod_{\ell=0}^{i-1}(1+q^{-\ell}X)=(-X)^{n}.

Proof.

Let
g_{i}(X)=\prod_{\ell=0}^{i-1}(q^{-\ell}X+1)
. Since
\{g_{i}(X)\}
forms a basis of the vector space of polynomials of degree
\leq n
, there exist
a_{n,i}\in\mathbb{C}
such that

\displaystyle(-X)^{n}=\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{n,i}\cdot g_{i}(X).

Notice that
g_{i+1}(X)=(1+q^{-i}X)g_{i}(X)
, hence
Xg_{i}(X)=q^{i}(g_{i+1}(X)-g_{i}(X))
. Then we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{n+1}a_{n+1,i}\cdot g_{i}(X)=(-X)^{n+1}=(-X)\cdot(-X)^{n}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{n}(-a_{n,i})\cdot Xg_{i}(X)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{n}(-a_{n,i})q^{i}\cdot(g_{i+1}(X)-g_{i}(X)).

As a result, we have

(5.14)

\displaystyle a_{n+1,i}=\begin{cases}a_{n,0}&\text{ if $i=0$},\\
q^{i}a_{n,i}-q^{i-1}a_{n,i-1}&\text{ if $0<i<n+1$},\\
-a_{n,n}q^{n}&\text{ if $i=n+1$}.\end{cases}

It is easy to check that
b_{n,i}=(-1)^{i}q^{\frac{i(i-1)}{2}}\cdot\binom{n}{i}_{q}
satisfies (
5.14
) and that
a_{1,i}=b_{1,i}
. So we have
a_{n,i}=b_{n,i}
.
∎

6.
Decomposition of primitive local densities

This section is devoted to prove the following decomposition of the primitive local density polynomial, which is a vast generalization of
[
HSY23a
, Proposition A.14]
and one of the main tools we use to prove Theorem
7.1
.

Theorem 6.1
.

Assume that
L
is an integral lattice of rank
n
. For any
m\geq 0
we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pden}(I_{m},L,X)=\sum_{i=0}^{n}\mathrm{Pden}^{n-i}(I_{m},L,X),

where
\mathrm{Pden}^{n-i}(I_{m},L,X)
is a polynomial characterized by

(6.1)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pden}^{n-i}(I_{m},L,q^{-2k})

\displaystyle=q^{-2ki}\mathrm{Pden}(H^{k},0_{n-i})\sum_{V_{1}\in\mathrm{Gr}(i,\overline{L})(\mathbb{F}_{q})}q^{(n-i)i}\mathrm{Pden}(I_{m},L_{V_{1}}),

where
0_{n-i}
is a totally isotropic lattice of rank
n-i
and
L_{V_{1}}\subset L
is a sublattice of rank
i
such that
\overline{L}_{V_{1}}=V_{1}
.

Here, an important special case is when
m=n
. In this case,
\mathrm{Pden}^{0}(I_{n}^{-\epsilon},L,X)=0
since
\chi(L)\neq\chi(I_{n}^{-\epsilon})
. Hence,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pden}(I_{n},L,X)=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\mathrm{Pden}^{n-i}(I_{n},L,X).

Applying the formulas of
\mathrm{Pden}(H^{k},0_{n-i})
and
\mathrm{Pden}(I_{m},L_{V_{1}})
given in Lemmas
5.12
and
5.16
, we obtain the following corollary.

Corollary 6.2
.

Let
L
be an integral lattice of rank
n
. We have

(6.2)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pden}(I_{m},L,X)=\sum_{i=0}^{n}\Big((q^{n-m}X)^{i}\prod_{\ell=0}^{n-i-1}(1-q^{2\ell}X)\Big)\cdot\sum_{V_{1}\in\mathrm{Gr}(i,\overline{L})(\mathbb{F}_{q})}|\mathrm{O}(V_{1},\overline{I_{m}})|.

In particular,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pden}^{\prime}(I_{n},L)=2\sum_{i=0}^{n}

\displaystyle\Big(\prod_{\ell=1}^{n-i-1}(1-q^{2\ell})\Big)\cdot\sum_{V_{1}\in\mathrm{Gr}(i,\overline{L})(\mathbb{F}_{q})}|\mathrm{O}(V_{1},\overline{I_{n}})|.

When
L
is a full type lattice of rank
n
,
\overline{L}
is totally isotropic. So we obtain

Corollary 6.3
.

Let
L
be a full type lattice of rank
n
. Then

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pden}(I_{m},L,X)=\sum_{i=0}^{n}

\displaystyle\Big((q^{n-m}X)^{i}\prod_{\ell=0}^{n-i-1}(1-q^{2\ell}X)\Big)\cdot\binom{n}{i}_{q}|\mathrm{O}(0_{i},\overline{I_{m}})|.

In particular,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pden}^{\prime}(I_{n},L)=2\sum_{i=0}^{n}

\displaystyle\Big(\prod_{\ell=1}^{n-i-1}(1-q^{2\ell})\Big)\cdot\binom{n}{i}_{q}|\mathrm{O}(0_{i},\overline{I_{n}})|.

Here by Lemma
5.16
, we have

\displaystyle|\mathrm{O}(0_{i},\overline{I_{m}})|

\displaystyle=q^{\frac{i(i-1)}{2}}\cdot\begin{cases}\prod_{1\leq\ell\leq i}\left(q^{m+1-2\ell}-1\right)&\text{if $m$ is odd},\\
\left(q^{m/2}-\chi(I_{m})\right)\left(q^{m/2-i}+\chi(I_{m})\right)\cdot\prod_{1\leq\ell<i}\left(q^{m-2\ell}-1\right)&\text{if $m$ is even}.\end{cases}

Proof of Theorem
6.1
.

To save notation, we use
M
to denote
I_{m}
in this proof.
Recall that by (
5.3
),

\mathrm{Pden}(M,L,q^{-2k})=\lim_{d\to\infty}q^{-dn(2(m+2k)-n)}|\mathrm{Pherm}_{L,M\obot H^{k}}(O_{F_{0}}/(\pi_{0}^{d}))|.

First, we define a partition

\mathrm{Pherm}_{L,M\obot H^{k}}(O_{F_{0}}/(\pi_{0}^{d}))=\bigsqcup_{0\leq i\leq n}\mathrm{Pherm}_{L,M\obot H^{k}}^{i}(O_{F_{0}}/(\pi_{0}^{d})),

where

(6.3)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pherm}_{L,M\obot H^{k}}^{i}(O_{F_{0}}/(\pi_{0}^{d}))

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{\phi\in\mathrm{Pherm}_{L,M\obot H^{k}}(O_{F_{0}}/(\pi_{0}^{d}))\mid\mathrm{dim}_{\mathbb{F}_{q}}\overline{\mathrm{Pr}_{H^{k}}(\phi(L))}=i\}.

Here
\mathrm{Pr}_{H^{k}}
denote the projection map to
H^{k}
, and
\overline{\mathrm{Pr}_{H^{k}}(\phi(L))}
denote the image of
\mathrm{Pr}_{H^{k}}(\phi(L))
in
\overline{H}^{k}
.
As a result, we have

\mathrm{Pden}(M,L,X)=\sum_{i=0}^{n}\mathrm{Pden}^{i}(M,L,X),

where
\mathrm{Pden}^{i}(M,L,X)
is the function such that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pden}^{i}(M,L,q^{-2k})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\lim_{d\to\infty}q^{-(2n(2k+m)-n^{2})d}|\mathrm{Pherm}_{L,M\obot H^{k}}^{i}(O_{F_{0}}/(\pi_{0}^{d}))|.

We need to count
|\mathrm{Pherm}_{L,M\obot H^{k}}^{i}(O_{F_{0}}/(\pi_{0}^{d}))|
. For
\phi\in\mathrm{Pherm}_{L,M\obot H^{k}}^{i}(O_{F_{0}}/(\pi_{0}^{d})),
it induces

\overline{\phi}:V=\overline{L}\longrightarrow M\obot H^{k}/\pi(M\obot H^{k}),\text{ and }\overline{\phi}_{H^{k}}\coloneqq\mathrm{Pr}_{\overline{H}^{k}}\circ\overline{\phi}.

By the definition of
\mathrm{Pherm}_{L,M\obot H^{k}}^{i}(O_{F_{0}}/(\pi_{0}^{d}))
,
For a
(n-i)
-dimensional subspace
V_{1}\subset\overline{L}
, let

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pherm}_{L,M\obot H^{k}}^{V_{1}}(O_{F_{0}}/(\pi_{0}^{d}))=\{\phi\in\mathrm{Pherm}_{L,M\obot H^{k}}^{i}(O_{F_{0}}/(\pi_{0}^{d}))\mid\mathrm{Ker}(\overline{\phi}_{H^{k}})=V_{1}\subset\overline{L}\}.

Since
\mathrm{Ker}(\overline{\phi}_{H^{k}})\subset\overline{L}
has dimension
n-i
for any
\phi\in\mathrm{Pherm}_{L,M\obot H^{k}}^{i}(O_{F_{0}}/(\pi_{0}^{d}))
, we have

(6.4)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pherm}_{L,M\obot H^{k}}^{i}(O_{F_{0}}/(\pi_{0}^{d}))=\bigsqcup_{V_{1}\in\mathrm{Gr}(n-i,V)(\mathbb{F}_{q})}\mathrm{Pherm}_{L,M\obot H^{k}}^{V_{1}}(O_{F_{0}}/(\pi_{0}^{d})).

We need to show

(6.5)

\displaystyle q^{-(2(m+2k)n-n^{2})d}|\mathrm{Pherm}_{L,M\obot H^{k}}^{V_{1}}(O_{F_{0}}/(\pi_{0}^{d}))|=q^{(n-i)i}X^{n-i}\mathrm{Pden}(H^{k},L_{V_{2}})\cdot\mathrm{Pden}(M,L_{V_{1}}).

Let
V_{2}
be a subspace of
V
such that
V=V_{1}\oplus V_{2}
. Let
L_{V_{1}}\subset L
be a sublattice of rank
n-i
such that the image of
L_{V_{1}}
in
V
is
V_{1}
. Similarly, let
L_{V_{2}}\subset L
be a sublattice of rank
i
such that the image of
L_{V_{2}}
in
V
is
V_{2}
. Let
\phi_{i}=\phi|_{L_{V_{i}}}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
. According to Lemma
6.4
, the number of different choices of
\phi_{2}
is given by

(6.6)

\displaystyle|\mathrm{Pherm}_{L_{V_{2}},M\obot H^{k}}^{i}(O_{F_{0}}/(\pi_{0}^{d}))|=q^{i(2(m+2k)-i)d}\mathrm{Pden}(H^{k},L_{V_{2}}).

Now for a fixed
\phi_{2}\in\mathrm{Pherm}_{L_{V_{2}},M\obot H^{k}}^{i}(O_{F_{0}}/(\pi_{0}^{d}))
, let

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pherm}_{L_{V_{1}},M\obot H^{k}}^{\phi_{2}}(O_{F_{0}}/(\pi_{0}^{d}))

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{\phi_{1}\in\mathrm{Pherm}_{L_{V_{1}},M\obot H^{k}}(O_{F_{0}}/(\pi_{0}^{d}))\mid(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})\in\mathrm{Pherm}_{L,M\obot H^{k}}^{V_{1}}(O_{F_{0}}/(\pi_{0}^{d}))\}.

Claim: For any
\phi_{2}\in\mathrm{Pherm}_{L_{V_{2}},M\obot H^{k}}^{i}(O_{F_{0}}/(\pi_{0}^{d}))
,

(6.7)

\displaystyle|\mathrm{Pherm}_{L_{V_{1}},M\obot H^{k}}^{\phi_{2}}(O_{F_{0}}/(\pi_{0}^{d}))|

\displaystyle=q^{(2d-1)(2k-i)(n-i)}|\mathrm{Pherm}_{L_{V_{1}},M}(O_{F_{0}}/(\pi_{0}^{d}))|

\displaystyle=q^{(2d-1)(2k-i)(n-i)+(2m(n-i)-(n-i)^{2})d}\mathrm{Pden}(M,L_{V_{1}}).

Assuming the claim holds, (
6.5
) follows from (
6.6
) and (
6.7
) since for any fixed
\phi_{2}
we have

\displaystyle|\mathrm{Pherm}_{L,M\obot H^{k}}^{V_{1}}(O_{F_{0}}/(\pi_{0}^{d}))|=|\mathrm{Pherm}_{L_{V_{2}},M\obot H^{k}}^{i}(O_{F_{0}}/(\pi_{0}^{d}))|\cdot|\mathrm{Pherm}_{L_{V_{1}},M\obot H^{k}}^{\phi_{2}}(O_{F_{0}}/(\pi_{0}^{d}))|.

Proof of the claim:
For
\phi_{1}\in\mathrm{Pherm}_{L_{V_{1}},M\obot H^{k}}^{\phi_{2}}(O_{F_{0}}/(\pi_{0}^{d})),
write
\phi_{1}=\phi_{1,H^{k}}+\phi_{1,M}
, where
\phi_{1,H^{k}}=\mathrm{Pr}_{H^{k}}\circ\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{1,M}=\mathrm{Pr}_{M}\circ\phi_{1}
. First,
for any
g\in\mathrm{U}(M\obot H^{k})
, one can directly check the map

(6.8)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pherm}_{L_{V_{1}},M\obot H^{k}}^{\phi_{2}}(O_{F_{0}}/(\pi_{0}^{d}))

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Pherm}_{L_{V_{1}},g(M\obot H^{k})}^{g\circ\phi_{2}}(O_{F_{0}}/(\pi_{0}^{d})),

\displaystyle\phi_{1}

\displaystyle\mapsto g\circ\phi_{1}

is well-defined and is in fact a bijection.
Then according to Lemma
6.5
, we may assume
\phi_{2}(L_{V_{2}})\subset H^{k}
.

Now finding
\phi_{1}
such that
(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})\in\mathrm{Pherm}_{L,M\obot H^{k}}^{V_{1}}(O_{F_{0}}/(\pi_{0}^{d}))
is equivalent to finding
\phi_{1}
such that
\phi_{1,M}
is primitive,
\phi_{1,H^{k}}\in\pi H^{k}
,

(6.9)

\displaystyle(\phi_{1}(v),\phi_{2}(w))\equiv(v,w)\mod(\pi^{2d-1})\text{ for any $v\in L_{V_{1}},w\in L_{V_{2}}$},

and

(6.10)

\displaystyle(\phi_{1}(v),\phi_{1}(w))\equiv(v,w)\mod(\pi^{2d-1})\text{ for any $v\in L_{V_{1}},w\in L_{V_{1}}$}.

We consider condition (
6.9
) first. Since
\phi_{2}(L_{V_{2}})\subset H^{k}
, we have

\displaystyle(\phi_{1,H^{k}}(v),\phi_{2}(w))\equiv(\phi_{1}(v),\phi_{2}(w))\text{ for any $v\in L_{V_{1}},w\in L_{V_{2}}$}.

When
k
is large enough, we can always find and fix a
\phi_{1,H^{k}}^{\prime}
that satisfies (
6.9
). Then finding
\phi_{1,H^{k}}
that satisfies (
6.9
) is equivalent to find
\Phi_{1,H^{k}}=\phi_{1,H^{k}}-\phi_{1,H^{k}}^{\prime}
, which satisfies

\displaystyle(\Phi_{1,H^{k}}(v),\phi_{2}(w))\equiv 0\mod(\pi^{2d-1})\text{ for any $v\in L_{V_{1}},w\in L_{V_{2}}$},

Then according to Lemma
6.6
, the number of different choices for
\phi_{1,H^{k}}
is
q^{(2d-1)(2k-i)(n-i)}.

Now we consider condition (
6.10
). Since
\phi_{1,H^{k}}(v)\in\pi H^{k}
for any
v\in L_{V_{1}}
,
(
6.10
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle(\phi_{1,M}(v),\phi_{1,M}(w))+\pi\alpha\equiv(\phi_{1}(v),\phi_{1}(w))\equiv(v,w)\mod(\pi^{2d-1})\text{ for any $v,w\in L_{V_{1}}$},

for some
\alpha\in\mathcal{O}_{F}
. By Lemma
5.16
, for a unimodular lattice
M
and any integral lattice
N
,
\mathrm{Pden}(M,N)
only depends on
\overline{M}
and
\overline{N}
.
Hence, for our purpose, we may replace (
6.10
) by

\displaystyle(\phi_{1,M}(v),\phi_{1,M}(w))\equiv(\phi_{1}(v),\phi_{1}(w))\equiv(v,w)\mod(\pi^{2d-1})\text{ for any $v,w\in L_{V_{1}}$}.

Therefore the number of different choices of primitive
\phi_{1,M}
is given by

\displaystyle q^{(2m(n-i)-(n-i)^{2})d}\mathrm{Pden}(M,L_{V_{1}}).

As a result, we have

\displaystyle|\mathrm{Pherm}_{L_{V_{1}},M\obot H^{k}}^{\phi_{2}}(O_{F_{0}}/(\pi_{0}^{d}))|=q^{(2d-1)(2k-i)(n-i)}\cdot q^{(2m(n-i)-(n-i)^{2})d}\mathrm{Pden}(M,L_{V_{1}}).

This finishes the proof of the claim.
∎

Lemma 6.4
.

Assume that
L
is an integral lattice of rank
n
and
k\geq n
.
Then

\displaystyle|\mathrm{Pherm}_{L,I_{m}\obot H^{k}}^{n}(O_{F_{0}}/(\pi_{0}^{d}))|=q^{2mnd}|\mathrm{Pherm}_{L,H^{k}}(O_{F_{0}}/(\pi_{0}^{d}))|.

Proof.

For
\phi\in\mathrm{Pherm}_{L,I_{m}\obot H^{k}}^{n}(O_{F_{0}}/(\pi_{0}^{d}))
, we may identify
\phi
with
(\phi_{H^{k}},\phi_{I_{m}})
where
\phi_{H^{k}}=\mathrm{Pr}_{H^{k}}\circ\phi
and
\phi_{I_{m}}=\mathrm{Pr}_{I_{m}}\circ\phi
. As

|\Hom_{O_{F}}(L,I_{m})(O_{F_{0}}/(\pi_{0}^{d}))|=q^{2mnd},

it suffices to show that for any fixed
\varphi\in\Hom_{O_{F}}(L,I_{m})
, we have

(6.11)

\displaystyle|\{\phi\in\mathrm{Pherm}_{L,I_{m}\obot H^{k}}^{n}(O_{F_{0}}/(\pi_{0}^{d}))\mid\phi_{I_{m}}=\varphi\}|=|\mathrm{Pherm}_{L,H^{k}}(O_{F_{0}}/(\pi_{0}^{d}))|.

Let
L_{\varphi}
be the lattice
L
equipped with the hermitian form
(x,y)_{L_{\varphi}}\coloneqq(\phi_{H^{k}}(x),\phi_{H^{k}}(y))
where
\phi
is any element in
\mathrm{Pherm}_{L,I_{m}\obot H^{k}}^{n}(O_{F_{0}}/(\pi_{0}^{d}))
such that
\phi_{I_{m}}=\varphi
. Since each such
\phi
is an isometry and
\phi_{I_{m}}
is fixed,
(\,,\,)_{L_{\varphi}}
is independent of the choice of
\phi
.
Then we have a bijection

(6.12)

\displaystyle\{\phi\in\mathrm{Pherm}_{L,I_{m}\obot H^{k}}^{n}(O_{F_{0}}/(\pi_{0}^{d}))\mid\phi_{I_{m}}=\varphi\}

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Pherm}_{L_{\varphi},H^{k}}(O_{F_{0}}/(\pi_{0}^{d})),

\displaystyle\phi

\displaystyle\mapsto\phi_{H^{k}}.

Since
L
is integral and
\phi
is an isometric embedding,
L_{\phi_{H^{k}}}
is also integral. Then according to
[
LL22
, Lemma 2.16]
,

\displaystyle|\mathrm{Pherm}_{L_{\varphi},H^{k}}(O_{F_{0}}/(\pi_{0}^{d}))|=|\mathrm{Pherm}_{L,H^{k}}(O_{F_{0}}/(\pi_{0}^{d}))|.

Combining with the bijection in (
6.12
), this proves (
6.11
), hence finishes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Lemma 6.5
.

Assume that
M
is a unimodular lattice,
L
is an integral lattice of rank
n
, and
\phi:L\rightarrow M\obot H^{k}
is a primitive isometric embedding such that
\mathrm{Pr}_{H^{k}}(\phi(L))
is primitive in
H^{k}
. Then there exists a
g\in\mathrm{U}(M\obot H^{k})
such that
g(\phi(L))\subset H^{k}
.

Proof.

Consider the non-degenerate symplectic space over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
:
(\overline{H}^{k},\langle\,,\,\rangle)=(\overline{H}^{k},\overline{\pi(\,,\,)})
. Let
\overline{v}
denote the image of
v\in H^{k}
in
\overline{H}^{k}
. Since
\mathrm{Pr}_{H^{k}}(\phi(L))
is primitive in
H^{k}
,
V(L)\coloneqq\overline{\mathrm{Pr}_{H^{k}}(\phi(L))}
is a
n
-dimensional subspace. Since
M
and
L
are integral,
\mathrm{Pr}_{H^{k}}(\phi(L))
is integral. Hence
V(L)
is an isotropic subspace. Let
\{\ell_{1},\cdots,\ell_{n}\}
be a basis of
L
,
\ell_{s,H^{k}}=\mathrm{Pr}_{H^{k}}(\ell_{s})
and
e_{s}=\overline{\ell}_{s,H^{k}}
. Since
V(L)
is an
n
-dimensional isotropic space, we have
k\geq n
and we can extend
\{e_{1},\cdots,e_{n}\}
to a standard symplectic basis
\{e_{1},f_{1},\cdots,e_{k},f_{k}\}
of
\overline{H}^{k}
, where
(e_{s},f_{t})=\delta_{st}
, and
(e_{s},e_{t})=(f_{s},f_{t})=0
for
1\leq s,t\leq k
.

Now let
\{\tilde{f}_{1},\cdots,\tilde{f}_{n}\}
be a lifting of
\{f_{1},\cdots,f_{n}\}
. In particular, for
1\leq s\leq n
, we have
(\ell_{s},\tilde{f}_{s})=\pi^{-1}+x
for some
x\in O_{F}
. Therefore,
L\oplus\langle\tilde{f}_{1}\cdots,\tilde{f}_{n}\rangle\cong H^{n}
. Hence, there exists
g\in\mathrm{U}(M\obot H^{k})
such that
g(L\oplus\langle\tilde{f}_{1}\cdots,\tilde{f}_{n}\rangle)\subset H^{k}
.
∎

Lemma 6.6
.

Let
N\subset H^{k}
be a primitive integral lattice of rank
i
. Then

\displaystyle\#\{w\in\pi H^{k}/\pi_{0}^{d}H^{k}\mid(N,w)=0\mod(\pi^{2d-1})\}=q^{(2d-1)(2k-i)}.

Proof.

Through this proof, we use
\overline{L}
to denote the image of
L
in
\overline{H}^{k}
for any sublattice
L
of
H^{k}
.
Let
N^{\perp}
be the perpendicular lattice of
N
in
H^{k}
. First we show
N^{\perp}
is primitive of rank
2k-i
. If
N^{\perp}
is not primitive, then there exists
v\in N^{\perp}
such that
\pi^{-1}v\in H^{k}\setminus N^{\perp}.
However,
(\pi^{-1}v,N)=0
, hence
\pi^{-1}v\in N^{\perp}
, which is a contradiction.

We claim that for any
w\in\pi H^{k}
and
\pi(N,w)=0\mod\pi^{a}
with
a\geq 0
, there exists a
x\in\pi^{a}H^{k}
such that
w-x\in N^{\perp}
. We prove the lemma by assuming the claim, and give the proof of the claim in the last paragraph. Taking
a=2d
, the claim implies that

\displaystyle\#\{w\in\pi H^{k}/\pi_{0}^{d}H^{k}\mid(N,w)=0\mod(\pi^{2d-1})\}

\displaystyle=\#\{w\in\pi(N^{\perp}+\pi_{0}^{d}H^{k})/\pi_{0}^{d}H^{k}\mid(N,w)=0\mod(\pi^{2d-1})\}.

Since
N^{\perp}
is primitive of rank
2k-i
, we have

\displaystyle\#\{w\in\pi N^{\perp}/\pi_{0}^{d}H^{k}\mid(N,w)=0\mod(\pi^{2d-1})\}=q^{(2d-1)(2k-i)}.

This proves the lemma assuming the claim.

Now we prove the claim.
Consider the symplectic space over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
:
(\overline{H}^{k},\overline{\pi(\,,\,)})
. Since
N
is integral,
\overline{N}
is isotropic in
\overline{H}^{k}
. Let
\overline{N}
be spanned by
\{e_{1},\cdots,e_{i}\}
. Then we may extend
\{e_{1},\cdots,e_{i}\}
to a standard symplectic basis
\{e_{1},f_{1},\cdots,e_{k},f_{k}\}
of
\overline{H}^{k}
, where
(e_{s},f_{t})=\delta_{st}
, and
(e_{s},e_{t})=(f_{s},f_{t})=0
for
1\leq s,t\leq k
. Let
\{\tilde{e}_{1},\tilde{f}_{1},\cdots,\tilde{e}_{k},\tilde{f}_{k}\}
be a lifting of
\{e_{1},f_{1},\cdots,e_{k},f_{k}\}
. By our choice of
\tilde{e}_{s}
, we can find a basis
\{w_{1},\cdots,w_{i}\}
of
N
such that
w_{s}-\tilde{e}_{s}\in\pi H^{k}
for any
1\leq s\leq i
. Consider
x=a_{1}\tilde{f}_{1}+\cdots+a_{i}\tilde{f}_{i}\in\langle\tilde{f}_{1},\cdots,\tilde{f}_{k}\rangle
. In order to have
w-x\in N^{\perp}
, we need to solve the following system of equations:

(6.13)

\displaystyle\pi(w_{s},x)=\pi(w_{s},w)\text{ for $1\leq s\leq i$}.

Let
A
denote the
i\times i
matrix corresponding to this system of linear equations. Since
w_{s}-\tilde{e}_{s}\in\pi H^{k}
, we have
A\equiv\mathrm{Id}_{i}\mod(\pi)
. Therefore, there exists a unique solution
x
of (
6.13
). Moreover, since
\pi(N,w)=0\mod\pi^{a}
, we have
\pi(w_{s},w)\in(\pi^{a})
for
1\leq s\leq i
. Then (
6.13
) implies that
a_{s}\in(\pi^{a})
, hence
x\in\pi^{a}H^{k}
. The claim is proved.
∎

7.
Explicit formulas for
\mathrm{Pden}^{\prime}(L)

7.1.
Explicit formulas and consequences

The goal of this section is to establish the following formulas for

(7.1)

\displaystyle\partial\mathrm{Pden}(L)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Pden}^{\prime}(L)+\sum_{j=0}^{t_{\mathrm{max}}/2}c_{2j}\mathrm{Pden}_{2j}(L).

Here

\mathrm{Pden}^{\prime}(L)=\frac{\mathrm{Pden}^{\prime}(I_{n},L)}{\mathrm{Den}(I_{n},I_{n})}

is normalized as in (
5.4
). Recall, from Lemma
5.9
, that

(7.2)

\partial\mathrm{Pden}(L)=\mathrm{Pden}^{\prime}(L)

when
L
is not dual to some vertex lattice
\Lambda_{t}
of positive type
t>0
.

Theorem 7.1
.

Let
L\subset\mathbb{V}
be a lattice of rank
n
.

(1)

If
L
is not integral, then

\partial\mathrm{Pden}(L)=0.

(2)

If
L
is unimodular, then

\partial\mathrm{Pden}(L)=\mathrm{Pden}^{\prime}(L)=\begin{cases}1,&\text{if $n$ is odd},\\
0,&\text{if $n$ is even}.\end{cases}

(3)

If
L=I_{n-t}\obot L_{2}
where
L_{2}
is of full type
t
, then

\displaystyle\partial\mathrm{Pden}(L)=\mathrm{Pden}^{\prime}(L)=\begin{cases}\prod_{\ell=1}^{\frac{t-1}{2}}(1-q^{2\ell}),&\text{ if $t$ is odd},\\
(1-\chi(L_{2})q^{\frac{t}{2}})\prod_{\ell=1}^{\frac{t}{2}-1}(1-q^{2\ell}),&\text{ if $t$ is even}.\end{cases}

Corollary 7.2
.

Let
L
be a lattice. Then
\partial\mathrm{Den}(L)\in\mathbb{Z}.
Moreover,
\partial\mathrm{Den}(L)=0
for non-integral
L
.

Proof.

According to Corollary
5.4
, we have

\partial\mathrm{Den}(L)=\sum_{L\subset L^{\prime}\subset L_{F}}\partial\mathrm{Pden}(L^{\prime}).

Now Theorem
7.1
implies that
\partial\mathrm{Pden}(L^{\prime})\in\mathbb{Z}
, hence
\partial\mathrm{Den}(L)\in\mathbb{Z}
. If
L
is non-integral, then
\partial\mathrm{Pden}(L^{\prime})=0
for each
L^{\prime}
such that
L\subset L^{\prime}
by
(1)
of Theorem
7.1
.
∎

As another corollary, we prove the following cancellation law for
\partial\mathrm{Den}(L)
. Recall that for Hermitian lattices
L
and
L^{\prime}
of the same rank,
n(L^{\prime},L)=\#\{L^{\prime\prime}\subset L_{F}\mid L\subset L^{\prime\prime},L^{\prime\prime}\cong L^{\prime}\}
.

Corollary 7.3
.

Let
L=L_{1}\obot L_{2}\subset\mathbb{V}
be a rank
n
lattice, with

L_{1}
being unimodular and
L_{i}
of rank
n_{i}
. Then

(7.3)

\displaystyle\partial\mathrm{Den}(L)-\partial\mathrm{Den}(L_{2})=n(I_{n_{2}},L_{2})\cdot(\delta_{\mathrm{odd}}(n)-\delta_{\mathrm{odd}}(n_{2})).

Proof.

By Corollary
5.4
and Lemma
7.4
, we have

\displaystyle\partial\mathrm{Den}(L)=\sum_{L\subset L^{\prime}\subset L_{F}}\partial\mathrm{Pden}(L^{\prime})=\sum_{L_{2}\subset L_{2}^{\prime}\subset L_{2,F}}\partial\mathrm{Pden}(L_{1}\obot L_{2}^{\prime}).

Similarly,

\displaystyle\partial\mathrm{Den}(L_{2})=\sum_{L_{2}\subset L_{2}^{\prime}\subset L_{2,F}}\partial\mathrm{Pden}(L_{2}^{\prime}).
