Hence

\displaystyle\partial\mathrm{Den}(L)-\partial\mathrm{Den}(L_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{L_{2}\subset L_{2}^{\prime}\subset L_{2,F}}(\partial\mathrm{Pden}(L_{1}\obot L_{2}^{\prime})-\partial\mathrm{Pden}(L_{2}^{\prime})).

If
L_{2}^{\prime}
is not integral, then both
\partial\mathrm{Pden}(L_{1}\obot L_{2}^{\prime})
and

\partial\mathrm{Pden}(L_{2}^{\prime})
vanishes by
(1)
of Theorem
7.1
.
If
L_{2}^{\prime}
is integral but not unimodular, then
(3)
of Theorem
7.1
implies
\partial\mathrm{Pden}(L_{1}\obot L_{2}^{\prime})-\partial\mathrm{Pden}(L_{2}^{\prime})=0.

Hence

(7.4)

\displaystyle\partial\mathrm{Den}(L)-\partial\mathrm{Den}(L_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}L_{2}\subset L_{2}^{\prime}\subset L_{2,F}\\
L_{1}\obot L_{2}^{\prime}\cong\Lambda_{0}\end{subarray}}(\partial\mathrm{Pden}(L_{1}\obot L_{2}^{\prime})-\partial\mathrm{Pden}(L_{2}^{\prime})).

Combining (
7.4
) with (2) of Theorem
7.1
, we have

\displaystyle\partial\mathrm{Den}(L)-\partial\mathrm{Den}(L_{2})=n(I_{n_{2}},L_{2})\cdot(\delta_{\mathrm{odd}}(n)-\delta_{\mathrm{odd}}(n_{2})).

This proves the corollary.
∎

Lemma 7.4
.

Assume
L=L_{1}\obot L_{2}
is a lattice where
L_{1}
is unimodular. If
L\subsetneq L^{\prime}\subset\pi^{-1}L
and
L^{\prime}
is not of the form
L_{1}\obot L_{2}^{\prime}
, then
L^{\prime}
is not integral and
\partial\mathrm{Pden}(L^{\prime})=0
.

Proof.

Consider the
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-vector space
\pi^{-1}L/L
. Since we assume
L^{\prime}
is not of the form
L_{1}\obot L_{2}^{\prime}
, there exists
v\in L^{\prime}\setminus L
such that
\mathrm{Pr}_{\pi^{-1}L_{1}}(v)\not=0
, which in turn implies that
L^{\prime}
is not integral. Hence
\partial\mathrm{Pden}(L^{\prime})=0
by
(1)
of Theorem
7.1
.
∎

7.2.
Proof strategy

The proof of Theorem
7.1
occupies the rest of this section. Since the proof is rather long and technical, we summarize the main idea of the proof first. When there is some
x\in L
with
{\mathrm{val}}(x)\leq-1
,
\partial\mathrm{Pden}(L)=0
by Lemma
5.2
. Otherwise, write

L=H^{j}\obot I_{n_{1}-t}\obot L_{2},

where
L_{2}
is of full type
t
. There are four cases.

(a)

The case
n_{1}-t=0
(i.e.,
L=H^{j}\oplus L_{2}
) is significantly simpler than the general case, and we will deal with it in next subsection although it is part of the general case. For example, when
L
is of full type, the reduction
\overline{L}
of
L
modulo
\pi
is a totally isotropic quadratic space over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. Hence, the summation in Corollary
6.2
is simply:

\displaystyle\sum_{V_{1}\in\mathrm{Gr}(i,\overline{L})(\mathbb{F}_{q})}|\mathrm{O}(V_{1},\overline{I_{m}})|=\binom{n}{i}_{q}|\mathrm{O}(0_{i},\overline{I_{m}})|.

An application of
q
-binomial theorem settles this case.

(b)

The case
j=0
, i.e.,
L
is integral.

(c)

The case
j>0
and
t>0
.

(d)

The case that
t=0
and
j>0
is part of the modification assumption.

In general, the problem becomes harder when
n_{1}-t
is larger. In fact, when
t>0
, i.e.,
n_{1}-t<n_{1}
, (b) and (c) can be proved via Corollary
6.2
and an involved application of the induction formulas of
\mathrm{Den}(I_{n},L)
established in
[
HSY23a
]
. However, when
t=0
, i.e.,
L
is unimodular, this method fails. To overcome this difficulty and give a uniform proof of
(b)
and
(c)
, we introduce a new method which is different from
[
HSY23a
]
even in the case
n\leq 3
.

To illustrate the idea, we stick to case
(b)
for now. The first key step is to discover a finer structure of
\partial\mathrm{Pden}(L)
and prove the following formula (Lemma
7.11
):

(7.5)

\displaystyle\partial\mathrm{Pden}(L)=(*)\sum_{r=0}^{\min\{n-t,n-1\}}\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\binom{n-r}{i-r}_{q}(-1)^{n-1+i+r}q^{\frac{(i-r)(i-r-1)}{2}}q^{rt}\cdot g(n,n_{1},r,q^{-i}),

where
(*)
is some constant number and
g(n,n_{1},r,X)
is a linear combination of polynomials of degree
n-1
. The second key observation is that there is a lot of cancellation underlying this linear combination. Indeed, we show for
r<n
that
g(n,n_{1},r,X)
is actually of degree
\leq n-r-1
and is essentially a simple multiple of some simple polynomial (denoted by
h(n,r,X)
) (Lemmas
7.13
and
7.15
). This enables us to apply
q
-binomial theorem (Corollary
5.25
) to the inner sums in (
7.5
). Consequently, we obtain

(7.6)

\displaystyle\partial\mathrm{Pden}(L)=(*)\sum_{r=0}^{\mathrm{m}(n,t)}(-1)^{r}q^{\frac{(n-r)(n-r-1)}{2}}q^{rt}\cdot g(n,n-t,r,q^{-n}).

The last step is to evaluate this sum and the result is given by Lemma
7.16
. It is in this step that the case
n_{1}=n
(
L
is unimodular) becomes different: the sum above is a sum from
r=0
only to
n-1
, not to
n_{1}=n
. To make it worse, the ‘missing’ term
g(n,n,n,X)
is in fact ill-behaved.

One common strategy in proving Lemmas
7.15
and
7.16
is to express both sides of the identity as (uniquely) linear combinations of certain basis of polynomials, and prove that the coefficients satisfy the same recursion formulas and the same initial conditions. Here we use crucially the combinatorical properties of
m(U,V)
(Lemma
5.18
) for
U
and
V
quadratic spaces over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
.

In Case (c), the derivative becomes the value of some primitive local density polynomials at some non-central point
q^{j}
by Lemma
5.13
. Strikingly, the formula for this value (see (
7.6
)) is very similar to the formula for the derivative
\mathrm{Pden}(I_{n_{1}-t}\obot L_{2})
(see (
7.6
)). Proof of Cases (b) and (c) will be given in Subsection
7.6
after long preparation in Subsections
7.4
and
7.5
.

7.3.
The case
n_{1}-t=0

In this subsection we assume that
n_{1}-t=0
and divide it further into two subcases:
j=0
or
j>0
.

Proposition 7.5
.

Assume that
L=H^{j}\obot L_{2}
where
j>0
and
L_{2}
is of full type and has rank
n_{2}=n-2j
. Then

\displaystyle\partial\mathrm{Pden}(L)=0.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.13
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pden}^{\prime}(I_{n},L)=2\Big(\prod_{\ell=1}^{j-1}(1-q^{2\ell})\Big)\mathrm{Pden}(I_{n},L_{2},q^{2j}).

Hence it suffice to show

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pden}(I_{n},L_{2},q^{n-n_{2}})=0.

We prove the odd
n
case and leave the even
n
case to the reader. According to Corollary
6.3
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pden}(I_{n},L_{2},q^{n-n_{2}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{n_{2}}(-1)^{n_{2}-i}q^{\frac{i(i-1)}{2}}\binom{n_{2}}{i}_{q}\cdot\prod_{\ell=0}^{n_{2}-i-1}(q^{2\ell+n-n_{2}}-1)\cdot\prod_{\ell=1}^{i}(q^{n+1-2\ell}-1)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{n_{2}}(-1)^{n_{2}-i}q^{\frac{i(i-1)}{2}}\binom{n_{2}}{i}_{q}\cdot\prod_{\ell=\frac{n-n_{2}}{2}}^{\frac{n+n_{2}}{2}-i-1}(q^{2\ell}-1)\cdot\prod_{\ell=\frac{n+1}{2}-i}^{\frac{n-1}{2}}(q^{2\ell}-1).

We can factor out

\displaystyle\prod_{\ell=\frac{n-n_{2}}{2}}^{\frac{n-1}{2}}(q^{2\ell}-1)

so that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pden}(I_{n},L_{2},q^{n-n_{2}})\prod_{\ell=\frac{n-1}{2}}^{\frac{n-n_{2}}{2}}(q^{2\ell}-1)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{n_{2}}(-1)^{n_{2}-i}q^{\frac{i(i-1)}{2}}\binom{n_{2}}{i}_{q}g(n_{2},q^{-i}),

where

(7.7)

g(n_{2},X)=\prod_{\ell=\frac{n+1}{2}}^{\frac{n_{2}+n}{2}-1}(q^{2\ell}X^{2}-1)

is a polynomial of
X
of degree
n_{2}-1
. Now
\mathrm{Pden}(I_{n},L_{2},q^{n-n_{2}})=0
by Corollary
5.25
.
∎

Proposition 7.6
.

Assume that
L
is a full type lattice of rank
n
. We have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pden}^{\prime}(L)=\begin{cases}\prod_{\ell=1}^{\frac{n-1}{2}}(1-q^{2\ell}),&\text{ if $n$ is odd},\\
(1-\epsilon q^{\frac{n}{2}})\prod_{\ell=1}^{\frac{n}{2}-1}(1-q^{2\ell}),&\text{ if $n$ is even}.\end{cases}

Proof.

First of all, recall that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pden}^{\prime}(L)=\frac{\mathrm{Pden}^{\prime}(I_{n},L)}{\mathrm{Den}(I_{n},I_{n})},

where

(7.8)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Den}(I_{n},I_{n})=\begin{cases}2q^{(\frac{n-1}{2})^{2}}\prod_{\ell=1}^{\frac{n-1}{2}}(q^{2\ell}-1)&\text{ if $n$ is odd},\\
2q^{(\frac{n}{2})(\frac{n}{2}-1)}(q^{\frac{n}{2}}+\epsilon)\prod_{\ell=1}^{\frac{n}{2}-1}(q^{2\ell}-1)&\text{ if $n$ is even}.\end{cases}

We verify the even
n
case and leave the odd
n
case to the reader.
Direct calculation using Corollary
6.3
gives

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pden}^{\prime}(I_{n},L)\Big((q^{\frac{n}{2}}+\epsilon)\prod_{\ell=1}^{\frac{n}{2}-1}(q^{2\ell}-1)\Big)^{-1}

\displaystyle=2\sum_{i=0}^{\frac{n}{2}-1}(-1)^{n-i-1}q^{\frac{i(i-1)}{2}}\binom{n}{i}_{q}(q^{\frac{n}{2}-i}-\epsilon)\prod_{\ell=\frac{n}{2}-i+1}^{n-i-1}(q^{2\ell}-1)

\displaystyle=2\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}(-1)^{n-i-1}q^{\frac{i(i-1)}{2}}\binom{n}{i}_{q}(q^{\frac{n}{2}-i}-\epsilon)\prod_{\ell=\frac{n}{2}+1}^{n-1}(q^{2\ell-2i}-1).

According to Corollary
5.25
,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}(-1)^{n-i-1}q^{\frac{i(i-1)}{2}}\binom{n}{i}_{q}(q^{\frac{n}{2}-i}-\epsilon)\prod_{\ell=\frac{n}{2}+1}^{n-1}(q^{2\ell-2i}-1)

\displaystyle=q^{\frac{n(n-1)}{2}}(q^{-\frac{n}{2}}-\epsilon)\prod_{\ell=\frac{n}{2}+1}^{n-1}(q^{2\ell-2n}-1)

\displaystyle=q^{(\frac{n}{2})(\frac{n}{2}-1)}(1-\epsilon q^{\frac{n}{2}})\prod_{\ell=1}^{\frac{n}{2}-1}(1-q^{2\ell}).

Now by (
7.8
), we conclude that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pden}^{\prime}(L)=(1-\epsilon q^{\frac{n}{2}})\prod_{\ell=1}^{\frac{n}{2}-1}(1-q^{2\ell}),

as claimed.
∎

7.4.
Preparation

In this subsection, we rewrite
\mathrm{Pden}^{\prime}(I_{n},L)
as a linear combination of special values of certain polynomial
g_{\epsilon_{1}}(n,m,r,X)
as in Lemma
7.11
. We then express general
g_{\epsilon_{1}}(n,m,r,X)
as a simple combination of
g_{\epsilon_{3}}(n,r,r,X)
, see Lemma
7.13
.

Let
L=I_{n-t}^{\epsilon_{1}}\obot L_{2}
where
L_{2}
is of full type
t
. By Corollary
6.2
, we have

(7.9)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pden}^{\prime}(I_{n},L)=2\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\prod_{\ell=1}^{n-i-1}(1-q^{2\ell})\sum_{0\leq j\leq i}\sum_{\epsilon_{2}\in\{\pm 1\}}m\left(0_{j}\oplus U_{i-j}^{\epsilon_{2}},0_{t}\oplus U_{n-t}^{\epsilon_{1}}\right)\left|\mathrm{O}\left(0_{j}\obot U_{i-j}^{\epsilon_{1}},U_{n}^{-\epsilon}\right)\right|.

Here and in the following, we interpret
\sum_{\epsilon_{2}\in\{\pm 1\}}f(U_{0}^{\epsilon_{2}})
as
f(U_{0}^{1})
for a function
f
with
U_{i}^{\epsilon_{2}}
as input.

Let
s
and
n
be integers such that
0\leq s<n
, and let
\epsilon_{2}=\pm 1
. For odd
n
, we define

(7.10)

\displaystyle f_{\epsilon_{2}}(n,s,X)=\begin{cases}\prod_{\ell=\frac{n+1}{2}}^{\frac{n+s-2}{2}}(q^{2\ell}X^{2})\cdot q^{\frac{n+s}{2}}X(q^{\frac{n+s}{2}}X-\epsilon\epsilon_{2})\cdot\prod_{\ell=\frac{n+s+2}{2}}^{n-1}(q^{2\ell}X^{2}-1)&\text{ if $s$ is odd,}\\
\prod_{\ell=\frac{n+1}{2}}^{\frac{n-1}{2}+\frac{s}{2}}(q^{2\ell}X^{2})\cdot\prod_{\ell=\frac{n+1+s}{2}}^{n-1}(q^{2\ell}X^{2}-1)&\text{ if $s$ is even.}\end{cases}

Similarly, for an even integer
n>0
and
0\leq s<n
, we define

(7.11)

\displaystyle f_{\epsilon_{2}}(n,s,X)=\begin{cases}\prod_{\ell=\frac{n}{2}}^{\frac{n+s-3}{2}}(q^{2\ell}X^{2})q^{\frac{n}{2}+s-1}X\cdot\prod_{\ell=\frac{n+s+1}{2}}^{n-1}(q^{2\ell}X^{2}-1)&\text{ if $s$ is odd,}\\
\prod_{\ell=\frac{n}{2}}^{\frac{n+s-2}{2}}(q^{2\ell}X^{2})\cdot q^{\frac{s}{2}}(q^{\frac{n+s}{2}}X-\epsilon\epsilon_{2})\cdot\prod_{\ell=\frac{n+s+2}{2}}^{n-1}(q^{2\ell}X^{2}-1)&\text{ if $s$ is even.}\end{cases}

Here when
s=0
, we always take
\epsilon_{2}=1
. Notice that
f_{\epsilon_{2}}(n,s,X)
is a polynomial of degree
n-1
.

Lemma 7.7
.

(1)

Assume
0\leq i<n
. We have

\displaystyle\prod_{\ell=1}^{n-i-1}(1-q^{2\ell})|\mathrm{O}(0_{i-s}\obot U_{s}^{\epsilon_{2}},U_{n}^{-\epsilon})|=(-1)^{n-i-1}q^{\frac{i(i-1)}{2}}I_{\epsilon}(n)f_{\epsilon_{2}}(n,s,q^{-i}),

where

\displaystyle I_{\epsilon}(n)\coloneqq\begin{cases}\prod_{\ell=1}^{\frac{n-1}{2}}(q^{2\ell}-1)&\text{ if $n$ is odd},\\
(q^{\frac{n}{2}}+\epsilon)\prod_{\ell=1}^{\frac{n}{2}-1}(q^{2\ell}-1)&\text{ if $n$ is even}.\end{cases}

(2)

Assume
0\leq i\leq n
,
s<n
and that
n^{\prime}-n>0
is even. We have

\displaystyle\prod_{\ell=0}^{n-i-1}(1-q^{2\ell+n^{\prime}-n})|\mathrm{O}(0_{i-s}\obot U_{s}^{\epsilon_{2}},U_{n^{\prime}}^{-\epsilon})|=(-1)^{n-i}q^{\frac{i(i-1)}{2}}I_{\epsilon}(n^{\prime},n)f_{\epsilon_{2}}(n,s,q^{\frac{n^{\prime}-n}{2}-i}),

where

\displaystyle I_{\epsilon}(n^{\prime},n)\coloneqq\begin{cases}\prod_{\ell=\frac{n^{\prime}-n}{2}}^{\frac{n^{\prime}-1}{2}}(q^{2\ell}-1)&\text{ if $n$ is odd},\\
(q^{\frac{n^{\prime}}{2}}+\epsilon)\prod_{\ell=\frac{n^{\prime}-n}{2}}^{\frac{n^{\prime}}{2}-1}(q^{2\ell}-1)&\text{ if $n$ is even}.\end{cases}

Proof.

This follows from the formula of
|\mathrm{O}(0_{i-s}\obot U_{s}^{\epsilon_{2}},U_{n}^{-\epsilon})|
given in Lemma
5.16
and a straightforward computation.
∎

Lemma 7.8
.

For integers
0\leq i,t\leq n
, we have

(7.12)

\displaystyle\binom{t}{i}_{q}=\sum_{a=0}^{n-t}(-1)^{a}\cdot q^{a(t+1-i)}q^{\frac{a(a-1)}{2}}\cdot\binom{n-t}{a}_{q}\cdot\binom{n-a}{i-a}_{q}.

Proof.

The identity is automatically true for
i>t
as both sides are zero.
Recall the following analogue of Pascal’s identity for
q
-binomial coefficients:

(7.13)

\displaystyle\binom{t}{i}_{q}=\binom{t+1}{i}_{q}-q^{t-i+1}\binom{t}{i-1}_{q}.

By this identity, we obtain
2
terms, one with the
t
-index raised, another with the
i
-index lowered.
Applying again (
7.13
) to
\binom{t+1}{i}_{q}
and
\binom{t}{i-1}_{q}
respectively, we obtain

\displaystyle\binom{t}{i}_{q}=\binom{t+2}{i}_{q}-q^{t-i+2}\binom{t+1}{i-1}_{q}-q^{t-i+1}\Big(\binom{t+1}{i-1}_{q}-q^{t-i+2}\binom{t-1}{i-2}_{q}\Big).

We may continue this process and after
n-t
steps, we obtain
2^{n-t}
many terms. Each term corresponds to a lattice path starting from the origin, going to north and east as follows. If the
\ell
-th step raises the index of
t
(resp. reduces the index of
i
), we define the lattice path goes towards north for the
\ell
-th step (resp. east). We use
I=(i_{1},\cdots,i_{n-t})
where
i_{\ell}\in\{0,1\}
to denote the path whose
\ell
-th step goes towards north (east) if
i_{\ell}=0
(
i_{\ell}
=1) and let
|I|=i_{1}+\cdots+i_{n-t}
. We use
P_{I}
to denote the term corresponding to
I
. Now the lemma follows from the following claim.

Claim:

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I,|I|=a\end{subarray}}P_{I}=(-1)^{a}\cdot q^{a(t+1-i)}q^{\frac{a(a-1)}{2}}\cdot\binom{n-t}{a}_{q}\cdot\binom{n-a}{i-a}_{q}.

Indeed a direct calculation shows that

\displaystyle P_{(1^{a},0^{n-t-a})}=(-1)^{a}\cdot q^{a(t+1-i)}q^{\frac{a(a-1)}{2}}\cdot\binom{n-a}{i-a}_{q}.

Let
A_{I}
denote the area bounded by the lattice path
I
, the horizontal axis, and the vertical line given by
x=|I|
. Then a direct computation shows that

\displaystyle P_{I}=q^{A_{I}}\cdot P_{(1^{a},0^{n-t-a})}.

Now the claim follows from the well-known formula of
q
-binomial coefficient (see
[
Cam
, Theorem 6.9]
for example):

\displaystyle\sum_{I,|I|=a}q^{A_{I}}=\binom{n-t}{a}_{q}.

This proves the claim and the lemma.
∎

Lemma 7.9
.

For an integer
n\geq 0
and
\epsilon=\pm 1
, let

\displaystyle\alpha(n)=q^{\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor\lfloor\frac{n-1}{2}\rfloor}=\begin{cases}q^{(\frac{n-1}{2})^{2}}&\hbox{ if $n$ is odd},\\
q^{\frac{n}{2}(\frac{n}{2}-1)}&\hbox{ if $n$ is even},\end{cases}\quad\text{ and }\quad\beta_{\epsilon}(n)=\begin{cases}(-1)^{\frac{n-1}{2}}&\hbox{ if $n$ is odd},\\
\epsilon(-1)^{\frac{n}{2}}&\hbox{ if $n$ is even},\\
1&\text{ if $n=0$}.\end{cases}

Then

\sum_{j=0}^{n}(-1)^{j}q^{\frac{j(j-1)}{2}}m(0_{j},U_{n}^{\epsilon})=\alpha(n)\beta_{\epsilon}(n).

Proof.

If
n=0
, the statement both sides are
1
by definition. From now on we assume
n>0
. By
[
LZ22b
, Lemma 3.2.2.]
, we have

|m(0_{j},U_{n}^{\epsilon})|=\binom{d}{j}_{q}\cdot\prod_{l=1}^{j}(q^{d+e-l}+1),

with

d=\begin{cases}\frac{n-1}{2}&\mathrm{if}\,n\,\hbox{ is odd},\\
\frac{n}{2}-\frac{1-\epsilon}{2}&\mathrm{if}\,n\,\hbox{ is even},\end{cases}\quad\text{ and }\quad e=\begin{cases}1&\mathrm{if}\,n\hbox{ is odd},\\
1-\epsilon&\mathrm{if}\,n\hbox{ is even}.\end{cases}

So we have

\sum_{j=0}^{n}(-1)^{j}q^{\frac{j(j-1)}{2}}\cdot m(0_{j},U_{n}^{\epsilon})=\sum_{j=0}^{d}(-1)^{j}q^{\frac{j(j-1)}{2}}\binom{d}{j}_{q}\prod_{l=1}^{j}(q^{d+e-l}+1),

which by Lemma
5.26
equals to

(-q^{d+e-1})^{d}
. A direct calculation checks that
(-q^{d+e-1})^{d}=\alpha(n)\beta_{\epsilon}(n).

∎

Definition 7.10
.

For
0\leq r\leq m\leq n
, we define

\displaystyle g_{\epsilon_{1}}(n,m,r,X)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{k=0}^{r}(-1)^{k}q^{\frac{k(k-1)}{2}}\sum_{\epsilon_{2}\in\{\pm 1\}}m(U_{k}^{\epsilon_{2}},U_{m}^{\epsilon_{1}})\sum_{j=0}^{r-k}(-1)^{j}q^{\frac{j(j-1)}{2}}\cdot\binom{m-j-k}{r-j-k}_{q}\cdot m(0_{j},U_{m-k}^{\delta})f_{\epsilon_{2}}(n,k,X)

with
\delta=\delta(m,k,\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2})
as defined in (
5.12
).
In the following, when
n
is clear in the context, we simply write
g_{\epsilon_{1}}(m,r,X)
for
g_{\epsilon_{1}}(n,m,r,X)
.

In particular,
g_{\epsilon_{1}}(n,m,0,X)=f_{1}(n,0,X)
and by Lemma
7.9
,

(7.14)

\displaystyle g_{\epsilon_{1}}(n,r,r,X)=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{r}(-1)^{k}q^{\frac{k(k-1)}{2}}\cdot\sum_{\epsilon_{2}\in\{\pm 1\}}m(U_{k}^{\epsilon_{2}},U_{r}^{\epsilon_{1}})\cdot\alpha(r-k)\beta_{\delta}(r-k)\cdot f_{\epsilon_{2}}(n,k,X).

In the rest of this section, we let
m=n-t
without explicit mentioning.

Lemma 7.11
.

Assume
L
is a lattice of rank
n
and type
t
.

(1)

Let
\mathrm{m}(n,t)\coloneqq\mathrm{min}\{n-t,n-1\}
. Then for
0\leq i\leq n-1
, we have

(\mathrm{Pden}^{n-i})^{\prime}(I_{n},L)=2I_{\epsilon}(n)\cdot\sum_{r=0}^{\mathrm{m}(n,t)}\binom{n-r}{i-r}_{q}(-1)^{i-r+n-1}q^{\frac{(i-r)(i-r-1)}{2}}q^{r(n-m)}\cdot g_{\epsilon_{1}}(n,m,r,q^{-i}).

(2)

Assume that
n^{\prime}-n
is a positive even integer and
m<n
. Then for
0\leq i\leq n
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pden}^{n-i}(I_{n^{\prime}}^{-\epsilon},L,q^{n^{\prime}-n})=I_{\epsilon}(n^{\prime},n)\sum_{r=0}^{m}\binom{n-r}{i-r}_{q}(-1)^{i-r+n}q^{\frac{(i-r)(i-r-1)}{2}}q^{r(n-m)}\cdot g_{\epsilon_{1}}(n,m,r,q^{\frac{n^{\prime}-n}{2}-i}).

Proof.

We prove
(1)
first.
By (
7.9
), Lemma
7.7
and Lemma
5.19
, we have

\displaystyle(\mathrm{Pden}^{n-i})^{\prime}(I_{n},L)\cdot\Big(2(-1)^{n-i-1}I_{\epsilon}(n)\Big)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=0}^{t}\binom{t}{\ell}_{q}\sum_{k=0}^{i-\ell}\sum_{\epsilon_{2}\in{\pm 1}}q^{(t-\ell)(i-\ell)}q^{\frac{i(i-1)}{2}}m(0_{i-k-\ell}\obot U_{k}^{\epsilon_{2}},U_{n-t}^{\epsilon_{1}})f_{\epsilon_{2}}(n,k,q^{-i})

\displaystyle=\sum^{i}_{s=\mathrm{max}\{i-t,0\}}\binom{t}{i-s}_{q}\sum_{k=0}^{s}\sum_{\epsilon_{2}\in{\pm 1}}q^{(t-(i-s))s}q^{\frac{i(i-1)}{2}}m(0_{s-k}\obot U_{k}^{\epsilon_{2}},U_{n-t}^{\epsilon_{1}})f_{\epsilon_{2}}(n,k,q^{-i}),

where the last identity is obtained by setting
s=i-\ell
. Notice that if
s>n-t
, then
m(0_{s-k}\obot U_{k}^{\epsilon_{2}},U_{n-t}^{\epsilon_{1}})=0
. Hence we may assume
s\leq n-t
, or equivalently
t\leq n-s
in the above summation.
Now applying (
7.12
) to
i-s\leq t\leq n-s
and let
m=n-t
, we may rewrite the above summation as

\displaystyle\sum^{i}_{s=\mathrm{max}\{i-t,0\}}\sum_{a=0}^{m-s}(-1)^{a}\binom{n-s-a}{i-s-a}_{q}\cdot\binom{m-s}{a}_{q}\cdot

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\sum_{k=0}^{s}\sum_{\epsilon_{2}\in{\pm 1}}q^{a(n-m+1-(i-s))+\frac{a(a-1)}{2}+(n-m+s-i)s}q^{\frac{i(i-1)}{2}}m(0_{s-k}\obot U_{k}^{\epsilon_{2}},U_{m}^{\epsilon_{1}})f_{\epsilon_{2}}(n,k,q^{-i})

\displaystyle=\sum^{i}_{s=\mathrm{max}\{i-t,0\}}\sum_{a=0}^{m-s}\binom{n-s-a}{i-s-a}_{q}q^{\frac{(i-(s+a))(i-(s+a+1))}{2}}q^{(s+a)(n-m)}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\cdot(-1)^{a}\binom{m-s}{a}_{q}\sum_{k=0}^{s}\sum_{\epsilon_{2}\in{\pm 1}}q^{(s+a)(s-\frac{s+a+1}{2})+\frac{a(a+1)}{2}}m(0_{s-k}\obot U_{k}^{\epsilon_{2}},U_{m}^{\epsilon_{1}})f_{\epsilon_{2}}(n,k,q^{-i}).

Now let
r=s+a
. Notice that
r\leq m
and
\binom{n-r}{i-r}_{q}=0
for
r>i
. Rearranging the summation index, we obtain

\displaystyle(\mathrm{Pden}^{n-i})^{\prime}(I_{n},L)\cdot\Big((-1)^{n-i-1}I_{\epsilon}(n)\Big)^{-1}=\sum_{r=0}^{\mathrm{m}\{n,t\}}\binom{n-r}{i-r}_{q}q^{\frac{(i-r)(i-r-1)}{2}}q^{r(n-m)}\cdot g_{\epsilon_{1}}(n,m,r,q^{-i}),

where

\displaystyle g_{\epsilon_{1}}(n,m,r,X)

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=0}^{r}(-1)^{r-s}\cdot\binom{m-s}{r-s}_{q}\cdot\sum_{k=0}^{s}\sum_{\epsilon_{2}\in{\pm 1}}q^{\frac{s(s-1)}{2}}m(0_{s-k}\obot U_{k}^{\epsilon_{2}},U_{m}^{\epsilon_{1}})f_{\epsilon_{2}}(n,k,X)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{r}(-1)^{k}q^{\frac{k(k-1)}{2}}\sum_{\epsilon_{2}\in\{\pm 1\}}m(U_{k}^{\epsilon_{2}},U_{m}^{\epsilon_{1}})\sum_{j=0}^{r-k}(-1)^{j}q^{\frac{j(j-1)}{2}}\cdot\binom{m-j-k}{r-j-k}_{q}\cdot m(0_{j},U_{m-k}^{\delta})f_{\epsilon_{2}}(n,k,X).

Here, we use Lemma
5.18
to obtain the last identity.

Using
(2)
of Lemma
7.7
, the same proof of
(1)
proves
(2)
.
∎

We conclude this subsection by establishing a formula to express
g_{\epsilon_{1}}(m,r,X)
in terms of
g_{\epsilon_{3}}(r,r,X)
, which, as we will see, has a particular simple form (Lemma
7.15
). First, we need the following identity which might have independent interest.

Lemma 7.12
.

For any integers
0\leq r\leq n
, we have

(7.15)

\sum_{j=0}^{r}(-1)^{j}q^{j(j-1)/2}\binom{n-j}{r-j}_{q}m(0_{j},U_{n}^{\epsilon})=\sum_{\tau\in\{\pm 1\}}m(U_{r}^{\tau},U_{n}^{\epsilon})\alpha(r)\beta_{\tau}(r),

where
\alpha(r)
and
\beta_{\tau}(r)
are defined in Lemma
7.9
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
n
. The case
n=1
is obvious.
Now recall the identities

\displaystyle m(0_{j},U_{n}^{\epsilon})

\displaystyle=\binom{n}{j}_{q}-\sum_{i=1}^{j}\sum_{\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}}m(0_{j-i}\obot U_{i}^{\sigma},U_{n}^{\epsilon})

\displaystyle=\binom{n}{j}_{q}-\sum_{i=1}^{j}\sum_{\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}}q^{-(j-i)i}m(U_{i}^{\sigma},U_{n}^{\epsilon})m(0_{j-i},U_{n-i}^{\delta(n,i,\epsilon,\sigma)}),

by (
5.11
), and

(7.16)

\sum_{j=0}^{r}(-1)^{j}q^{j(j-1)/2}\binom{n-j}{r-j}_{q}\binom{n}{j}_{q}=\sum_{j=0}^{r}(-1)^{j}q^{j(j-1)/2}\binom{n}{r}_{q}\binom{r}{j}_{q}=0.

These imply that

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{r}(-1)^{j}q^{j(j-1)/2}\binom{n-j}{r-j}_{q}m(0_{j},U_{n}^{\epsilon})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sum_{j=0}^{r}(-1)^{j}q^{j(j-1)/2}\binom{n-j}{r-j}_{q}\sum_{i=1}^{j}\sum_{\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}}q^{-(j-i)i}m(U_{i}^{\sigma},U_{n}^{\epsilon})m(0_{j-i},U_{n-i}^{\delta(n,i,\epsilon,\sigma)})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{r}\sum_{\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}}(-1)^{i+1}q^{i(i-1)/2}m(U_{i}^{\sigma},U_{n}^{\epsilon})\sum_{j=0}^{r-i}(-1)^{j}q^{j(j-1)/2}\binom{n-i-j}{r-i-j}_{q}m(0_{j},U_{n-i}^{\delta(n,i,\epsilon,\sigma)}).

where in the last step we switch the order of summation and substitute
j
by
j+i
.

We can now use the induction hypothesis

\sum_{j=0}^{r-i}(-1)^{j}q^{j(j-1)/2}\binom{n-i-j}{r-i-j}_{q}m(0_{j},U_{n-i}^{\delta(n,i,\epsilon,\sigma)})=\sum_{\tau\in\{\pm 1\}}m(U_{r-i}^{\tau},U_{n-i}^{\delta(n,i,\epsilon,\sigma)})\alpha(r-i)\beta_{\tau}(r-i)

and

m(U_{i}^{\sigma},U_{n}^{\epsilon})m(U_{r-i}^{\tau},U_{n-i}^{\delta(n,i,\epsilon,\sigma)})=m(U_{r}^{\delta^{\prime}},U_{n}^{\epsilon})m(U_{i}^{\sigma},U_{r}^{\delta^{\prime}}),

where
\delta^{\prime}\in\{\pm 1\}
such that
\tau=\delta(r,i,\delta^{\prime},\sigma)
(see Lemma
5.22
) to obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{r}(-1)^{j}q^{j(j-1)/2}\binom{n-j}{r-j}_{q}m(0_{j},U_{n}^{\epsilon})

(7.17)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{r}\sum_{\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}}(-1)^{i+1}q^{i(i-1)/2}\sum_{\tau\in\{\pm 1\}}m(U_{r}^{\delta^{\prime}},U_{n}^{\epsilon})m(U_{i}^{\sigma},U_{r}^{\delta^{\prime}})\alpha(r-i)\beta_{\tau}(r-i)

Hence (
7.15
) is equivalent to

(7.18)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{r}\sum_{\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}}(-1)^{i}q^{i(i-1)/2}\sum_{\tau\in\{\pm 1\}}m(U_{r}^{\delta^{\prime}},U_{n}^{\epsilon})m(U_{i}^{\sigma},U_{r}^{\delta^{\prime}})\alpha(r-i)\beta_{\tau}(r-i)=0

Now applying Lemma
7.9
, the left hand side of (
7.18
) is equal to

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{r}\sum_{\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}}(-1)^{i}q^{i(i-1)/2}\sum_{\tau\in\{\pm 1\}}m(U_{r}^{\delta^{\prime}},U_{n}^{\epsilon})m(U_{i}^{\sigma},U_{r}^{\delta^{\prime}})\sum_{j=0}^{r-i}(-1)^{j}q^{j(j-1)/2}m(0_{j},U_{r-i}^{\tau})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\tau\in\{\pm 1\}}m(U_{r}^{\delta^{\prime}},U_{n}^{\epsilon})\sum_{i=0}^{r}\sum_{j=0}^{r-i}\sum_{\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}}(-1)^{i+j}q^{(i+j)(i+j-1)/2}m(0_{j}\obot U_{i}^{\sigma},U_{r}^{\delta^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\delta^{\prime}\in\{\pm 1\}}m(U_{r}^{\delta^{\prime}},U_{n}^{\epsilon})\sum_{k=0}^{r}(-1)^{k}q^{k(k-1)/2}\binom{r}{k}_{q}=0.

The lemma is proved.
∎

Lemma 7.13
.

For
0\leq r\leq m<n
, we have

\displaystyle g_{\epsilon_{1}}(m,r,X)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\epsilon_{3}\in\{\pm 1\}}m(U_{r}^{\epsilon_{3}},U_{m}^{\epsilon_{1}})g_{\epsilon_{3}}(r,r,X).

Proof.

When
r=0
, we have by definition

g(m,0,X)=f_{1}(n,0,X)=\sum_{\epsilon_{3}\in\{\pm 1\}}m(U_{0}^{\epsilon_{3}},U_{m}^{\epsilon_{1}})g_{\epsilon_{3}}(0,0,X).

Now we assume
r>0
,
\delta=\delta(m,k,\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2})
and
\delta^{\prime}=\delta(r,k,\epsilon_{3},\epsilon_{2})
.
On the one hand, by definition

\displaystyle g_{\epsilon_{1}}(m,r,X)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{r}(-1)^{k}q^{\frac{k(k-1)}{2}}\sum_{\epsilon_{2}\in\{\pm 1\}}m(U_{k}^{\epsilon_{2}},U_{m}^{\epsilon_{1}})\sum_{j=0}^{r-k}(-1)^{j}q^{\frac{j(j-1)}{2}}\cdot\binom{m-j-k}{r-j-k}_{q}\cdot m(0_{j},U_{m-k}^{\delta})f_{\epsilon_{2}}(n,k,X).

On the other hand, we have by (
7.14
),

\displaystyle\sum_{\epsilon_{3}\in\{\pm 1\}}m(U_{r}^{\epsilon_{3}},U_{m}^{\epsilon_{1}})g_{\epsilon_{3}}(r,r,X)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{r}(-1)^{k}q^{\frac{k(k-1)}{2}}\cdot\sum_{\epsilon_{2}\in\{\pm 1\}}\sum_{\epsilon_{3}\in\{\pm 1\}}m(U_{k}^{\epsilon_{2}},U_{r}^{\epsilon_{3}})m(U_{r}^{\epsilon_{3}},U_{m}^{\epsilon_{1}})\cdot\alpha(r-k)\beta_{\delta^{\prime}}(r-k)\cdot f_{\epsilon_{2}}(n,k,X).

By Lemma
5.22
,

\displaystyle m(U_{k}^{\epsilon_{2}},U_{r}^{\epsilon_{3}})m(U_{r}^{\epsilon_{3}},U_{m}^{\epsilon_{1}})=m(U_{k}^{\epsilon_{2}},U_{m}^{\epsilon_{1}})m(U_{r-k}^{\delta^{\prime}},U_{m-k}^{\delta}).

Hence, in order to prove the theorem, it suffices to show for any
k
and
\epsilon_{2}

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{r-k}(-1)^{j}q^{\frac{j(j-1)}{2}}\cdot\binom{m-j-k}{r-j-k}_{q}\cdot m(0_{j},U_{m-k}^{\delta})=\sum_{\delta^{\prime}\in\{\pm 1\}}m(U_{r-k}^{\delta^{\prime}},U_{m-k}^{\delta})\cdot\alpha(r-k)\beta_{\delta^{\prime}}(r-k),

which is exactly the content of Lemma
7.12
.
∎

7.5.
Some identities between polynomials

Although
g_{\epsilon_{1}}(r,r,X)
by definition is a complicated linear combination of
f_{\epsilon_{2}}(n,k,X)
. We show in this subsection that in fact
g_{\epsilon_{1}}(r,r,X)
has a very simple form (Lemma
7.15
). Similarly, although
\mathrm{Pden}^{\prime}(I_{n},L)
is a complicated linear combination of the special values of
g_{\epsilon_{1}}(n-t,r,X)
, certain linear combination of
g_{\epsilon_{1}}(n-t,r,X)
is of a simple form (Lemma
7.16
).
By a direct computation, we can check the following lemma.

Lemma 7.14
.

For
0\leq s\leq n-1
, let

(7.19)

h_{\epsilon_{1}}(n,s,X)\coloneqq\begin{cases}\prod_{l=\frac{n+s+1}{2}}^{n-1}(q^{2l}X^{2}-1)&\mathrm{if}\text{ $n-s$ is odd},\\
(q^{\frac{n+s}{2}}X-\epsilon\epsilon_{1})\prod_{l=\frac{n+s+2}{2}}^{n-1}(q^{2l}X^{2}-1)&\mathrm{if}\text{ $n-s$ is even}.\end{cases}

Then

(7.20)

\displaystyle h_{\epsilon_{1}}(n,j,qX)

\displaystyle=h_{\epsilon_{1}}(n+1,j+1,X),

(7.21)

\displaystyle q^{\lfloor\frac{n+j+2}{2}\rfloor}Xh_{\epsilon_{1}}(n+1,j+1,X)

\displaystyle=h_{\epsilon_{1}}(n+1,j,X)+(-1)^{n+j+1}\epsilon\epsilon_{1}h_{\epsilon_{1}}(n+1,j+1,X).

Lemma 7.15
.

For integers
0<r\leq n-1
and
\epsilon,\epsilon_{1}=\pm 1
, we have

g_{\epsilon_{1}}(r,r,X)=(-1)^{r(n-1)}\epsilon_{1}^{n}\epsilon^{r}\alpha(r)h_{\epsilon_{1}}(n,r,X).

In particular,
g_{\epsilon_{1}}(r,r,X)
is a polynomial of degree
n-r-1
.

Proof.

We prove the case when
n
case is odd and
r
is even, and leave the other three cases to the reader. The idea is the same (a little bit more complicated). In this case,
we need to show

(7.22)

\displaystyle\frac{g_{\epsilon_{1}}(r,r,X)}{h_{\epsilon_{1}}(n,r,X)}=\sum_{k=0}^{r}(-1)^{k}q^{\frac{k(k-1)}{2}}\cdot\sum_{\epsilon_{2}\in\{\pm 1\}}m(U_{k}^{\epsilon_{2}},U_{r}^{\epsilon_{1}})\cdot\alpha(r-k)\beta_{\delta}(r-k)\cdot\frac{f_{\epsilon_{2}}(n,k,X)}{h_{\epsilon_{1}}(n,r,X)}=\epsilon_{1}\alpha(r),

where
\delta=\delta(r,k,\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2})
.
Since
n
is odd and
r
is even, we have

\displaystyle\frac{f_{\epsilon_{2}}(n,k,X)}{h_{\epsilon_{1}}(n,r,X)}=\begin{cases}\prod_{\ell=\frac{n+1}{2}}^{\frac{n+k-2}{2}}(q^{2\ell}X^{2})\cdot q^{\frac{n+k}{2}}X(q^{\frac{n+k}{2}}X-\epsilon\epsilon_{2})\prod_{\ell=\frac{n+2+k}{2}}^{\frac{n+r-1}{2}}(q^{2\ell}X^{2}-1)&\text{ if $k$ is odd},\\
\prod_{\ell=\frac{n+1}{2}}^{\frac{n-1+k}{2}}(q^{2\ell}X^{2})\cdot\prod_{\ell=\frac{n+1+k}{2}}^{\frac{n+r-1}{2}}(q^{2\ell}X^{2}-1)&\text{ if $k$ is even}.\end{cases}

As a result, dividing (
7.22
) by
q^{(n+\frac{r-1}{2})(\frac{r-1}{2})+\frac{n+r}{2}}\cdot X^{r}
and setting
Y=(q^{\frac{n+1}{2}}X)^{-1},j=r-k
, (
7.22
) is equivalent to

(7.23)

\displaystyle\tilde{g}_{\epsilon_{1}}(r,Y)\coloneqq\sum_{j=0}^{r}(-1)^{r-j}q^{\frac{(r-j)(r-j-1)}{2}}\cdot\alpha(j)\cdot\sum_{\epsilon_{2}\in\{\pm 1\}}m(U_{r-j}^{\epsilon_{2}},U_{r}^{\epsilon_{1}})\beta_{\delta}(j)\cdot\tilde{f}_{\epsilon_{2}}(j,Y)=\epsilon_{1}Y^{r},

where
\tilde{f}_{\epsilon_{2}}(j,Y)
is a polynomial of degree
j
defined as follows:

\displaystyle\tilde{f}_{\epsilon_{2}}(j,Y)\coloneqq\begin{cases}\prod_{\ell=\frac{r-j}{2}}^{\frac{r-2}{2}}(1-q^{-2\ell}Y^{2})&\text{ if $j$ is even},\\
(1-\epsilon\epsilon_{2}q^{-\frac{r-j-1}{2}}Y)\cdot\prod_{\ell=\frac{r-j+1}{2}}^{\frac{r-2}{2}}(1-q^{-2\ell}Y^{2})&\text{ if $j$ is odd}.\end{cases}

Since
\{\tilde{f}_{1}(j,Y),0\leq j\leq r\}
forms a basis of the space of polynomials with degree
\leq r
, there exists unique tuples
(a_{j})
and
(b_{j})
such that

\displaystyle f(Y)=\epsilon_{1}Y^{r}=\sum_{j=0}^{r}a_{j}\tilde{f}_{1}(j,Y),\quad\text{and}\quad\tilde{g}_{\epsilon_{1}}(r,Y)=\sum_{j=0}^{r}b_{j}\tilde{f}_{1}(j,Y).

We need to show
(a_{j})=(b_{j})
. It is easy to check

a_{r}=b_{r}=(-1)^{\frac{r}{2}}\epsilon_{1}\alpha(r).

Now to prove
a_{j}=b_{j}
for all
j
, it suffices to prove that both
a_{j}
and
b_{j}
satisfy the recursion formula for
j<r

(7.24)

a_{j}=\begin{cases}-q^{j}\frac{1-q^{-j-2}}{1-q^{j-r}}a_{j+2}&\text{ if $j$ is even},\\
0&\text{ if $j$ is odd}.\end{cases}

We start with
a_{j}
. First of all, we have

(7.25)

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{r}q^{-r}a_{j}\tilde{f}_{1}(j,Y)=q^{-r}f(Y)=f(q^{-1}Y)=\sum_{j=0}^{r}a_{j}\tilde{f}_{1}(j,q^{-1}Y).

Notice that

\tilde{f}_{1}(j,q^{-1}Y)=(1-q^{-r}Y^{2})\tilde{f}_{1}(j-2,Y).

Since

\displaystyle\tilde{f}_{1}(j+1,Y)/\tilde{f}_{1}(j,Y)=\begin{cases}1-\epsilon q^{-\frac{r-j-2}{2}}Y&\text{ if $j$ is even},\\
1+\epsilon q^{-\frac{r-j-1}{2}}Y&\text{ if $j$ is odd},\end{cases}

we have

\displaystyle Y\tilde{f}_{1}(j,Y)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}-\epsilon q^{\frac{r-j-2}{2}}(\tilde{f}_{1}(j+1,Y)-\tilde{f}_{1}(j,Y))&\text{ if $j$ is even},\\
\epsilon q^{\frac{r-j-1}{2}}(\tilde{f}_{1}(j+1,Y)-\tilde{f}_{1}(j,Y))&\text{ if $j$ is odd},\end{cases}

\displaystyle Y^{2}\tilde{f}_{1}(j,Y)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}-q^{r-j-2}(\tilde{f}_{1}(j+2,Y)-\tilde{f}_{1}(j,Y))&\text{ if $j$ is even},\\
-q^{r-j-2}(\tilde{f}_{1}(j+2,Y)+(q-1)\tilde{f}_{1}(j+1,Y)-q\tilde{f}_{1}(j,Y))&\text{ if $j$ is odd}.\end{cases}

Therefore,

\displaystyle\tilde{f}_{1}(j,q^{-1}Y)=\begin{cases}q^{-j}\tilde{f}_{1}(j,Y)+(1-q^{-j})\tilde{f}_{1}(j-2,Y)&\text{ if $j$ is even},\\
q^{-j}\tilde{f}_{1}(j,Y)+(q-1)q^{-j}\tilde{f}_{1}(j-1,Y)+(1-q^{1-j})\tilde{f}_{1}(j-2,Y)&\text{ if $j$ is odd}.\end{cases}

Plugging this into (
7.25
), we obtain

\displaystyle(q^{-r}-q^{-j})a_{j}=\begin{cases}(q-1)q^{-j-1}a_{j+1}+(1-q^{-j-2})a_{j+2}&\text{ if $j$ is even},\\
(1-q^{-j-1})a_{j+2}&\text{ if $j$ is odd},\end{cases}

with
a_{r+1}=a_{r+2}=0
. So we have (
7.24
). We remark that in other cases, we have similar recursion formula as above but could not be simplified like (
7.24
).

Now we compute
b_{j}
for
j<r
.
Recall that
r
is even. First, if
j=0
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\epsilon_{2}\in\{\pm 1\}}m(U_{r-j}^{\epsilon_{2}},U_{r}^{\epsilon_{1}})\beta_{\delta}(j)\cdot\tilde{f}_{\epsilon_{2}}(j,Y)=\tilde{f}_{\epsilon_{1}}(0,Y).

It is easy to check that

(7.26)

\tilde{f}_{\epsilon_{2}}(j,Y)=\begin{cases}\tilde{f}_{1}(j,Y)&\text{ if $j$ is even},\\
(1-\epsilon\epsilon_{2}q^{-\frac{r-j-1}{2}})\tilde{f}_{1}(j-1,Y)&\text{ if $j$ is odd},\end{cases}

Now Lemmas
5.21
and
7.9
imply for
j\neq 0
and
\delta=\delta(r,j,\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2})
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\epsilon_{2}\in\{\pm 1\}}m(U_{r-j}^{\epsilon_{2}},U_{r}^{\epsilon_{1}})\cdot\beta_{\delta}(j)\cdot\tilde{f}_{\epsilon_{2}}(j,Y)=\begin{cases}\frac{2\epsilon_{1}(-1)^{\frac{j}{2}}(\epsilon_{1}q^{-\frac{j}{2}}+q^{-\frac{r-j}{2}})}{(1+\epsilon_{1}q^{-\frac{j}{2}})(1+q^{-\frac{r-j}{2}})}m(U_{r-j}^{1},U_{r}^{\epsilon_{1}})\tilde{f}_{1}(j,Y)&\text{ if $j$ is even},\\
2(-1)^{\frac{j-1}{2}}m(U_{r-j}^{1},U_{r}^{\epsilon_{1}})\tilde{f}_{1}(j-1,Y)&\text{ if $j$ is odd}.\end{cases}

Plugging this into the definition of
\tilde{g}_{\epsilon_{1}}(r,X)
as in (
7.23
), we obtain

\tilde{g}_{\epsilon_{1}}(r,X)=\sum_{j=0}^{r}b_{j}\tilde{f}_{1}(j,Y)

with
b_{j}=0
for odd
j
,
b_{0}=q^{\frac{r(r-1)}{2}}(1-2q^{-(r-1)}m(U_{1}^{1},U_{r}^{\epsilon_{1}})),
and

\displaystyle b_{j}=

\displaystyle 2(-1)^{r-\frac{j}{2}}q^{\frac{(r-j)(r-j-1)}{2}}\frac{(q^{-\frac{j}{2}}+\epsilon_{1}q^{-\frac{r-j}{2}})}{(1+\epsilon_{1}q^{-\frac{j}{2}})(1+q^{-\frac{r-j}{2}})}m(U_{r-j}^{1},U_{r}^{\epsilon_{1}})

\displaystyle-2(-1)^{r-\frac{j}{2}}q^{\frac{(r-j-1)(r-j-2)}{2}}\alpha(j+1)m(U_{r-j-1}^{1},U_{r}^{\epsilon_{1}})

for even
j\neq 0
.
Applying Lemma
5.21
, for even
j\neq 0
, we have

\displaystyle b_{j}

\displaystyle=2(-1)^{r-\frac{j}{2}}q^{\frac{(r-j)(r-j-1)}{2}}\frac{q^{-\frac{r-j}{2}}(\epsilon_{1}+q^{-\frac{r}{2}})}{(1+\epsilon_{1}q^{-\frac{j}{2}})(1+q^{-\frac{r-j}{2}})}\alpha(j)m(U_{r-j}^{1},U_{r}^{\epsilon_{1}}).

Now applying Lemma
5.21
twice, we can check that
b_{j}
satisfy (
7.24
).
∎

Recall that
\mathrm{m}(n,t)\coloneqq\mathrm{min}\{n-t,n-1\}.

Lemma 7.16
.

For
0\leq t\leq n
, and
m=n-t
, we have

(7.27)

\displaystyle\sum_{r=0}^{\mathrm{m}(n,t)}(-1)^{r}q^{\frac{(n-r)(n-r-1)}{2}}q^{rt}\cdot g_{\epsilon_{1}}(m,r,X)=F_{\epsilon_{1}}(n,m,X),

where

\displaystyle F_{\epsilon_{1}}(n,m,X)=\begin{cases}q^{\frac{n(n-1)}{2}}f_{\epsilon_{1}}(n,m,X)&\text{ if $t\not=0$},\\
(-1)^{n-1}\alpha(n)\sum_{\ell=0}^{n-1}(-q^{n}X)^{\ell}&\text{ if $t=0$}.\end{cases}

Proof.

We treat the case
t=0
first. In this case,
\epsilon_{1}=\epsilon
. Before we give the details of the proof, we summarize the main idea.
Since
\{h_{\epsilon}(n,s,X),0\leq s\leq n-1\}
forms a basis of the space of polynomials with degree
\leq n-1
, there exists unique tuples
(a_{n,j})
and
(b_{n,j})\in\mathbb{Q}^{n}
such that

(7.28)

\displaystyle F_{\epsilon}(n,n,X)=\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}a_{n,j}h_{\epsilon}(n,j,X),\quad\text{and}\quad\sum_{r=0}^{n-1}(-1)^{r}q^{\frac{(n-r)(n-r-1)}{2}}\cdot g_{\epsilon}(n,r,X)=\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}b_{n,j}h_{\epsilon}(n,j,X).

We need to show
a_{n,j}=b_{n,j}
for all
n
and
j
. We first show that
a_{n,j}
satisfy the recursion relations (
7.32
), which gives a description of
a_{n+1,j}
in terms of
a_{n,j}
and
a_{n,j-1}
. We can directly check
a_{1,0}=b_{1,0}=1
. Then by an induction on
n
, it suffices to show
b_{n,j}
also satisfies (
7.32
).

Now we derive (
7.32
).
It is easy to check that

(7.29)

F_{\epsilon}(n+1,n+1,X)=q^{\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor}(q^{n+1}X)F_{\epsilon}(n,n,qX)+(-1)^{n}\alpha(n+1).

Plugging (
7.28
) into the above formula and applying Lemma
7.14
, we obtain

(7.30)

\sum_{j=0}^{n}a_{n+1,j}h_{\epsilon}(n+1,j,X)=q^{\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor}(q^{n+1}X)\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}a_{n,j}h_{\epsilon}(n+1,j+1,X)+(-1)^{n}\alpha(n+1)h_{\epsilon}(n,n-1,X).

Let

(7.31)

\gamma(n,j)=\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+n+1-\lfloor\frac{n+j+2}{2}\rfloor=\begin{cases}n-\frac{j}{2}&\mathrm{if}\,j\hbox{ is even},\\
n-\lfloor\frac{j-(-1)^{n}}{2}\rfloor&\mathrm{if}\,j\hbox{ is odd}.\end{cases}

Then Lemma
7.14
and (
7.30
) imply

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{n}a_{n+1,j}h_{\epsilon}(n+1,j,X)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}a_{n,j}q^{\gamma(n,j)}h_{\epsilon}(n+1,j,X)+\sum_{j=1}^{n}(-1)^{n+j}a_{n,j-1}q^{\gamma(n,j-1)}h_{\epsilon}(n+1,j,X)

\displaystyle\quad+(-1)^{n}\alpha(n+1)h_{\epsilon}(n+1,n,X).

That is

(7.32)

\begin{cases}a_{n+1,0}=q^{n}a_{n,0},\\
a_{n+1,j}=q^{\gamma(n,j)}a_{n,j}+(-1)^{n+j}q^{\gamma(n,j-1)}a_{n,j-1},\quad 0<j<n,\\
a_{n+1,n}=q^{\gamma(n,n-1)}a_{n,n-1}+(-1)^{n}\alpha(n+1).\end{cases}

Now we compute
b_{n,j}
. A direct computation shows that
b_{n,0}=q^{\frac{n(n-1)}{2}}
. In the following, we compute
b_{j}
for
j\neq 0
.

For
r=0
, we have

g_{\epsilon}(n,0,X)=f_{1}(n,0,X)=\begin{cases}h_{\epsilon}(n,0,X)&\text{ if $n$ is odd},\\
h_{\epsilon}(n,0,X)+(1-\epsilon)h_{\epsilon}(n,1,X)&\text{ if $n$ is even}.\end{cases}

Now we assume
r\neq 0
.
Recall that by Lemma
7.13
, we have

\displaystyle g_{\epsilon}(n,r,X)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\epsilon_{3}\in\{\pm 1\}}m(U_{r}^{\epsilon_{3}},U_{n}^{\epsilon})g_{\epsilon_{3}}(r,r,X).

Notice that when
n-r
is odd,
h_{\epsilon_{1}}(n,r,X)
is independent of
\epsilon_{1}
. Then a direct calculation using Lemma
7.15
and the formula for
m(U_{r}^{\epsilon_{1}},U_{n}^{\epsilon})
gives

(7.33)

\displaystyle g_{\epsilon}(n,r,X)=\alpha(r)h_{\epsilon}(n,r,X)m(U_{r}^{\epsilon},U_{n}^{\epsilon})\begin{cases}\frac{2q^{-\frac{r}{2}}}{1+\epsilon q^{-\frac{r}{2}}}&\mathrm{if}\,n\equiv r-1\equiv 1\pmod{2},\\
-2\epsilon&\mathrm{if}\,n\equiv r-1\equiv 0\pmod{2}.\end{cases}

When
n-r
is even, we have

(7.34)

h_{\epsilon_{3}}(n,r,X)=(q^{\frac{n+r}{2}}X-\epsilon\epsilon_{3})h_{\epsilon}(n,r+1,X).

So a direct calculation gives

(7.35)

g_{\epsilon}(n,r,X)=\alpha(r)h_{\epsilon}(n,r+1,X)m(U_{r}^{\epsilon},U_{n}^{\epsilon})\left(q^{\frac{n+r}{2}}X-1+(-1)^{n}(q^{\frac{n+r}{2}}X+1)\frac{m(U_{r}^{-\epsilon},U_{n}^{\epsilon})}{m(U_{r}^{\epsilon},U_{n}^{\epsilon})}\right).

We have by Lemma
5.18

\frac{m(U_{r}^{-\epsilon},U_{n}^{\epsilon})}{m(U_{r}^{\epsilon},U_{n}^{\epsilon})}=\frac{1-q^{-\frac{n-r}{2}}}{1+q^{-\frac{n-r}{2}}}\begin{cases}1&\mathrm{if}\,n\equiv r\equiv 1\pmod{2},\\
\frac{1-\epsilon q^{-\frac{r}{2}}}{1+\epsilon q^{-\frac{r}{2}}}&\mathrm{if}\,n\equiv r\equiv 0\pmod{2}.\end{cases}

The equation (
7.34
) gives

Xh_{\epsilon}(n,r+1,X)=q^{-\frac{n+r}{2}}(h_{\epsilon}(n,r,X)+h_{\epsilon}(n,r+1,X)).

So when
n\equiv r\equiv 1\pmod{2}
, we have

g_{\epsilon}(n,r,X)=2\alpha(r)m(U_{r}^{\epsilon},U_{n}^{\epsilon})\left(\frac{q^{-\frac{n-r}{2}}}{1+q^{-\frac{n-r}{2}}}h_{\epsilon}(n,r,X)+\frac{q^{-\frac{n-r}{2}}-1}{1+q^{-\frac{n-r}{2}}}h_{\epsilon}(n,r+1,X)\right),

and when
n\equiv r\equiv 0\pmod{2}
and
r\neq 0
, we have

\displaystyle g_{\epsilon}(n,r,X)

\displaystyle=\frac{2\alpha(r)m(U_{r}^{\epsilon},U_{n}^{\epsilon})}{(1+q^{-\frac{n-r}{2}})(1+\epsilon q^{-\frac{r}{2}})}\left((1+\epsilon q^{-\frac{n}{2}})h_{\epsilon}(n,r,X)+(1-q^{-\frac{n-r}{2}})(1-\epsilon q^{-\frac{r}{2}})h_{\epsilon}(n,r+1,X)\right).

In summary, we have the numbers
b_{n,j}
for
j\neq 0
are given by the following.

If
n
and
j
are odd, then

\displaystyle b_{n,j}=2(-1)^{j}q^{\frac{(n-j)(n-j-1)}{2}}\alpha(j)\frac{m(U_{j}^{\epsilon},U_{n}^{\epsilon})}{1+q^{\frac{n-j}{2}}}.

If
n
is odd and
j
is even, then

\displaystyle b_{n,j}=2(-1)^{j}q^{\frac{(n-j)(n-j-1)}{2}}\left(\frac{\alpha(j)q^{-\frac{j}{2}}}{1+\epsilon q^{-\frac{j}{2}}}m(U_{j}^{\epsilon},U_{n}^{\epsilon})-\frac{q^{n-j}\alpha(j-1)(q^{-\frac{n-j+1}{2}}-1)}{1+q^{-\frac{n-j+1}{2}}}m(U_{j-1}^{\epsilon},U_{n}^{\epsilon})\right).

If
n
and
j
are even, then

\displaystyle b_{n,j}=\frac{2(-1)^{j}q^{\frac{(n-j)(n-j-1)}{2}}\alpha(j)(1+\epsilon q^{-\frac{n}{2}})}{(1+q^{-\frac{n-j}{2}})(1+\epsilon q^{-\frac{j}{2}})}m(U_{j}^{\epsilon},U_{n}^{\epsilon}).

If
n
is even, then for
j=1
we have

\displaystyle b_{n,1}

\displaystyle=q^{\frac{n(n-1)}{2}}(1-\epsilon+2\epsilon q^{-(n-1)})m(U_{1}^{1},U_{n}^{\epsilon}),

and for odd
j>1
,
b_{n,j}
is equal to

\displaystyle 2(-1)^{j+1}q^{\frac{(n-j)(n-j-1)}{2}}\left(\epsilon\alpha(j)m(U_{j}^{\epsilon},U_{n}^{\epsilon})+\frac{q^{n-j}\alpha(j-1)(1-q^{-\frac{n-j+1}{2}})(1-\epsilon q^{-\frac{j-1}{2}})}{(1+q^{-\frac{n-j+1}{2}})(1+\epsilon q^{-\frac{j-1}{2}})}m(U_{j-1}^{\epsilon},U_{n}^{\epsilon})\right).

Using the explicit formulas, direct calculation shows that
b_{n,j}
satisfies
(
7.32
).

From now, we assume
t\not=0
and let
m=n-t
. The proof is essentially the same as the proof of Lemma
7.15
and we only prove the case that
n
is odd and
m
is even in detail.
According to Lemma
7.13
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{r=0}^{\mathrm{m}(n,t)}(-1)^{r}q^{\frac{(n-r)(n-r-1)}{2}}q^{rt}\cdot g_{\epsilon_{1}}(m,r,X)

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=0}^{\mathrm{m}(n,t)}(-1)^{r}q^{\frac{(n-r)(n-r-1)}{2}}q^{rt}\cdot\sum_{\epsilon_{3}\in\{\pm 1\}}m(U_{r}^{\epsilon_{3}},U_{m}^{\epsilon_{1}})g_{\epsilon_{3}}(r,r,X)

\displaystyle=q^{\frac{(n-m)(n+m-1)}{2}}\sum_{r=0}^{\mathrm{m}(n,t)}(-1)^{r}q^{\frac{(m-r)(m-r-1)}{2}}\cdot\sum_{\epsilon_{3}\in\{\pm 1\}}m(U_{r}^{\epsilon_{3}},U_{m}^{\epsilon_{1}})g_{\epsilon_{3}}(r,r,X).

Assume that
n
is odd and
m
is even. Factoring out
h_{\epsilon_{1}}(n,m,X)
, replacing
X
by
q^{\frac{n-1}{2}}X
, and apply Lemma
7.15
, we have that (
7.27
) is equivalent to

(7.36)

\displaystyle q^{\frac{(n-m)(n+m-1)}{2}}\sum_{r=0}^{m}(-1)^{r}q^{\frac{(m-r)(m-r-1)}{2}}\cdot\sum_{\epsilon_{3}\in\{\pm 1\}}m(U_{r}^{\epsilon_{3}},U_{m}^{\epsilon_{1}})\alpha(r)g_{\epsilon_{3}}^{\prime}(m,r,X)=F^{\prime}(n,m,X),

where

\displaystyle g_{\epsilon_{3}}^{\prime}(m,r,X)=\begin{cases}\epsilon\epsilon_{3}(q^{\frac{r+1}{2}}X-\epsilon\epsilon_{3})\cdot\prod_{\ell=\frac{r+3}{2}}^{\frac{m}{2}}(q^{2\ell}X^{2}-1)&\text{ if $r$ is odd},\\
\epsilon_{3}\prod_{\ell=\frac{r+2}{2}}^{\frac{m}{2}}(q^{2\ell}X^{2}-1)&\text{ if $r$ is even},\end{cases}

and

\displaystyle F^{\prime}(n,m,X)=q^{\frac{n(n-1)}{2}}\prod_{\ell=1}^{\frac{m}{2}}(q^{2\ell}X^{2}).

Since
g_{\epsilon}^{\prime}(r,m,X)
forms a basis of the space of polynomials with degree
\leq m
, there exists unique tuples
(a_{j})
and
(b_{j})
such that

\displaystyle\text{LHS of \eqref{eq: eq t not 0}}=\sum_{j=0}^{m}a_{j}g_{\epsilon}^{\prime}(m,j,X),\quad\text{RHS of \eqref{eq: eq t not 0}}=\sum_{j=0}^{m}b_{j}g_{\epsilon}^{\prime}(m,j,X).

It suffices to show that
a_{0}=b_{0}
and both
(a_{j})
and
(b_{j})
satisfy the following recursive relation for
0<r\leq m
:

(7.37)

\displaystyle a_{r}=\begin{cases}0&\text{ if $r$ is odd,}\\
\frac{q^{m-r+2}-1}{q^{m}-q^{m-r}}a_{r-2}&\text{ if $r$ is even}.\end{cases}

We derive the recursive relation for
a_{j}
first.
Notice that

\displaystyle g_{\epsilon}^{\prime}(m,r,qX)=(q^{m+2}X^{2}-1)g_{\epsilon}^{\prime}(m,r+2,X).

Moreover,

\displaystyle Xg_{\epsilon}^{\prime}(m,r,X)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}q^{-\frac{r+1}{2}}(\epsilon g_{\epsilon}^{\prime}(m,r-1,X)-g_{\epsilon}^{\prime}(m,r,X))&\text{ if $r$ is odd,}\\
q^{-\frac{r}{2}}(\epsilon g_{\epsilon}^{\prime}(m,r-1,X)+g_{\epsilon}^{\prime}(m,r,X))&\text{ if $r$ is even.}\end{cases}

\displaystyle X^{2}g_{\epsilon}^{\prime}(m,r,X)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}q^{-(r+1)}(qg_{\epsilon}^{\prime}(m,r-2,X)+\epsilon(q-1)g_{\epsilon}^{\prime}(m,r-1,X)+g_{\epsilon}^{\prime}(m,r,X))&\text{ if $r$ is odd,}\\
q^{-r}(g_{\epsilon}^{\prime}(m,r-2,X)+g_{\epsilon}^{\prime}(m,r,X))&\text{ if $r$ is even.}\end{cases}

Hence if
m
is even, then

\displaystyle g_{\epsilon}^{\prime}(m,r,qX)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}q^{m-r}g_{\epsilon}^{\prime}(m,r,X)+\epsilon(q-1)q^{m-r-1}g_{\epsilon}^{\prime}(m,r+1,X)+(q^{m-r-1}-1)g_{\epsilon}^{\prime}(m,r+2,X)&\text{ if $r$ is odd,}\\
q^{m-r}g_{\epsilon}^{\prime}(m,r,X)+(q^{m-r}-1)g_{\epsilon}^{\prime}(m,r+2,X)&\text{ if $r$ is even.}\end{cases}

Hence for
1<r\leq m+1

(7.38)

\displaystyle(q^{m}-q^{m-r})a_{r}=\begin{cases}(q^{m-r+1}-1)a_{r-2}&\text{ if $r$ is odd,}\\
\epsilon(q-1)q^{m-r}a_{r-1}+(q^{m-r+2}-1)a_{r-2}&\text{ if $r$ is even},\end{cases}

with
a_{m+1}=0
, which implies (
7.37
).

Now we compute
b_{j}
.
First, when
r=0
,

\displaystyle\sum_{\epsilon_{3}\in\{\pm 1\}}m(U_{r}^{\epsilon_{3}},U_{m}^{\epsilon_{1}})g_{\epsilon_{3}}^{\prime}(m,0,X)=\epsilon g_{\epsilon}^{\prime}(m,0,X).

For
r\neq 0
,

\displaystyle\sum_{\epsilon_{3}\in\{\pm 1\}}m(U_{r}^{\epsilon_{3}},U_{m}^{\epsilon_{1}})g_{\epsilon_{3}}^{\prime}(m,r,X)=\begin{cases}-2\epsilon m(U_{r}^{\epsilon},U_{m}^{\epsilon_{1}})g_{\epsilon}^{\prime}(m,r+1,X)&\text{ if $r$ is odd,}\\
\epsilon(m(U_{r}^{1},U_{m}^{\epsilon_{1}})-m(U_{r}^{-1},U_{m}^{\epsilon_{1}}))g_{\epsilon}^{\prime}(m,r,X)&\text{ if $r$ is even}.\end{cases}

Then

\displaystyle b_{r}=\begin{cases}0&\text{ if $r$ is odd,}\\
a_{r-1}^{\prime}+a_{r}^{\prime}&\text{ if $r$ is even.}\end{cases}

where

\displaystyle a_{r}^{\prime}=q^{\frac{(n-m)(n+m-1)}{2}}\begin{cases}-2(-1)^{r}q^{\frac{(m-r)(m-r-1)}{2}}\alpha(r)\epsilon m(U_{r-1}^{\epsilon},U_{m}^{\epsilon_{1}})&\text{ if $r$ is odd,}\\
(-1)^{r}\alpha(r)q^{\frac{(m-r)(m-r-1)}{2}}\epsilon(m(U_{r}^{1},U_{m}^{\epsilon_{1}})-m(U_{r}^{-1},U_{m}^{\epsilon_{1}}))&\text{ if $r$ is even.}\end{cases}

Finally, a direct calculation shows that
a_{0}=b_{0}=\epsilon q^{\frac{n(n-1)}{2}}
and
b_{r}
satisfies (
7.38
).
∎

7.6.
Proof of Theorem
7.1

Now we are ready to prove Theorem
7.1
.

Recall that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pden}^{\prime}(L)=\frac{\mathrm{Pden}^{\prime}(I_{n},L)}{\mathrm{Pden}(I_{n},I_{n})},

and

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pden}(I_{n},I_{n})=2\alpha(n)I_{\epsilon}(n),

where
I_{\epsilon}(n)
is defined in Lemma
7.7
.

We first assume that
L=H^{j}\obot L_{1}
with
j>0
where
L_{1}=I_{n_{1}-t}\obot L_{2}
is an integral lattice of rank
n_{1}
and type
t>0
(the other non-integral cases were taken care of in the summary of the proof at the beginning of this section). By Lemma
5.13
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pden}^{\prime}(I_{n},L)=2\Big(\prod_{\ell=1}^{j-1}(1-q^{2\ell})\Big)\mathrm{Pden}(I_{n},L_{1},q^{2j}).

It suffices to show
\mathrm{Pden}(I_{n},L_{1},q^{2j})=0
. By Theorem
6.1
and Lemma
7.11
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pden}(I_{n},L,q^{2j})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{n_{1}}\mathrm{Pden}^{n_{1}-i}(I_{n}^{-\epsilon},L,q^{2j})

(7.39)

\displaystyle=I_{\epsilon}(n,n_{1})\sum_{r=0}^{n_{1}-t}\sum_{i=0}^{n_{1}}\binom{n_{1}-r}{i-r}_{q}(-1)^{n_{1}+i-r}q^{\frac{(i-r)(i-r-1)}{2}}q^{rt}\cdot g_{\epsilon_{1}}(n_{1},n_{1}-t,r,q^{j-i}).

Notice that the assumption
t>0
implies that
r\leq n_{1}-t\leq n_{1}-1
. Hence
g_{\epsilon_{1}}(n_{1},n-t,r,X)
is a polynomial of degree
n_{1}-r-1
by Lemma
7.15
. Then we may apply Corollary
5.25
to conclude

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{n_{1}}\binom{n_{1}-r}{i-r}_{q}(-1)^{n_{1}+i-r}q^{\frac{(i-r)(i-r-1)}{2}}q^{rt}\cdot g_{\epsilon_{1}}(n_{1},n_{1}-t,r,q^{\frac{n-n_{1}}{2}-i})=0.

Hence

\mathrm{Pden}^{\prime}(L)=\frac{2\Big(\prod_{\ell=1}^{j-1}(1-q^{2\ell})\Big)}{\mathrm{Pden}(I_{n},I_{n})}\mathrm{Pden}(I_{n},L,q^{n-n_{1}})=0.

Next, we assume
L=I_{n-t}\obot L_{2}
is integral of rank
n
and type
t
(Cases (2) and (3) or equivalently Case (b) in the summary of the proof at the beginning of this section). Similarly, by Theorem
6.1
and Lemma
7.11
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pden}^{\prime}(I_{n},L)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}(\mathrm{Pden}^{n-i})^{\prime}(I_{n},L)

(7.40)

\displaystyle=2I_{\epsilon}(n)\sum_{r=0}^{\mathrm{m}(n,t)}\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\binom{n-r}{i-r}_{q}(-1)^{n-1+i+r}q^{\frac{(i-r)(i-r-1)}{2}}q^{rt}\cdot g_{\epsilon_{1}}(n,n-t,r,q^{-i}).

Here, recall that
\mathrm{m}(n,t)\coloneqq\mathrm{min}\{n-t,n-1\}
. Applying Corollary
5.25
as before, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{n}\binom{n-r}{i-r}_{q}(-1)^{r}(-1)^{n-1+i}q^{\frac{(i-r)(i-r-1)}{2}}q^{rt}\cdot g_{\epsilon_{1}}(n,n-t,r,q^{-i})=0.

Hence,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pden}^{\prime}(I_{n},L)=2I_{\epsilon}(n)\sum_{r=0}^{\mathrm{m}(n,t)}(-1)^{r}q^{\frac{(n-r)(n-r-1)}{2}}q^{rt}\cdot g_{\epsilon_{1}}(n,n-t,r,q^{-n}).

By Lemma
7.16
, if
t\not=0
, then

\displaystyle\sum_{r=0}^{\mathrm{m}(n,t)}(-1)^{r}q^{\frac{(n-r)(n-r-1)}{2}}q^{rt}\cdot g_{\epsilon_{1}}(n,n-t,r,q^{-n})

\displaystyle=F_{\epsilon_{1}}(n,n-t,q^{-n})

\displaystyle=\alpha(n)\cdot\begin{cases}\prod_{\ell=1}^{\frac{t-1}{2}}(1-q^{2\ell})&\text{ if $t$ is odd},\\
(1-\epsilon\epsilon_{1}q^{\frac{t}{2}})\prod_{\ell=1}^{\frac{t}{2}-1}(1-q^{2\ell})&\text{ if $t$ is even}.\end{cases}

Notice that if
t
is even, then
\chi(I_{n-t}^{\epsilon_{1}})\chi(L_{2})=\epsilon
. Hence
\epsilon\epsilon_{1}=\chi(L_{2})
.

If
t=0
, then by Lemma
7.16
,

\displaystyle\sum_{r=0}^{\mathrm{m}(n,t)}(-1)^{r}q^{\frac{(n-r)(n-r-1)}{2}}q^{rt}\cdot g_{\epsilon_{1}}(n,n,r,q^{-n})=F_{\epsilon_{1}}(n,n,q^{-n})=\alpha(n)\cdot\begin{cases}1&\text{ if $n$ is odd},\\
0&\text{ if $n$ is even}.\end{cases}

This proves the theorem.

8.
Fourier transform: the analytic side

In this section, we study the partial Fourier transform of the vertical part of the analytic side following Section 8 of
[
LZ22b
]
. The main result is Theorem
8.2
.

Definition 8.1
.

For a non-degenerate lattice
L^{\flat}\subset\mathbb{V}
of rank
n-1
, and
x\in\mathbb{V}\setminus L_{F}^{\flat}
, we define

\displaystyle\partial\mathrm{Den}_{L^{\flat},\mathscr{V}}(x)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}L^{\flat}\subset L^{\prime}\subset L^{\prime\sharp}\\
L^{\prime\flat}\not\in\mathrm{Hor}(L^{\flat})\end{subarray}}\partial\mathrm{Pden}(L^{\prime})1_{L^{\prime}}(x),

where
L^{\prime\flat}=L^{\prime}\cap L_{F}^{\flat}.

Theorem 8.2
.

Let
L^{\flat}\subset\mathbb{V}
be a non-degenerate lattice of rank
n-1
, and let
\mathbb{W}=(L^{\flat}_{F})^{\bot}
be the perpendicular space of
L^{\flat}_{F}
in
\mathbb{V}
. Recall the partial Fourier transform

\displaystyle\partial\mathrm{Den}^{\perp}_{L^{\flat},\mathscr{V}}(x)\coloneqq\int_{L^{\flat}_{F}}\partial\mathrm{Den}_{L^{\flat},\mathscr{V}}(x+y)\,dy,\quad x\in\mathbb{W}\setminus\{0\}.

Then
\partial\mathrm{Den}^{\perp}_{L^{\flat},\mathscr{V}}(x)
is constant on
\mathbb{W}^{\geq 0}\setminus\{0\}
and is zero for
x\in\mathbb{W}^{<0}
.

Proof.

It suffices to show that if
{\mathrm{val}}(x)>0
, then

\displaystyle\partial\mathrm{Den}^{\perp}_{L^{\flat},\mathscr{V}}(x)-\partial\mathrm{Den}^{\perp}_{L^{\flat},\mathscr{V}}(\pi^{-1}x)=0.

By definition, we have

\displaystyle\partial\mathrm{Den}^{\perp}_{L^{\flat},\mathscr{V}}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{L^{\flat}_{F}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}L^{\flat}\subset L^{\prime}\subset L^{\prime\sharp}\\
L^{\prime\flat}\not\in\mathrm{Hor}(L^{\flat})\end{subarray}}\partial\mathrm{Pden}(L^{\prime})1_{L^{\prime}}(x+y)\,dy,

where
L^{\prime}
runs over lattices of rank
n
in
L_{F}^{\flat}+\langle x\rangle
.

Recall that
\mathrm{Pr}_{L_{F}^{\flat}}
denotes the projection to
L_{F}^{\flat}
. We rewrite the summation based on
L^{\prime}\cap L_{F}^{\flat}
and
\mathrm{Pr}_{L_{F}^{\flat}}(L^{\prime})
. For lattices
L^{\prime\flat}\subset\widetilde{L}^{\prime\flat}
in
L_{F}^{\flat}
of rank
n-1
, let

\displaystyle\mathrm{Lat}(L^{\prime\flat},\widetilde{L}^{\prime\flat})\coloneqq\{L^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{V}\mid L^{\prime}\cap L^{\flat}_{F}=L^{\prime\flat},\quad\mathrm{Pr}_{L_{F}^{\flat}}(L^{\prime})=\widetilde{L}^{\prime\flat}\}.

Then by Lemmas
7.2.1
and
7.2.2
of
[
LZ22a
]
, we have

\displaystyle\partial\mathrm{Den}^{\perp}_{L^{\flat},\mathscr{V}}(x)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}L^{\flat}\subset L^{\prime\flat}\\
L^{\prime\flat}\not\in\mathrm{Hor}(L^{\flat})\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}L^{\prime\flat}\subset\widetilde{L}^{\prime\flat}\\
\text{$\widetilde{L}^{\prime\flat}/L^{\prime\flat}$ cyclic}\end{subarray}}\sum_{L^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Lat}(L^{\prime\flat},\widetilde{L}^{\prime\flat})}\partial\mathrm{Pden}(L^{\prime})\int_{L^{\flat}_{F}}1_{L^{\prime}}(x+y)\,dy.

Here we can switch the order of the sum and integral because there are only finitely many nonzero terms in the sum for a fixed
x
. Since
\tilde{L}^{\prime\flat}/L^{\prime\flat}
is cyclic, it has a generator
u^{\flat}\in L_{F}^{\flat}
. Moreover, for
L^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Lat}(L^{\prime\flat},\widetilde{L}^{\prime\flat})
, we can write
L^{\prime}=L^{\prime\flat}+\langle u\rangle
with
u=u^{\flat}+u^{\perp}\in\mathbb{V}
where
0\neq u^{\perp}\in\mathbb{W}
.
Moreover,
write
x=\alpha u^{\perp}
with
\alpha\in F^{\times}
, then

x+y=\alpha u+(y-\alpha u^{\flat})\in L^{\prime}

if and only if
\alpha\in O_{F}
and
y-\alpha u^{\flat}\in L^{\prime\flat}
. As a result, we have

\displaystyle\int_{L^{\flat}_{F}}1_{L^{\prime}}(x+y)-1_{L^{\prime}}(\pi^{-1}x+y)dy=\begin{cases}\mathrm{vol}(L^{\prime\flat}),&\text{ if $\langle x\rangle=\langle u^{\perp}\rangle$},\\
0,&\text{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

Therefore, we have

(8.1)

\displaystyle\partial\mathrm{Den}^{\perp}_{L^{\flat},\mathscr{V}}(x)-\partial\mathrm{Den}^{\perp}_{L^{\flat},\mathscr{V}}(\pi^{-1}x)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}L^{\flat}\subset L^{\prime\flat}\\
L^{\prime\flat}\not\in\mathrm{Hor}(L^{\flat})\end{subarray}}\mathrm{vol}(L^{\prime\flat})D(L^{\prime\flat})(x),

where

(8.2)

\displaystyle D(L^{\prime\flat})(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}L^{\prime\flat}\subset\widetilde{L}^{\prime\flat}\\
\text{$\widetilde{L}^{\prime\flat}/L^{\prime\flat}$ cyclic}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u^{\perp}\in\langle x\rangle\text{ generator}\\
L^{\prime}=L^{\prime\flat}+\langle u^{\flat}+u^{\perp}\rangle\end{subarray}}\partial\mathrm{Pden}(L^{\prime})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u^{\flat}\in(L^{\prime\flat})^{\sharp}/L^{\prime\flat}\\
{\mathrm{val}}(u^{\flat})\geq 0\end{subarray}}\partial\mathrm{Pden}(L^{\prime\flat}+\langle u^{\flat}+x\rangle).

Here the last step uses the fact that
L^{\prime}=L^{\prime\flat}+\langle u^{\flat}+x\rangle
is integral if and only if
u^{\flat}\in(L^{\prime\flat})^{\sharp}/L^{\prime\flat}
and
{\mathrm{val}}(u^{\flat})\geq 0
(since
{\mathrm{val}}(x)>0
).

It suffices to show
D(L^{\prime\flat})(x)=0
for any
L^{\prime\flat}
such that
L^{\flat}\subset L^{\prime\flat}
and
L^{\prime\flat}\not\in\mathrm{Hor}(L^{\flat})
. To show this, we write
L^{\prime\flat}=I_{n_{1}}^{\epsilon_{1}}\obot L_{2}
where
L_{2}
is of full type (of rank
n-1-n_{1}
). Let
u_{2}
be the projection of
u^{\flat}
to
L_{2}
.
Then

(L^{\prime\flat})^{\sharp}/L^{\prime\flat}=(I_{n_{1}}^{\epsilon_{1}}\obot(L_{2})^{\sharp})/L^{\prime\flat}\cong L_{2}^{\sharp}/L_{2}\quad\text{ and }\quad L^{\prime\flat}+\langle u^{\flat}+x\rangle=L^{\prime\flat}+\langle u_{2}+x\rangle.

We consider a partition of

(L_{2}^{\sharp})^{\circ}/L_{2}=S^{+}(L_{2})\sqcup S^{0}(L_{2})\sqcup S^{-}(L_{2})

with

\displaystyle S^{+}(L_{2})=(\pi L_{2}^{\sharp})^{\circ\circ}/L_{2},\quad S^{0}(L_{2})=((\pi L_{2}^{\sharp})^{\circ}-(\pi L_{2}^{\sharp})^{\circ\circ})/L_{2},\quad S^{-}(L_{2})=((L_{2}^{\sharp})^{\circ}-(\pi L_{2}^{\sharp})^{\circ})/L_{2}.

Here, for a lattice
L
,

\displaystyle L^{\circ}\coloneqq\{x\in L\mid{\mathrm{val}}(x)\geq 0\}\text{ and }L^{\circ\circ}\coloneqq\{x\in L\mid{\mathrm{val}}(x)>0\}.

In general, for a full type lattice
L_{2}
, we also define

(8.3)

\displaystyle\mu^{+}(L):=|(\pi L^{\sharp})^{\circ\circ}/L|,\quad\mu^{0}(L):=|((\pi L^{\sharp})^{\circ}-(\pi L^{\sharp})^{\circ\circ})/L|,\quad\mu^{-}(L)\coloneqq|((L^{\sharp})^{\circ}-(\pi L^{\sharp})^{\circ})/L|.

For
\nu\in\{\pm 1\}
, let

(8.4)

\displaystyle\mu^{0,\nu}(L):=|\{u\in(\pi L^{\sharp})^{\circ}-(\pi L^{\sharp})^{\circ\circ}:\chi((u,u))=\nu\}/L|.

Since
L^{\prime}=L^{\prime\flat}+\langle u_{2}+x\rangle
with
u_{2}
integral and
{\mathrm{val}}(x)>0
, it is not hard to check that

\displaystyle t(L^{\prime})=\begin{cases}t(L^{\prime\flat})+1&\mathrm{if}\,u_{2}\in S^{+}(L_{2}),\\
t(L^{\prime\flat})&\mathrm{if}\,u_{2}\in S^{0}(L_{2}),\\
t(L^{\prime\flat})-1&\mathrm{if}\,u_{2}\in S^{-}(L_{2}).\end{cases}

Set
t=t(L^{\prime\flat})
. There are two cases.

When
t
is odd, we can write

L^{\prime}=L^{\prime\flat}+\langle u_{2}+x\rangle=\begin{cases}I_{n_{1}}^{\epsilon_{1}}\obot L_{2}^{\prime}&\mathrm{if}\,u\in S_{2}^{+}(L_{2}),\\
I_{n_{1}}^{\epsilon_{1}}\obot I_{2}^{1}\obot L_{2}^{\prime}&\mathrm{if}\,u\in S_{2}^{-}(L_{2}).\end{cases}

In both cases, a simple calculation gives

\chi(L_{2}^{\prime})=\epsilon\epsilon_{1}.

For
t>1
, by Theorem
7.1
,

\displaystyle\partial\mathrm{Pden}(L^{\prime})=(1-\epsilon\epsilon_{1}q^{\frac{t-1}{2}})\prod_{\ell=1}^{\frac{t-1}{2}-1}(1-q^{2\ell})\cdot\begin{cases}(1-\epsilon\epsilon_{1}q^{\frac{t+1}{2}})(1+\epsilon\epsilon_{1}q^{\frac{t-1}{2}})&\text{ if $u_{2}\in S^{+}(L_{2})$},\\
1+\epsilon\epsilon_{1}q^{\frac{t-1}{2}}&\text{ if $u_{2}\in S^{0}(L_{2})$},\\
1&\text{ if $u_{2}\in S^{-}(L_{2})$}.\end{cases}

For
t=1
,
S^{-}(L_{2})
is empty and
L^{\prime\flat}\not\in\mathrm{Hor}(L^{\flat})
implies that
L^{\prime}=L_{n_{1}}^{\epsilon_{1}}\obot L_{2}^{\prime}
with
\chi(L_{2}^{\prime})=1
, i.e.
\epsilon_{1}=\epsilon
. In this case, by Theorem
7.1
,

\partial\mathrm{Pden}(L^{\prime})=\begin{cases}1-q&\mathrm{if}\,u\in S^{+}(L_{2}),\\
1&\mathrm{if}\,u\in S^{0}(L_{2}).\end{cases}

Hence by (
8.2
), we have
D(L^{\prime\flat})=0
if

(8.5)

(1-\epsilon\epsilon_{1}q^{\frac{t+1}{2}})(1+\epsilon\epsilon_{1}q^{\frac{t-1}{2}})\mu^{+}(L_{2})+(1+\epsilon\epsilon_{1}q^{\frac{t-1}{2}})\mu^{0}(L_{2})+\mu^{-}(L_{2})=0.

When
t=t(L^{\prime\flat})
is even,
L^{\prime\flat}\not\in\mathrm{Hor}(L^{\flat})
implies that
t>0
. Moreover, if
u_{2}\in S^{0}(L_{2})
, we have a decomposition (since
u_{2}\in L_{2}
is perpendicular to
I_{n_{1}}^{\epsilon_{1}}
)

L^{\prime}=L^{\prime\flat}+\langle u_{2}+x\rangle=I_{n_{1}}^{\epsilon_{1}}\obot\langle u_{2}+x\rangle\obot L_{2}^{\prime}

for some full type lattice
L_{2}^{\prime}
of rank
t
. Then a direct calculation gives

\chi(L_{2}^{\prime})=(-1)^{n_{1}}\epsilon_{1}\epsilon\chi(u_{2}).

So we have by Theorem
7.1
,

\displaystyle\partial\mathrm{Pden}(L^{\prime})=\prod_{\ell=1}^{\frac{t}{2}-1}(1-q^{2\ell})\cdot\begin{cases}(1-q^{t})&\text{ if $u_{2}\in S^{+}(L_{2})$},\\
1-(-1)^{n_{1}}\epsilon_{1}\epsilon\chi(u_{2})q^{\frac{t}{2}}&\text{ if $u_{2}\in S^{0}(L_{2})$},\\
1&\text{ if $u_{2}\in S^{-}(L_{2})$}.\end{cases}

Hence by (
8.2
), we have
D(L^{\prime\flat})=0
if

(8.6)

(1-q^{t})\mu^{+}(L_{2})+(1-(-1)^{n_{1}}\epsilon_{1}\epsilon q^{\frac{t}{2}})\mu^{0,1}(L_{2})+(1+(-1)^{n_{1}}\epsilon_{1}\epsilon q^{\frac{t}{2}})\mu^{0,-1}(L_{2})+\mu^{-}(L_{2})=0.

Now (
8.5
) follows from Proposition
8.7
and (
8.6
) follows from Proposition
8.9
. Hence we have
D(L^{\prime\flat})=0
for
L^{\prime\flat}
such that
L^{\flat}\subset L^{\prime\flat}
and
L^{\prime\flat}\not\in\mathrm{Hor}(L^{\flat})
. Now the theorem follows from (
8.1
).
∎

To complete the proof of Theorem
8.2
, we are left to state and prove Propositions
8.7
and
8.9
.

Definition 8.3
.

Let
L
and
L^{\prime}
be lattices of full type such that
L\subset L^{\prime}\subset\pi^{-1}L
. For
?\in\{+,0,-,\{0,+1\},\{0,-1\}\}
, define

\displaystyle\mu^{?}(L,L^{\prime}):=\mu^{?}(L)-[L^{\prime}:L]\mu^{?}(L^{\prime}),

where
\mu^{?}(L)
is defined in (
8.3
) and (
8.4
).

Lemma 8.4
.

Let
L
be a full type lattice of rank
t
. Then

\mu^{+}(L)+\mu^{0}(L)+\mu^{-}(L)=q^{t}\cdot\mu^{+}(L).

Let
L
and
L^{\prime}
be full type lattices of rank
t
such that
L\subset L^{\prime}\subset\pi^{-1}L
. Then

\displaystyle\mu^{+}(L,L^{\prime})+\mu^{0}(L,L^{\prime})+\mu^{-}(L,L^{\prime})=q^{t}\cdot\mu^{+}(L,L^{\prime}).

Proof.

It suffices to show that the following map

\displaystyle(L^{\sharp})^{\circ}/L{\rightarrow}(\pi L^{\sharp})^{\circ\circ}/L,\quad x\mapsto\pi x

is surjective and every fiber of this map has size
q^{t}
. For
x\in(\pi L^{\sharp})^{\circ\circ}=\pi(L^{\sharp})^{\circ}
, the fiber at
x
is

\displaystyle\{\pi^{-1}(y+x)\in(L^{\sharp})^{\circ}:y\in\overline{L}\}.

Since
x\in\pi(L^{\sharp})^{\circ}
,

\displaystyle\pi^{-1}(y+x)\in(L^{\sharp})^{\circ}\iff(y+x)\in\pi(L^{\sharp})^{\circ}\iff y\in\pi(L^{\sharp})^{\circ}.

Moreover, the assumption that
L
is a full type lattice implies that
L\subset\pi(L^{\sharp})^{\circ}
. Hence

\displaystyle|\{\pi^{-1}(y+x)\in(L^{\sharp})^{\circ}:y\in\overline{L}\}|=|\overline{L}|=q^{t}.

This proves the first statement. The second statement follows from the first and the definition of
\mu^{?}(L,L^{\prime})
.
∎

Definition 8.5
.

Let
L
be a full type lattice of rank
t
. We call
L
maximal of type
t
if
t(L^{\prime})<t
for any
L^{\prime}
such that
L\subsetneq L^{\prime}\subset L_{F}
.

Lemma 8.6
.

If
L
is non-maximal full type lattice of rank
t
, then there exists a
L^{\prime}
such that
L\subset L^{\prime}\subset\pi^{-1}L
and

\displaystyle\mu^{+}(L,L^{\prime})+\mu^{0}(L,L^{\prime})=q\cdot\mu^{+}(L,L^{\prime}).

Proof.

We need to find a
L^{\prime}
such that
L\subset L^{\prime}\subset\pi^{-1}L
and

\displaystyle|((\pi L^{\sharp})^{\circ}-(\pi L^{\prime\sharp})^{\circ})/L|=q\cdot|((\pi L^{\sharp})^{\circ\circ}-(\pi L^{\prime\sharp})^{\circ\circ})/L|.

Let
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{t})
be the fundamental invariants of
L
. We consider two cases seperately.

(\mathrm{i})
If
a_{t}
is even and
a_{t}\geq 4
, then we may choose a normal basis
\{\ell_{1},\cdots,\ell_{t}\}
of
L
such that
\langle\ell_{1},\cdots,\ell_{t-1}\rangle\perp\ell_{t}
. Write
(\ell_{t},\ell_{t})=u_{t}(-\pi_{0})^{\frac{a_{t}}{2}}
. In this case, we choose
L^{\prime}=\langle\ell_{1},\cdots,\ell_{t-1},\pi^{-1}\ell_{t}\rangle
, with fundamental invariants
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{t-1},a_{t}-2)
.
Then

\displaystyle\pi L^{\sharp}=\langle\pi^{-a_{1}+1}\ell_{1},\cdots,\pi^{-a_{t}+1}\ell_{t}\rangle\text{ and }\pi L^{\prime\sharp}=\langle\pi^{-a_{1}+1}\ell_{1},\cdots,\pi^{-a_{t-1}+1}\ell_{t-1},\pi^{-a_{t}+2}\ell_{t}\rangle.

For a fixed
x_{0}=\sum_{1\leq i<t}s_{i}\pi^{-a_{i}+1}\ell_{i}
where
s_{i}\in O_{F}
, let

\displaystyle S_{x_{0}}^{\circ}

\displaystyle:=\{x\in(\pi L^{\sharp})^{\circ}-(\pi L^{\prime\sharp})^{\circ}:x=x_{0}+s_{t}\pi^{-a_{t}+1}\ell_{t},s_{t}\in O_{F}\}/L,

\displaystyle S_{x_{0}}^{\circ\circ}

\displaystyle:=\{x\in(\pi L^{\sharp})^{\circ\circ}-(\pi L^{\prime\sharp})^{\circ\circ}:x=x_{0}+s_{t}\pi^{-a_{t}+1}\ell_{t},s_{t}\in O_{F}\}/L.

It suffices to show
|S_{x_{0}}^{\circ}|=q\cdot|S_{x_{0}}^{\circ\circ}|
.
Notice that
x=x_{0}+s_{t}\pi^{-a_{t}+1}\ell_{t}\in S^{\circ}_{x_{0}}
if and only if

\displaystyle s_{t}\in O_{F}^{\times},\quad(x,x)=u_{t}(-\pi_{0})^{-\frac{a_{t}}{2}+1}(u_{t}^{-1}(-\pi_{0})^{\frac{a_{t}}{2}-1}(x_{0},x_{0})+s_{t}\bar{s}_{t})\in O_{F_{0}},

and
x\in S^{\circ\circ}_{x_{0}}
if and only if

\displaystyle s_{t}\in O_{F}^{\times},\quad(x,x)=u_{t}(-\pi_{0})^{-\frac{a_{t}}{2}+1}(u_{t}^{-1}(-\pi_{0})^{\frac{a_{t}}{2}-1}(x_{0},x_{0})+s_{t}\bar{s}_{t})\in(\pi_{0}).

Consider the
\pi
-adic expansions

\displaystyle s_{t}=\sum_{i\geq 0}^{\infty}b_{i}\pi^{i},\quad-u_{t}^{-1}(-\pi_{0})^{\frac{a_{t}}{2}-1}(x_{0},x_{0})=\sum_{i\geq 0}^{\infty}c_{i}\pi^{i},

where
b_{i},c_{i}\in O_{F_{0}}/(\pi_{0})
.
Then
x\in S^{\circ}_{x_{0}}
if and only if
s_{t}\in O_{F}^{\times}
, and

\displaystyle c_{0}

\displaystyle=b^{2}_{0},

\displaystyle c_{1}

\displaystyle=b_{1}b_{0}-b_{0}b_{1},

\displaystyle c_{2}

\displaystyle=b_{2}b_{0}-b_{1}b_{1}+b_{0}b_{2},

\displaystyle\cdots

\displaystyle c_{a_{t}-3}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{a_{t}-3}(-1)^{i}b_{a_{t}-3-i}b_{i}.

Similarly,
x\in S^{\circ\circ}_{x_{0}}
if and only if
x\in S^{\circ}_{x_{0}}
and

\displaystyle c_{a_{t}-2}-\sum_{i=1}^{a_{t}-3}(-1)^{i}b_{a_{t}-2-i}b_{i}=2b_{a_{t}-2}b_{0}.

Since
s_{t}\in O_{F}^{\times}
,
b_{0}\neq 0
and
b_{a_{t}-2}
is uniquely determined by the above equation. Hence
|S_{x_{0}}^{\circ}|=q\cdot|S_{x_{0}}^{\circ\circ}|
as a result.

(\mathrm{ii})
If
a_{t}
is odd (since
L
is non-maximal,
a_{t}>1
in this case) or
a_{t}=2
, then we may choose a normal basis
\{\ell_{1},\cdots,\ell_{t}\}
of
L
such that the moment matrix of
\{\ell_{t-1},\ell_{t}\}
is
H_{a_{t}}
, where
H_{a_{t}}\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}0&\pi^{a_{t}}\\
(-\pi)^{a_{t}}&0\end{pmatrix}
. We may choose
L^{\prime}=\langle\ell_{1},\cdots,\ell_{t-2},\pi^{-1}\ell_{t-1},\ell_{t}\rangle
with fundamental invariants
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{t-2},a_{t}-1,a_{t}-1)
. In this case,

\displaystyle\pi L^{\sharp}=\langle\pi^{-a_{1}+1}\ell_{1},\cdots,\pi^{-a_{t}+1}\ell_{t}\rangle\text{ and }\pi L^{\prime\sharp}=\langle\pi^{-a_{1}+1}\ell_{1},\cdots,\pi^{-a_{t}+1}\ell_{t-1},\pi^{-a_{t}+2}\ell_{t}\rangle.

For a fixed
x_{0}=\sum_{1\leq i<t-1}s_{i}\pi^{-a_{i}+1}\ell_{i}
where
s_{i}\in O_{F}
, let

\displaystyle S_{x_{0}}^{\circ}

\displaystyle:=\{x\in(\pi L^{\sharp})^{\circ}-(\pi L^{\prime\sharp})^{\circ}:x=x_{0}+s_{t-1}\pi^{-a_{t}+1}\ell_{t-1}+s_{t}\pi^{-a_{t}+1}\ell_{t},\text{ where }s_{t-1},s_{t}\in O_{F}\}/L,

\displaystyle S_{x_{0}}^{\circ\circ}

\displaystyle:=\{x\in(\pi L^{\sharp})^{\circ\circ}-(\pi L^{\prime\sharp})^{\circ\circ}:x=x_{0}+s_{t-1}\pi^{-a_{t}+1}\ell_{t-1}+s_{t}\pi^{-a_{t}+1}\ell_{t},\text{ where }s_{t-1},s_{t}\in O_{F}\}/L.

It suffices to show
|S_{x_{0}}^{\circ}|=q\cdot|S_{x_{0}}^{\circ\circ}|
. Notice that
x=x_{0}+s_{t-1}\pi^{-a_{t}+1}\ell_{t-1}+s_{t}\pi^{-a_{t}+1}\ell_{t}\in S^{\circ}_{x_{0}}
if and only if

\displaystyle s_{t}\in O_{F}^{\times},\quad(x,x)=(x_{0},x_{0})+(s_{t-1}\bar{s}_{t}+(-1)^{a_{t}}\bar{s}_{t-1}s_{t})(-1)^{-a_{t}+1}\pi^{-a_{t}+2}\in O_{F_{0}},

and

x\in S^{\circ\circ}_{x_{0}}
if and only if

\displaystyle s_{t}\in O_{F}^{\times},\quad(x,x)=(x_{0},x_{0})+(s_{t-1}\bar{s}_{t}+(-1)^{a_{t}}\bar{s}_{t-1}s_{t})(-1)^{-a_{t}+1}\pi^{-a_{t}+2}\in(\pi_{0}).

Write

\displaystyle s_{t-1}=\sum_{i\geq 0}^{\infty}b_{i}\pi^{i},\quad s_{t}=\sum_{i\geq 0}^{\infty}c_{i}\pi^{i},\quad-(-1)^{a_{t}-1}\pi^{a_{t}-2}(x_{0},x_{0})=\sum_{i\geq 0}^{\infty}d_{i}\pi^{i},

where
b_{i},c_{i},d_{i}\in O_{F_{0}}/(\pi_{0})
. Then
x\in S_{x_{0}}^{\circ\circ}
if and only if
x\in S_{x_{0}}^{\circ}
and

\displaystyle d_{a_{t}-2}+S=-2b_{a_{t}-2}c_{0}.

where
S
is certain expression involving
b_{0},\cdots,b_{a_{t}-3}
and
c_{1},\cdots,c_{a_{t}-2}
.
Since
s_{t}\in O_{F}^{\times}
,
c_{0}\not=0
. Hence, for any given
S
, the number of choices of
b_{a_{t}-2}
is determined if
x\in S^{\circ\circ}_{x_{0}}
. As a result,
|S_{x_{0}}^{\circ}|=q\cdot|S_{x_{0}}^{\circ\circ}|
.
∎

Proposition 8.7
.

Assume that
t\geq 1
is odd and
L
is a full type lattice of rank
t
.
Then for any
\chi\in\{\pm 1\}
, we have

(1-\chi q^{\frac{t+1}{2}})(1+\chi q^{\frac{t-1}{2}})\mu^{+}(L)+(1+\chi q^{\frac{t-1}{2}})\mu^{0}(L)+\mu^{-}(L)=0.

Proof.

We prove this for maximal
L
first. We can choose a basis
\{\ell_{1},\cdots,\ell_{t}\}
of
L
with moment matrix
\mathrm{Diag}(H_{1}^{\frac{t-1}{2}},u_{t}(-\pi_{0}))
. Set
L_{1}=\langle\ell_{1},\cdots,\ell_{t-1}\rangle
and
L_{2}=\langle\ell_{t}\rangle
. Then we can directly compute that

\displaystyle(\pi L^{\sharp})^{\circ\circ}=L,\quad(\pi L^{\sharp})^{\circ}=L_{1}\obot\pi^{-1}L_{2},\quad(L^{\sharp})^{\circ}=\pi^{-1}L_{1}\obot\pi^{-1}L_{2}.

Hence

\displaystyle\mu^{+}(L)=|(\pi L^{\sharp})^{\circ\circ}/L|=1,\quad\mu^{0}(L)=|((\pi L^{\sharp})^{\circ}-(\pi L^{\sharp})^{\circ\circ})/L|=q-1,

and

\displaystyle\mu^{-}(L)=|((L^{\sharp})^{\circ}-(\pi L^{\sharp})^{\circ})/L|=q^{t}-q.

As a result,

\displaystyle(1-\chi q^{\frac{t+1}{2}})(1+\chi q^{\frac{t-1}{2}})\mu^{+}(L)+(1+\chi q^{\frac{t-1}{2}})\mu^{0}(L)+\mu^{-}(L)

\displaystyle=(1-\chi q^{\frac{t+1}{2}})(1+\chi q^{\frac{t-1}{2}})+(1+\chi q^{\frac{t-1}{2}})(q-1)+q^{t}-q

\displaystyle=0.

Now we assume
L
is not maximal and the proposition holds for
L^{\prime}
such that
L\subsetneq L^{\prime}
by an induction on
{\mathrm{val}}(L)
. With this assumption, it suffices to show

\displaystyle(1-\chi q^{\frac{t+1}{2}})(1+\chi q^{\frac{t-1}{2}})\mu^{+}(L,L^{\prime})+(1+\chi q^{\frac{t-1}{2}})\mu^{0}(L,L^{\prime})+\mu^{-}(L,L^{\prime})=0,

which follows from a combination of Lemmas
8.4
and
8.6
.
∎

Lemma 8.8
.

If
L
is non-maximal full type lattice of rank
t
, then there exists a
L^{\prime}
such that
L\subset L^{\prime}\subset\pi^{-1}L
and

\displaystyle\mu^{0,+}(L,L^{\prime})=\mu^{0,-}(L,L^{\prime}).

Proof.

Let

\displaystyle S^{\nu}:=\{x\in((\pi L^{\sharp})^{\circ}-(\pi L^{\sharp})^{\circ\circ})-((\pi L^{\prime\sharp})^{\circ}-(\pi L^{\prime\sharp})^{\circ\circ}):\chi(\langle x\rangle)=\nu\}/L.

We need to show
|S^{+1}|=|S^{-1}|
.
Let
\{\ell_{1},\cdots,\ell_{t}\}
be a normal basis of
L
, and let
\{a_{1},\cdots,a_{t}\}
be the set of fundamental invariants of
L
. We consider two cases seperately.

(\mathrm{i})
If
a_{t}
is even and
a_{t}\geq 4
, then we may choose a normal basis
\{\ell_{1},\cdots,\ell_{t}\}
of
L
such that
\langle\ell_{1},\cdots,\ell_{t-1}\rangle\perp\ell_{t}
. In this case, we choose
L^{\prime}=\langle\ell_{1},\cdots,\ell_{t-1},\pi^{-1}\ell_{t}\rangle
, with fundamental invariants
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{t-1},a_{t}-2)
.
Then

\displaystyle\pi L^{\sharp}=\langle\pi^{-a_{1}+1}\ell_{1},\cdots,\pi^{-a_{t}+1}\ell_{t}\rangle\text{ and }\pi L^{\prime\sharp}=\langle\pi^{-a_{1}+1}\ell_{1},\cdots,\pi^{-a_{t-1}+1}\ell_{t-1},\pi^{-a_{t}+2}\ell_{t}\rangle.

For a fixed
x_{0}=\sum_{1\leq i<t}s_{i}\pi^{-a_{i}+1}\ell_{i}
where
s_{i}\in O_{F}
, we set

\displaystyle S_{x_{0}}^{\nu}:=\{x\in((\pi L^{\sharp})^{\circ}-(\pi L^{\sharp})^{\circ\circ})-((\pi L^{\prime\sharp})^{\circ}-(\pi L^{\prime\sharp})^{\circ\circ}):x=x_{0}+s_{t}\pi^{-a_{t}+1}\ell_{t},s_{t}\in O_{F},\chi(\langle x\rangle)=\nu\}/L.

We need to show
|S_{x_{0}}^{+1}|=|S_{x_{0}}^{-1}|
. Write
(\ell_{t},\ell_{t})=u_{t}(-\pi_{0})^{\frac{a_{t}}{2}}
. Notice that
x=x_{0}+s_{t}\pi^{-a_{t}+1}\ell_{t}\in S^{\nu}_{x_{0}}
if and only if

(8.7)

\displaystyle s_{t}\in O_{F}^{\times},\quad(x,x)\in O_{F_{0}}^{\times},\quad\chi((x,x))=\nu.

Notice that

\displaystyle(x,x)=u_{t}(-\pi_{0})^{-\frac{a_{t}}{2}+1}(u_{t}^{-1}(-\pi_{0})^{\frac{a_{t}}{2}-1}(x_{0},x_{0})+s_{t}\bar{s}_{t}).

Write

\displaystyle s_{t}=\sum_{i\geq 0}^{\infty}b_{i}\pi^{i},\quad\text{ and }-u_{t}^{-1}(-\pi_{0})^{\frac{a_{t}}{2}-1}(x_{0},x_{0})=\sum_{i\geq 0}^{\infty}c_{i}\pi^{i},

where
b_{i},c_{i}\in O_{F_{0}}/(\pi_{0})
.
Then the conditions in (
8.7
) are equivalent to the following equations:

\displaystyle c_{0}

\displaystyle=b^{2}_{0}\not=0,

\displaystyle c_{1}

\displaystyle=b_{1}b_{0}-b_{0}b_{1},

\displaystyle c_{2}

\displaystyle=b_{2}b_{0}-b_{1}b_{1}+b_{0}b_{2},

\displaystyle\cdots

\displaystyle c_{a_{t}-3}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{a_{t}-3}(-1)^{i}b_{a_{t}-3-i}b_{i},

\displaystyle\nu

\displaystyle=\chi\Big(u_{t}(-\pi_{0})^{-\frac{a_{t}}{2}+1}\big(-c_{a_{t}-2}+\sum_{i=0}^{a_{t}-2}(-1)^{i}b_{a_{t}-2-i}b_{i}\big)\Big).

Since
a_{t}
is even by assumption, the last equation is the same with

\displaystyle\nu

\displaystyle=\chi\Big(u_{t}(-\pi_{0})^{-\frac{a_{t}}{2}+1}\big(-c_{a_{t}-2}+\sum_{i=1}^{a_{t}-3}(-1)^{i}b_{a_{t}-2-i}b_{i}+2b_{0}b_{a_{t}-2}\big)\Big).

Notice that the possible choices of
\{b_{0},\cdots,b_{a_{t}-3}\}
are determined by the first
a_{t}-2
equations. And for a given choice of
\{b_{0},\cdots,b_{a_{t}-3}\}
, the number of choices of
b_{a_{t}-2}
that satisfies the last equation is clearly independent of
\nu
since
b_{0}\not=0
.

(\mathrm{ii})
If
a_{t}
is odd or
a_{t}=2
, then we may choose a normal basis
\{\ell_{1},\cdots,\ell_{t}\}
of
L
such that the moment matrix of
\{\ell_{t-1},\ell_{t}\}
is
H_{a_{t}}
, where
H_{a_{t}}\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}0&\pi^{a_{t}}\\
(-\pi)^{a_{t}}&0\end{pmatrix}
. We may choose
L^{\prime}=\langle\ell_{1},\cdots,\ell_{t-2},\pi^{-1}\ell_{t-1},\ell_{t}\rangle
with fundamental invariants
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{t-2},a_{t-1}-1,a_{t}-1)
. In this case,

\displaystyle\pi L^{\sharp}=\langle\pi^{-a_{1}+1}\ell_{1},\cdots,\pi^{-a_{t}+1}\ell_{t}\rangle\text{ and }\pi L^{\prime\sharp}=\langle\pi^{-a_{1}+1}\ell_{1},\cdots,\pi^{-a_{t-1}+1}\ell_{t-1},\pi^{-a_{t}+2}\ell_{t}\rangle.

For a fixed
x_{0}=\sum_{1\leq i<t-1}s_{i}\pi^{-a_{i}+1}\ell_{i}
, we set

\displaystyle S_{x_{0}}^{\nu}:=\{x\in((\pi L^{\sharp})^{\circ}-(\pi L^{\sharp})^{\circ\circ})-((\pi L^{\prime\sharp})^{\circ}-(\pi L^{\prime\sharp})^{\circ\circ}):

\displaystyle x=x_{0}+s_{t-1}\pi^{-a_{t-1}+1}\ell_{t-1}+s_{t}\pi^{-a_{t}+1}\ell_{t},

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad s_{t-1},s_{t}\in O_{F},\chi(\langle x\rangle)=\nu\}/L.

It suffices to show
|S_{x_{0}}^{+1}|=|S_{x_{0}}^{-1}|
. Notice that
x=x_{0}+s_{t-1}\pi^{-a_{t-1}+1}\ell_{t-1}+s_{t}\pi^{-a_{t}+1}\ell_{t}\in S^{\nu}_{x_{0}}
if and only if

(8.8)

\displaystyle s_{t}\in O_{F}^{\times},\quad(x,x)=(x_{0},x_{0})+(s_{t-1}\bar{s}_{t}+(-1)^{a_{t}}\bar{s}_{t-1}s_{t})(-1)^{-a_{t}+1}\pi^{-a_{t}+2}\in O_{F_{0}}^{\times},\quad\chi((x,x))=\nu.

Write

\displaystyle s_{t-1}=\sum_{i\geq 0}^{\infty}b_{i}\pi^{i},\quad s_{t}=\sum_{i\geq 0}^{\infty}c_{i}\pi^{i},\quad-(-1)^{a_{t}-1}\pi^{a_{t}-2}(x_{0},x_{0})=\sum_{i\geq 0}^{\infty}d_{i}\pi^{i},

where
b_{i},c_{i},d_{i}\in O_{F_{0}}/(\pi_{0})
. Then the condition in (
8.8
) is equivalent to the following equations:

\displaystyle d_{0}

\displaystyle=b_{0}c_{0}+(-1)^{a_{t}}b_{0}c_{0},

\displaystyle d_{1}

\displaystyle=b_{1}c_{0}-b_{0}c_{1}+(-1)^{a_{t}}(-b_{1}c_{0}+b_{0}c_{0}),

\displaystyle\cdots

\displaystyle\nu

\displaystyle=\chi\Big((-1)^{a_{t}-1}\pi^{-a_{t}+2}\big(-d_{a_{t}-2}+S+2b_{a_{t}-2}c_{0}\big)\Big),

where
S
is certain expression involving
b_{0},\cdots,b_{a_{t}-3}
and
c_{1},\cdots,c_{a_{t}-2}
.
Since
s_{t}\in O_{F}^{\times}
,
c_{0}\not=0
. Hence, for any given
S
, the number of choices of
b_{a_{t}-2}
that satisfies the last equation is clearly independent of
\nu
.
∎

Proposition 8.9
.

Assume that
t\geq 1
is even and that
L
is a full type lattice of rank
t
. Then for any
\chi\in\{\pm 1\},
we have

\displaystyle(1-q^{t})\mu^{+}(L)+(1-\chi q^{\frac{t}{2}})\mu^{0,+1}(L)+(1+\chi q^{\frac{t}{2}})\mu^{0,-1}(L)+\mu^{-}(L)=0.

Proof.

We prove this for maximal
L
first. There are two cases we need to consider.

(i) If we can choose a basis
\{\ell_{1},\cdots,\ell_{t}\}
of
L
with moment matrix
\mathrm{Diag}(H_{1}^{\frac{t}{2}-1},u_{t-1}(-\pi_{0}),u_{t}(-\pi_{0}))
where
\chi(-u_{t-1}u_{t})=-1
, then set
L_{1}=\langle\ell_{1},\cdots,\ell_{t-2}\rangle
and
L_{2}=\langle\ell_{t-1},\ell_{t}\rangle
. In this case, a direct computation shows that

\displaystyle(\pi L^{\sharp})^{\circ\circ}=L,\quad(\pi L^{\sharp})^{\circ}=L_{1}\obot\pi^{-1}L_{2},\quad(L^{\sharp})^{\circ}=\pi^{-1}L.

Hence

(8.9)

\displaystyle\mu^{+}(L)=|(\pi L^{\sharp})^{\circ\circ}/L|=1,\quad\mu^{-}(L)=|((L^{\sharp})^{\circ}-(\pi L^{\sharp})^{\circ})/L|=q^{t}-q^{2}.

Moreover,

\displaystyle\mu^{0,\nu}(L)=|\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}-(0,0)\mid\chi(u_{t-1}x^{2}+u_{t}y^{2})=\nu\}|.

It is well known that

\displaystyle|\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}-(0,0)\mid u_{t-1}x^{2}+u_{t}y^{2}=1\}|=q-\chi(-u_{t-1}u_{t})=q+1.

Hence

(8.10)

\displaystyle\mu^{0,+1}(L)=\mu^{0,-1}(L)=\frac{q^{2}-1}{2}.

Combining (
8.9
) and (
8.10
), we have

\displaystyle(1-q^{t})\mu^{+}(L)+(1-\chi q^{\frac{t}{2}})\mu^{0,+}(L)+(1+\chi q^{\frac{t}{2}})\mu^{0,-}(L)+\mu^{-}(L)

\displaystyle=(1-q^{t})+(q^{2}-1)+(q^{t}-q^{2})=0.

(ii) If we can choose a basis
\{\ell_{1},\cdots,\ell_{t}\}
of
L
with moment matrix
H_{1}^{\frac{t}{2}}
, then we can directly compute that

\displaystyle(\pi L^{\sharp})^{\circ\circ}=L,\quad(\pi L^{\sharp})^{\circ}=L,\quad(L^{\sharp})^{\circ}=\pi^{-1}L.

Hence

\displaystyle\mu^{+}(L)=|(\pi L^{\sharp})^{\circ\circ}/L|=1,\quad\mu^{0}(L)=0,\quad\mu^{-}(L)=|((L^{\sharp})^{\circ}-(\pi L^{\sharp})^{\circ})/L|=q^{t}-1.

As a result we have

\displaystyle(1-q^{t})\mu^{+}(L)+(1-\chi q^{\frac{t}{2}})\mu^{0,+}(L)+(1+\chi q^{\frac{t}{2}})\mu^{0,-}(L)+\mu^{-}(L)=(1-q^{t})+(q^{t}-1)=0.

Now we assume
L
is not maximal and the proposition holds for
L^{\prime}
such that
L\subsetneq L^{\prime}
by an induction on
{\mathrm{val}}(L)
. With this assumption, it suffices to show

\displaystyle(1-q^{t})\mu^{+}(L,L^{\prime})+(1-\chi q^{\frac{t}{2}})\mu^{0,+1}(L,L^{\prime})+(1+\chi q^{\frac{t}{2}})\mu^{0,-1}(L,L^{\prime})+\mu^{-}(L,L^{\prime})=0,

which follows from a combination of Lemmas
8.4
and
8.8
.
∎

9.
Proof of the main theorem

We prove the main theorem in this section by an induction on
{\mathrm{val}}(L)
using the results we obtained about the partial Fourier transform in previous sections.

9.1.
Comparison of horizontal intersection numbers

Lemma 9.1
.

Let
L\subset\mathbb{V}
be a lattice.
If
L=L_{1}\obot L_{2}
where
L_{1}
is unimodular, then

\displaystyle\Int(L)-\partial\mathrm{Den}(L)=\Int(L_{2})-\partial\mathrm{Den}(L_{2}).

Proof.

The lemma follows from comparing (
7.3
) with (
2.12
).
∎

Definition 9.2
.

Let
L^{\flat}\subset\mathbb{V}
be a non-degenerate lattice of rank
n-1
, and
x\in\mathbb{V}\setminus L_{F}^{\flat}
. Define

(9.1)

\partial\mathrm{Den}_{L^{\flat},\mathscr{H}}(x)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}L^{\flat}\subset L^{\prime}\subset L^{\prime\sharp}\\
L^{\prime\flat}\in\mathrm{Hor}(L^{\flat})\end{subarray}}\partial\mathrm{Pden}(L^{\prime})1_{L^{\prime}}(x).

Lemma 9.3
.

If
L^{\flat}\subset\mathbb{V}
is horizontal, then

\Int_{L^{\flat},\mathscr{H}}(x)=\partial\mathrm{Den}_{L^{\flat},\mathscr{H}}(x),

where
\Int_{L^{\flat},\mathscr{H}}
is defined in Definition
4.7
.

Proof.

Let
L=L^{\flat}\oplus\langle x\rangle
.
By Lemma
4.9
, we know

(9.2)

\Int_{L^{\flat},\mathscr{H}}(x)=\Int_{L^{\flat}}(x)=\Int(L).

On the other hand, since
L^{\flat}
is horizontal, by Lemma
4.2
any integral lattice of rank
n-1
containing
L^{\flat}
is horizontal, hence we have

(9.3)

\partial\mathrm{Den}_{L^{\flat},\mathscr{H}}(x)=\partial\mathrm{Den}_{L^{\flat}}(x)=\partial\mathrm{Den}(L).

So it suffices to prove
\Int(L)=\partial\mathrm{Den}(L)
.

When
n=2
, by (
9.2
) and (
9.3
),
the lemma is a consequence of
[
Shi22
, Theorem 1.1]
and
[
HSY23b
, Theorem 1.1]
.
When
n>2
,
L^{\flat}
has a unimodular direct summand
L_{1}
of rank
n-2
such that
L=L_{1}\obot L_{2}
and
L_{2}^{\flat}:=L_{2,F}\cap L^{\flat}
is a horizontal lattice in
L_{2,F}
. The lemma follows from the the case
n=2
and Lemma
9.1
.
∎

Lemma 9.4
.

If
M^{\flat}\subset\mathbb{V}
is horizontal, then

\chi(\mathcal{N},{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}(M^{\flat})^{\circ}\cdot\mathcal{Z}(x))=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}M^{\flat}\subset L^{\prime}\subset L^{\prime\sharp}\\
L^{\prime\flat}=M^{\flat}\end{subarray}}\partial\mathrm{Pden}(L^{\prime})1_{L^{\prime}}(x),

where
{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}(M^{\flat})^{\circ}
is as in Definition
4.5
.

Proof.

By Definition
4.7
, we have

(9.4)

\Int_{M^{\flat},\mathscr{H}}(x)=\chi(\mathcal{N},{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}(M^{\flat})^{\circ}\cdot\mathcal{Z}(x))+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}L^{\prime\flat}\in\mathrm{Hor}(M^{\flat})\\
L^{\prime\flat}\neq M^{\flat}\end{subarray}}\chi(\mathcal{N},{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}(L^{\prime\flat})^{\circ}\cdot\mathcal{Z}(x)).

We now prove the lemma by induction on
{\mathrm{val}}(M^{\flat})
. When
M^{\flat}
is unimodular, the lemma is the same as Lemma
9.3
. In general notice that any integral lattice of rank
n-1
containing
M^{\flat}
is horizontal by Lemma
4.2
. Applying the induction hypothesis to the right hand side of the above formula and applying Lemma
9.3
to the left hand had, we obtain

(9.5)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}M^{\flat}\subset L^{\prime}\subset L^{\prime\sharp}\\
L^{\prime\flat}\in\mathrm{Hor}(M^{\flat})\end{subarray}}\partial\mathrm{Pden}(L^{\prime})1_{L^{\prime}}(x)=\chi(\mathcal{N},{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}(M^{\flat})^{\circ}\cdot\mathcal{Z}(x))+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}M^{\flat}\subset L^{\prime}\subset L^{\prime\vee}\\
L^{\prime\flat}\neq M^{\flat}\end{subarray}}\partial\mathrm{Pden}(L^{\prime})1_{L^{\prime}}(x).

Subtract the left hand side by the second term of the right hand side of the equation, the lemma is proved.
∎

Theorem 9.5
.

For a non-degenerate lattice
L^{\flat}\subset\mathbb{V}
of rank
n-1
, and
x\in\mathbb{V}\setminus L_{F}^{\flat}
, we have

\displaystyle\Int_{L^{\flat},\mathscr{H}}(x)=\partial\mathrm{Den}_{L^{\flat},\mathscr{H}}(x).

Proof.

By the definition of
\Int_{L^{\flat},\mathscr{H}}(x)
, we have

\Int_{L^{\flat},\mathscr{H}}(x)=\sum_{M^{\flat}\in\mathrm{Hor}(L^{\flat})}\chi(\mathcal{N},{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}(M^{\flat})^{\circ}\cdot\mathcal{Z}(x)).

The theorem now follows from (
9.1
) and Lemma
9.4
.
∎

9.2.
Proof of the main theorem

The following is an analogue of Lemma 9.3.1 of
[
LZ22b
]
.

Lemma 9.6
.

Let
L^{\flat}\subset\mathbb{V}
be a non-degenerate lattice of rank
n-1
and
\mathbb{W}=(L^{\flat}_{F})^{\perp}
. For
x\not\in L^{\flat}\obot\mathbb{W}
, there exists an
O_{F}
-lattice
L^{\prime\flat}
of rank
n-1
and
x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{V}
such that

\displaystyle\mathrm{val}(L^{\prime\flat})<\mathrm{val}(L^{\flat})\text{ and }L^{\prime\flat}+\langle x^{\prime}\rangle=L^{\flat}+\langle x\rangle.

Proof.

Assume that
L^{\flat}\subset\mathbb{V}
has fundamental invariants
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n-1})
. Let
\{\ell_{1},\cdots,\ell_{n-1}\}
be a basis of
L^{\flat}
whose moment matrix is

\mathrm{Diag}(H_{b_{1}},H_{b_{3}},\cdots,H_{b_{2s-1}},u_{2s+1}\pi^{b_{2s+1}},\cdots,u_{n-1}\pi^{b_{n-1}}),

where
b_{1},\cdots,b_{2s-1}
are odd and
H_{j}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\pi^{j}\\
(-\pi)^{j}&0\end{pmatrix}
. Notice that
\{b_{1},\cdots,b_{n-1}\}=\{a_{1},\cdots,a_{n-1}\}
.
The moment matrix of
\{\ell_{1},\cdots,\ell_{n-1},x\}
is

\displaystyle T=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}H_{b_{1}}&&&\left(\ell_{1},x\right)\\
&\ddots&&\vdots\\
